## Página PDF 3

Resumo
Esta dissertação apresenta a Catmming, um chatbot baseado em modelos de linguagem de grande
escala (LLM), concebido para apoiar a autorregulação (SRL) e co-regulação da aprendizagem no
ensino online assíncrono de engenharia de software. O objetivo principal é melhorar a experiência
de aprendizagem dos estudantes da unidade curricular Laboratório de Desenvolvimento de Soft-
ware (LDS) da Universidade Aberta, transformando a Catmming de uma figura narrativa estática
num agente conversacional inteligente. Recorre-se a técnicas de geração de resposta aumentada
por recuperação de informação (RAG) e processamento de linguagem natural para fornecer feed-
back personalizado baseado nas interações dos estudantes com atividades do Moodle, incluindo
fóruns, questionários e desafios metacognitivos. O sistema foi avaliado através de um conjunto de
testes funcionais alinhados com o modelo cíclico de SRL de Zimmerman e Moylan, garantindo
cobertura de todas as fases e subprocessos. Os resultados demonstram que o chatbot é capaz de
apoiar os estudantes em todo o seu processo de aprendizagem autorregulada, incluindo desafios
metacognitivos, e de promover dinâmicas de co-regulação através da interação ativa entre estu-
dante e chatbot. Esta dissertação propõe uma aplicação inovadora da tecnologia LLM no apoio à
autorregulação e co-regulação da aprendizagem, oferecendo observações práticas e teóricas para
o design de ferramentas de IA no contexto da educação digital.
i

[Vista original de la página 3](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-03.png)

## Página PDF 4

Abstract
This dissertation presents Catmming, a LLM-based chatbot, designed to support self-regulated
(SRL) and co-regulated learning in asynchronous online software engineering education. The
main objective is to improve the learning experience of students enrolled in the Software Devel-
opment Laboratory (LDS) course at Universidade Aberta, transforming Catmming from a static
narrative character into an intelligent conversational agent. Using retrieval-augmented generation
(RAG) techniques and natural language processing, the chatbot provides personalized feedback
based on students’ interactions with Moodle activities, such as forums, quizzes, and metacogni-
tive challenges. The system was evaluated through a set of functional tests aligned with Zim-
merman and Moylan’s cyclical model of SRL, ensuring coverage of all phases and subprocesses.
The results demonstrate that the chatbot is capable of supporting students throughout their self-
regulated learning processes, including metacognitive challenges, while simultaneously promoting
co-regulation dynamics through active interactions between the student and chatbot. This disser-
tation introduces an innovative application of LLM technology to support self- and co-regulation
of learning, offering practical and theoretical insights into the design of AI-driven tools in digital
education.
ii

## Página PDF 5

UN Sustainable Development Goals
The United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) provide a global framework to achieve
a better and more sustainable future for all. They include 17 goals that address the world’s most
pressing challenges, such as poverty, inequality, climate change, and access to quality education.
This dissertation contributes to the SDGs by presenting Catmming, a Large Language Model-
based chatbot developed to support Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) in online asynchronous higher
education. The system was implemented and evaluated in the context of the Laboratório de De-
senvolvimento de Software course at Universidade Aberta, where students are predominantly adult
working professionals engaged in online learning. Catmming promotes learner autonomy by guid-
ing students through SRL subprocesses without requiring direct teacher intervention. By utilizing
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with course-specific data, it ensures consistent, personal-
ized feedback across learners, enabling equitable access to strategic learning support and fostering
metacognitive and self-regulation capabilities in software engineering education.
The specific Sustainable Development Goals mentioned have the following names:
SDG 4 Ensure inclusive and equitable quality education and promote lifelong learning opportu-
nities for all.
SDG 10 Reduce inequality within and among countries.

## Página PDF 6

Agradecimentos
Gostaria de agradecer aos meus orientadores, Professor Fernando Cassola, Professor Leonel Mor-
gado e Professora Daniela Pedrosa, pelo apoio constante, pelas orientações críticas e pelos valiosos
contributos ao longo desta dissertação. A sua experiência nas áreas de inteligência artificial, en-
sino a distância e autorregulação da aprendizagem foi fundamental para o desenvolvimento deste
projeto.
Agradeço igualmente à Universidade Aberta e ao INESC TEC pelo fornecimento do ambi-
ente Moodle utilizado nos testes e pela disponibilização dos dados reais da unidade curricular
Laboratório de Desenvolvimento de Software (LDS), essenciais para a concretização do sistema
desenvolvido. Um agradecimento especial ao Professor Leonel Morgado, enquanto regente da
unidade curricular, pelo apoio e acompanhamento ao longo do processo.
Aos meus amigos, família e namorada, o meu mais sincero agradecimento pelo apoio incondi-
cional, pela paciência nos momentos difíceis e pelas palavras de incentivo que me ajudaram a
seguir em frente mesmo nos dias mais exigentes. O vosso apoio foi fundamental para a con-
cretização deste trabalho.
Por fim, agradeço às comunidades de software livre e aos programadores por trás das ferra-
mentas de LLM utilizadas neste trabalho, o vosso contributo foi essencial.
André Morais

## Página PDF 15

Chapter 1
Introduction
1.1
Context
The field of education is constantly evolving to keep students engaged in the learning process.
Recent advancements in Artificial Intelligence (AI), particularly large language models (LLMs),
have opened new opportunities to enhance the quality of education, especially in software en-
gineering. Studies like [107] highlight how LLMs, such as ChatGPT, can support self-directed
learning and improve student engagement by offering relevant information, although they are
best used as complementary tools to assist instructors in providing personalized feedback. In
learning environments where students often manage their educational experience independently,
educators face challenges not only in promoting student engagement but, more importantly, in
supporting the development of cognitive skills essential for better learning outcomes. Online
asynchronous higher education is a prime example of such an environment, where learners are
expected to take responsibility for their own learning paths with limited direct supervision. An
innovative educational approach is currently being explored in such an environment by INESC
TEC and Universidade Aberta, where an immersive narrative-driven didatic approach called e-
SimProgramming is being applied [71]. All students of Universidade Aberta, who participate in
the online asynchronous course "Software Development Laboratory" (or LDS in Portuguese) as
part of their Informatics Engineering degree, use the Moodle platform, where approach is used.
The course presents a six-topic syllabus designed to help students transition from novice to expe-
rienced programmers, emphasizing the importance of adhering to engineering structural qualities
when developing software [72]. Through this course, students not only learn software engineer-
ing while developing cognitive skills like metacognition, allowing them to take control of their
learning and improve their problem-solving abilities. These skills are essential for developing the
mental regulation needed in software engineering. Since most students are older, working profes-
sionals from diverse locations and with various academic backgrounds, the teaching method plays
a crucial role in maintaining their engagement as they work on team-based software projects.
e-SimProgramming [71] sets itself apart with its immersive narrative, where students become con-
nected to characters and feel part of a real professional environment. This approach includes the
1

[Vista original de la página 15](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-15.png)

![Página PDF 15, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-15.png)


<!-- PDF page 16 -->

## Página PDF 16

Introduction
2
inclusion of metacognitive challenges (MC), introduced at key points or at the end of each topic,
aimed at promoting self-reflection and self-regulated learning [70]. These challenges are crucial
for developing both the technical and cognitive skills needed in software engineering. However,
there is an opportunity to enhance the narrative and MCs, building on their current strengths. The
character Catmming, which serves as an AI within the narrative, introduces the metacognitive
challenges, guiding students through reflective tasks. While this provides a strong foundation,
integrating a real AI could elevate the experience by offering tailored feedback to students, a task
that is currently time-consuming for instructors [28]. This dissertation aims to transform Catm-
ming from a static narrative element into an AI-driven chatbot powered by a large language model
(LLM), such as ChatGPT, capable of dynamically engaging with students, analyzing their learn-
ing progress, and providing personalized feedback to support self-regulated learning. The goal
is to create a chatbot that, based on the static contents of Moodle and students’ submissions, can
provide personalized support for self-regulation and co-regulation, encouraging students to reflect
on their learning experiences. Nonetheless, this task presents several challenges. Accurately inter-
preting a wide variety of student inputs, expressed in different styles and contexts, can be difficult.
Moreover, leveraging Moodle’s content while ensuring relevance and timeliness is complex. Fur-
thermore, providing meaningful feedback that aligns with students’ needs and progress, without
overwhelming them, is another critical issue. These challenges underscore the need for a well-
designed technological solution that addresses these difficulties and facilitates the current process,
providing students with the right support to improve their self-reflection.
1.2
Motivation
Self-regulated learning (SRL) [41] is crucial in software engineering education, particularly in dis-
tance learning environments where students need to manage their own learning process and cannot
easily obtain feedback from instructors. Incorporating metacognitive activities in e-learning envi-
ronments, such as the metacognitive challenges already applied in e-SimProgramming, has shown
potential to enhance student engagement and success by allowing them to reflect on their learning
process. However, the challenge lies in the limited capacity of current approaches to monitor and
provide relevant feedback on metacognitive learning processes for each student. Instructors find it
difficult to provide timely, continuous, and constructive formative evaluation due to their limited
schedules. An AI-based chatbot, built on LLMs, has the potential to address these challenges by
automating feedback generation and enabling more dynamic interactions. In addition, students al-
ready find the current static narrative-driven approach engaging, appealing, and helpful [28]. With
the integration of a real chatbot that can provide rapid feedback, the learning experience could be
enhanced even further.

[Vista original de la página 16](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-16.png)

![Página PDF 16, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-16.png)


<!-- PDF page 17 -->

## Página PDF 17

1.3 Problem
3
1.3
Problem
The current metacognitive challenges in the e-SimProgramming environment use standardized
questions, which help raise awareness of the student’s perception of the learning regulation pro-
cesses but still make it difficult for teaching staff to monitor and provide personalized formative
feedback at scale. The current version of Catmming, being static and unable to provide rapid
feedback, prompts students to engage in self-reflection but does not critically analyze the effec-
tiveness of that reflection or offer advice on how to improve it. This could limit the benefits of
metacognitive challenges, which are essential for fostering self and co-regulated learning [70].
There is an opportunity for a technological solution that allows instructors to efficiently analyze
students’ contributions in forums and responses to metacognitive challenges on Moodle, or even
automate this process entirely. Analyzing the contributions of each student can lead to delays or
even the absence of feedback, limiting the ability of the students to make timely improvements. An
AI-based chatbot could provide the necessary timely and continuous feedback to guide students
toward academic success.
1.4
Research Questions
Based on the problems presented above, the following research questions can be considered:
• RQ1: To what extent can a large language model-based chatbot use Moodle course data
to generate context-aware responses tailored to learners’ queries in asynchronous online
software engineering education?
• RQ2: To what extent do the chatbot’s responses align with the subprocesses of Zimmer-
man’s model of self-regulated learning?
• RQ3: What potential do the chatbot’s responses show in supporting learners’ reflection on
metacognitive challenges, based on simulated inputs?

[Vista original de la página 17](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-17.png)

## Página PDF 18

Chapter 2
Scoping Review
This chapter outlines the scoping review process and presents the findings obtained. The review
was carried out in two successive steps. The first step involved an exploratory review aimed at
identifying relevant terms, concepts, and their definitions. This foundational step was essential for
refining the research focus, formulating a precise title, and delimiting the conceptual scope, con-
text, and perspective of the study. The second stage involved a more systematic mapping review,
in which existing literature reviews were collected and assessed based on predefined inclusion and
exclusion criteria.
The literature review process was conducted according to the protocol established in the study
by [61], which served as the methodological basis for this review.
Building on the results of this initial mapping, a deeper analysis was conducted on a curated
set of five key articles, selected for their relevance to the domain of LLM-based chatbots for
self-regulated and co-regulated learning in asynchronous online software engineering education.
While the exploratory phase focused on reviewing abstracts to define this selection, the second
phase employed a thematic analysis to extract and organize findings in greater depth.
The aim of this thematic analysis, guided by the method proposed by [88], was to critically
evaluate the selected articles and uncover both current advancements and existing gaps in the field.
This structured approach enables a more nuanced understanding of trends, limitations, underex-
plored areas, and future research directions related to the use of conversational agents in digital
education.
Through this process, a promising yet underexplored theme has emerged: “LLM-based chat-
bots that provide comprehensive self-regulated learning support across all phases in asynchronous
online higher education.” This theme will serve as the focal point for the systematic review pre-
sented in Chapter 3. By progressing from the initial broad survey to an in-depth thematic analysis
of the selected articles, this dissertation establishes a structured foundation that informs its devel-
opment and evaluation phases while contributing meaningful insights to address identified gaps in
the literature.
4

[Vista original de la página 18](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-18.png)

## Página PDF 19

2.1 Exploratory Literature Review
5
2.1
Exploratory Literature Review
The goal of the exploratory review was fourfold. First, it aimed to provide additional context and
gain insights into the terminology used in the field of LLM-based chatbots for self-regulation and
co-regulation in online higher education. Understanding this terminology is crucial to accurately
frame the research questions and guide the review process. Second, the exploratory review focused
on establishing effective search terms for the literature review, ensuring that the literature search
would capture relevant papers. Third, it sought to refine the inclusion criteria for the literature
review, facilitating a more targeted selection of literature that aligns with the research objectives.
Finally, the exploratory review phase aimed to enhance the overall results by providing a founda-
tional understanding of the subject, thereby ensuring a more informed approach to the literature
review process.
2.1.1
Defining the Scope
The first step of the protocol led to the formulation of the title: "Catmming: an LLM-based chatbot
designed for software engineering students in asynchronous online higher education to support
self-regulation and co-regulation of learning". This title was carefully crafted to align with three
essential guiding elements: concept, context, and perspective.
The concept represents the general area of interest: "Catmming - an LLM-based chatbot".
Here, "Catmming" is envisioned as an AI-powered conversational agent, aligning with the re-
search’s focus on using Large Language Models to facilitate interaction and learning support. This
general idea frames the research within the thematic space of chatbot technology in educational
contexts.
The context delineates the specific environment in which this concept is applied, as described
in the title: "designed for software engineering students in asynchronous online higher education".
This focuses the study on a particular student group within the specialized field of software en-
gineering, attending classes in an online asynchronous learning environment. This context limits
the research to a targeted educational scenario, outlining the environment, users, and application
settings that will define the chatbot’s design and functionality.
Finally, the perspective clarifies the approach to the concept within this context, stated in the
title as "to support self-regulation and co-regulation of learning". This perspective reflects the
general intent behind implementing the chatbot: to aid students in managing their learning both
independently (self-regulation) and collaboratively (co-regulation). By fostering these skills in this
specific learning context, the study seeks to enhance students’ ability to engage meaningfully with
their coursework, meet learning objectives, and navigate the challenges inherent in asynchronous
online education. This focus on supporting self-regulation and co-regulation shapes the chatbot’s
purpose and expected impact within the specified context.
Through these elements, the title encapsulates the research’s objectives within a cohesive
framework capable of guiding the literature review.

[Vista original de la página 19](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-19.png)

![Página PDF 19, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-19.png)


<!-- PDF page 20 -->

## Página PDF 20

Scoping Review
6
The literature review begins by identifying the current knowledge on building LLM-based
chatbots, examining the different approaches, unique characteristics, and design processes. It
then explores content related to software engineering in higher education, as well as the types of
activities and features characteristic of asynchronous online higher education. Particular atten-
tion is given to the concepts of self-regulation and co-regulation of learning, with an emphasis
on how they apply to asynchronous online education, particularly for software engineering. Fi-
nally, a state-of-the-art review is conducted on how chatbots are used to support these educational
goals, ideally intersecting all areas but more realistically in pairs. This reflects the pratical scope
of the field, where a search for "chatbots for self-regulation and co-regulation of learning in on-
line software engineering education" would likely yield few results, while “chatbots in software
engineering education” would be too broad to analyze meaningfully.
2.1.2
Definition of Terms
The second step of the protocol involved defining and clarifying the fundamental terms used
throughout this review. This step aimed to establish clear definitions for each term, distinguish-
ing their specific meanings within the context of this research from other possible interpretations.
By solidifying these definitions, the review ensures precision in its use of terminology, thereby
enhancing the coherence and focus of the analysis. Additionally, these definitions served as a
basis for developing inclusion and exclusion criteria, aiding in the selection of relevant literature.
The terms in this section, along with relevant citations, are presented to establish a foundational
understanding that will support the discussion on how LLM-based chatbots could support the self-
regulation and co-regulation of learning for students in a software engineering course within an
asynchronous online environment. To complement this conceptual groundwork, a technical ex-
ploratory review was also included to examine Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), a key
architectural approach used in the implementation of the proposed system.
2.1.3
Chatbot Definition
A chatbot is a system designed to facilitate human-computer interaction by simulating conversa-
tions with users, which can be based on artificial intelligence or predefined rules. While many
modern chatbots leverage Natural Language Processing (NLP) techniques and sometimes senti-
ment analysis to interpret and generate human-like responses via text or voice, earlier implemen-
tations relied on simple rule-based structures. Chatbots are useful in many fields including the
field of education. In this field, chatbots enhance productivity by providing engaging answers and
support to students, creating a more dynamic alternative to static information sources [1].
The history of chatbots is closely tied to the question posed by Alan Turing in 1950: “Can
machines think?”. In his seminal article Computing Machinery and Intelligence, Turing proposed
the now-famous Turing Test, a criterion to evaluate a machine’s ability to exhibit human-like in-
telligence by engaging in a conversation indistinguishable from that of a real person. This concept

[Vista original de la página 20](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-20.png)

![Página PDF 20, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-20.png)


<!-- PDF page 21 -->

## Página PDF 21

2.1 Exploratory Literature Review
7
laid the groundwork for chatbot development, as researchers began exploring how machines might
mimic human thought and conversation [101].
The first notable attempt to bring this vision to life came in 1966 with Eliza, one of the ear-
liest AI-driven programs developed by Professor Joseph Weizenbaum at MIT’s Artificial Intelli-
gence Laboratory. Named after the character Eliza Doolittle from G.B. Shaw’s play Pygmalion,
this chatbot was initially designed to simulate a Rogerian psychotherapist, responding with open-
ended questions that encouraged users to share more. Despite its simplicity and limited under-
standing, Eliza laid the groundwork for how people could engage with machines, especially in
conversational contexts. This advance in computing and artificial intelligence spurred the creation
of increasingly sophisticated chatbots [1][101].
Chatbots can be categorized based on their response generation methods. Broadly, there are
three primary types: rule-based, retrieval-based, and generative-based chatbots. Rule-based chat-
bots rely on pattern matching, predefined scripts, and decision trees, which make them suitable for
handling simple, structured tasks but limit their flexibility. Retrieval-based chatbots use Natural
Language Processing (NLP) techniques to select the most relevant responses from a knowledge
base, making them ideal for domains requiring accurate, fixed answers, such as customer support.
Generative-based chatbots, in contrast, utilize machine learning to create dynamic responses, en-
abling them to handle more complex, open-ended conversations. Additionally, chatbots may be
generic, designed to respond across a broad range of topics, or domain-specific, focusing on a
closed set of topics or a specific area. They can also be categorized according to their goals of
being: informative, conversational or task based [1].
In this dissertation, references to chatbots specifically denote AI-driven conversational chat-
bots that operate within a closed domain, using both generative and retrieval-based methods for
text-based communication. This type of chatbots is particularly suited for educational settings,
where personalized, contextually aware guidance enhances individual learning needs and engage-
ment. The specific functionalities and implementation details of these chatbots will be further
elaborated in the following sections.
2.1.4
Large Language Model Definition
Large Language Models (LLMs) are advanced machine learning models designed to understand
and generate human-like text. Built using vast neural network architectures, typically based on
transformers, LLMs capture intricate linguistic patterns and context from extensive datasets, al-
lowing them to perform various tasks such as translation, summarization, and question answering.
Trained initially on large-scale text corpora in a self-supervised manner, LLMs develop a gener-
alized representation of language that can be adapted across domains with minimal task-specific
fine-tuning. The scale of these models, with billions of parameters, enables emergent capabilities
like reasoning, planning, and in-context learning, where LLMs adapt responses based on exam-
ples within the input prompt. ChatGPT is one of the most widely recognized LLMs, known for its
ability to provide coherent, contextually aware responses across various domains [63].

[Vista original de la página 21](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-21.png)

![Página PDF 21, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-21.png)


<!-- PDF page 22 -->

## Página PDF 22

Scoping Review
8
Augmented LLMs extend this functionality by incorporating external sources of information,
such as databases, vectors, or even real-time web data, to address limitations like “hallucina-
tions", or the generation of inaccurate content. This augmentation allows LLMs to retrieve rele-
vant, up-to-date context without retraining, improving response accuracy and alignment with user
needs. Retrieval-augmented LLMs use a retriever component to access external data repositories
or internet-based sources to supplement responses. Zero-shot retrieval can enhance performance
by appending relevant information retrieved via pre-trained models like BERT. Additionally, re-
trieval pipelines can be trained jointly with the LLM, optimizing both components for greater
response accuracy. By leveraging these augmentation techniques, even smaller LLMs can match
the performance of larger models by effectively accessing and integrating external information
[63].
LLMs can be deployed through various infrastructures, balancing factors such as personal data
privacy, computational load, and performance quality. These models can be accessed via cloud-
based services from providers like OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google, offering high availability and
computational efficiency at the cost of external data processing, which may raise privacy concerns.
Alternatively, local deployments can be implemented at different levels: students may use personal
installations of open-source models, instructors may run local instances for instructional purposes,
or institutions may host on-premise LLMs to ensure data security and compliance with educational
policies. While local deployments offer greater control over personal data and customization, they
require significant computational resources to achieve performance levels comparable to cloud-
based solutions. The choice of deployment affects response latency, scalability, and operational
costs, necessitating a trade-off between accessibility, performance, and privacy considerations.
In this dissertation, both approaches were considered, ultimately favoring local deployment for
improved data control and integration with course resources.
In recent years, Large Language Models (LLMs) have become synonymous with broader terms
like Artificial Intelligence (AI) due to their rapid integration into various domains and user-friendly
platforms like ChatGPT. Although LLMs represent a specific subset of artificial intelligence, their
widespread application in tasks requiring comprehension, contextual reasoning, and interactive
dialogue has led to the public equating them with AI as a whole. For this reason, this dissertation
will consider the term AI to encompass LLMs within the context of this research, recognizing
both the overlap in public perception and the integral role LLMs play within the broader field of
artificial intelligence.
2.1.5
Self-Regulated Learning Definition
Self-regulation and self-regulated learning (SRL) are essential concepts in understanding how
learners actively manage their cognitive and behavioral processes to achieve educational goals.
Zimmerman (1986) applied self-regulation to the domain of learning, defining SRL as the sys-
tematic activation and maintenance of cognitive, motivational, and behavioral processes to achieve
learning goals. Zimmerman’s SRL framework illustrates four stages: observation (learning by
watching others), emulation (imitating observed behaviors), self-control (independently executing

[Vista original de la página 22](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-22.png)

![Página PDF 22, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-22.png)


<!-- PDF page 23 -->

## Página PDF 23

2.1 Exploratory Literature Review
9
behaviors), and self-regulation (refining and adapting skills autonomously). This progression un-
derscores the recursive, adaptive nature of SRL, fostering independence and goal-oriented learning
that empowers students to take charge of their educational journey [81].
Zimmerman conceptualization of SRL builds upon the earlier work of Flavell and Bandura.
According to [81], Flavell (1985) defined metacognition as "cognition about cognition", referring
to cognitive activities and knowledge targeting or regulating other cognitive processes. This defini-
tion highlights a foundational component in SRL, where learners become aware of and guide their
learning processes. Meanwhile, Bandura (1986) integrated self-regulation within his social cogni-
tive theory, describing it as the capacity to influence one’s environment through self-observation,
self-judgment, and self-reaction. These functions enable learners to assess their actions and adjust
them to align with learning objectives [81].
2.1.5.1
Zimmerman’s Self-Regulated Learning Model
[104] originally conceptualized self-regulated learning (SRL) as a cyclical model composed of
three interdependent phases: Forethought, Performance, and Self-Reflection. In this model, learn-
ers actively manage their thoughts, emotions, and behaviors in a dynamic process, where outcomes
from one phase inform and influence the next. Each phase includes specific processes and sub-
processes that support proactive, goal-directed learning behaviors. As shown in Figure 2.1, this
framework includes processes such as task analysis and self-control, and subprocesses like goal
setting and imagery, among others.
Figure 2.1: Zimmerman’s original model of the self-regulated learning cycle [104].

[Vista original de la página 23](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-23.png)

![Página PDF 23, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-23.png)

## Página PDF 24

Scoping Review
10
Later, [106] revisited and refined the model to integrate research on motivational strategies and
metacognitive regulation. This updated version, reviewed in detail by [68], is shown in Figure 2.2.
Compared to the original cycle (presented in Figure 2.1), the revised model introduces two new
motivational subprocesses, interest incentives and self-consequences, and emphasizes the role of
metacognitive monitoring as a central component of the performance phase. At the same time,
some subprocesses from the original model were removed or reformulated. Attention focusing,
which was previously identified as a separate subprocess to manage distractions, appears to have
been expanded into more granular strategies such as time management, environmental structuring,
and help-seeking. Similarly, self-experimentation, which referred to learners’ efforts to try out
different strategies and observe their effects, was removed in the model presented by [106] and
not explicitly replaced, possibly due to its overlap with other monitoring and strategic regulation
subprocesses [104]. These refinements make the updated model more operational and suitable for
designing targeted learning interventions.
Figure 2.2: Zimmerman and Moylan’s refined model of the self-regulated learning cycle [106].
While both models share the same overarching phase structure, the updated model offers a
more detailed and strategy-oriented framework, which is especially suitable for guiding interven-
tions in autonomous and digital learning environments. For this reason, the present dissertation
adopts this model as its theoretical foundation. Below is a brief description of each phase, its
processes and subprocesses, based on the revised model.

[Vista original de la página 24](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-24.png)

![Página PDF 24, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-24.png)


<!-- PDF page 25 -->

## Página PDF 25

2.1 Exploratory Literature Review
11
The Forethought phase consists of analyzing tasks and personal motivational beliefs, during
which goals are established and efficient strategies are planned to enable self-regulation of learning
[106, 68]. This phase includes two main processes: task analysis and self-motivation beliefs. Task
analysis comprises goal setting, where learners establish specific, achievable learning objectives
and strategic planning, which involves choosing methods and sequencing actions to meet those
goals. Self-motivation beliefs refer to four motivational constructs: self-efficacy (belief in one’s
ability to succeed), outcome expectations (anticipated consequences of success or failure), task
interest or value (perceived relevance or enjoyment of the task), and goal orientation (whether
learners adopt mastery or performance-oriented goals) [106, 68].
The Performance phase is where the task defined in the forethought phase is carried out. Stu-
dents adopt strategies to progress in the learning task, monitoring their effectiveness and gener-
ating motivation [106, 68]. It encompasses both self-control and self-observation. Self-control
includes a wide range of strategies that learners use to stay focused and productive. These include
task strategies (specific techniques for understanding and solving problems), self-instruction (ver-
bal prompts or inner speech used to guide actions), imagery (mental visualization of procedures
or outcomes), time management (planning and allocating time effectively), environmental struc-
turing (organizing one’s surroundings to reduce distractions), help-seeking (knowing when and
how to ask for assistance), interest incentives (deliberately increasing motivation through internal
or external rewards), and self-consequences (self-imposed rewards or punishments based on task
performance). It is worth recalling that subprocesses like attention focusing, explicitly named in
the earlier model, are no longer listed as standalone elements and are instead understood to be
distributed across other more concrete subprocesses, such as time management, environmental
structuring, and self-instruction, which regulate attention implicitly. Self-observation, the second
component of the Performance phase, consists of metacognitive monitoring and self-recording.
Metacognitive monitoring refers to the real-time awareness of one’s understanding and strategy
effectiveness during task execution, whereas self-recording involves documenting performance or
progress (e.g., through logs or notes) to support later reflection [106, 68].
The Self-Reflection phase occurs after learning and influences the student’s reactions through
self-judgment and self-reaction. It follows task engagement and allows learners to interpret and
react to their performance through these two processes [106, 68]. Self-judgment includes self-
evaluation, where learners compare their performance to goals or standards, and causal attribution,
which concerns how they explain their outcomes (e.g., attributing failure to lack of effort versus
task difficulty). Self-reaction encompasses self-satisfaction, reflecting emotional responses to the
outcome (such as pride or disappointment), and adaptive or defensive reactions, which involve
deciding whether to persist, revise strategies, or disengage [106, 68].
Altogether, this dissertation adopts [106] updated model as its primary theoretical framework.
This model provides a more detailed and systematic approach to self-regulation, ensuring that
all critical processes involved in learners’ engagement with their education are accounted for.
Its structured and expanded view aligns more effectively with modern instructional designs and
digital environments, such as those used in asynchronous online education environments like e-

![Página PDF 25, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-25.png)

SimProgramming. By following this model, this dissertation aims to better design and evaluate
chatbot-based interventions that effectively support students across all phases of the SRL cycle.
2.1.6
Co-regulated Learning Definition
Co-regulated learning (CRL) refers to a collaborative process where individuals, such as students
or instructors, engage in mutual support to regulate cognitive, behavioral, motivational, and emo-
tional aspects of learning. This process is characterized by the dynamic interaction between self-
regulation and external regulation, often facilitated by regulatory agents like peers, instructors, or
instructional materials [27]. CRL is not merely about sharing information but involves a deeper
level of engagement where participants co-construct knowledge, negotiate regulatory aspects, and
engage in reflective practices to enhance learning outcomes.
In the context of educational settings, CRL can be seen as a bridge between self-regulated
learning (SRL) and socially shared regulation of learning (SSRL). It plays a crucial role in helping
learners transition from external regulation to self-regulation, thereby fostering autonomy and self-
efficacy [27]. The process of CRL involves various activities such as co-planning, co-monitoring,
and co-evaluation, which are essential for developing a comprehensive understanding and mastery
of learning tasks.
Research indicates that CRL is particularly beneficial in environments that require greater su-
pervision and support, such as in higher education or when dealing with complex learning tasks.
By engaging in CRL, students not only enhance their learning but also build resilience and con-
fidence in their ability to manage learning challenges independently. Furthermore, CRL is in-
strumental in promoting dialogue, reflection, and monitoring, which are critical for developing
self-regulatory capacities. It also assists in the transition between different stages of regulation,
fostering a supportive learning environment that can enhance self-efficacy and motivation [78].
Therefore, in my research, I will adopt this definition of co-regulation of learning as a guid-
ing framework. By focusing on co-regulation as a social and collaborative regulation process
that supports the transition towards self-regulation, I can better examine how interactive learning
environments, such as those involving AI-driven chatbots, might facilitate or enhance students’
regulatory skills. This definition highlights the dual role of co-regulation in providing immediate
learning support while also fostering the development of self-regulatory abilities, which aligns
well with the objectives of my dissertation. Following this understanding of co-regulation will
allow me to assess how well these tools can serve as co-regulative partners, enhancing students’
confidence and effectiveness in self-regulated learning tasks.
2.1.7
E-learning Definition
The term e-learning is currently most often used to refer to learning that takes place fully online,
typically mediated by internet-based platforms and digital technologies. In many academic and
institutional contexts, it has become nearly synonymous with online learning [60]. However, its
original meaning was broader, encompassing any use of technology to support learning. Rather

[Vista original de la página 26](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-26.png)

![Página PDF 26, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-26.png)


<!-- PDF page 27 -->

## Página PDF 27

2.1 Exploratory Literature Review
13
than being limited to technological tools, e-learning integrates technology, delivery systems, com-
munication, and pedagogical approaches to support learning processes in digital environments
[80].
This broader perspective allows for the classification of e-learning into different instructional
models, with online learning being the most prevalent today. Online learning is typically organized
into two main formats: synchronous and asynchronous. Synchronous learning requires real-time
participation, where students and instructors interact simultaneously, allowing for immediate feed-
back and live discussion. Asynchronous learning, on the other hand, allows students to engage in
activities at their own pace, within flexible time frames, without needing to be online at the same
time as others [35].
This research will center on asynchronous online learning in higher education, specifically
within the Moodle platform, in alignment with the Virtual Pedagogical Model of [87] (MPV-UAb).
This model is grounded in four guiding principles: student-centred learning, flexibility, interaction,
and digital inclusion. It emphasizes flexibility by structuring learning activities around adaptable
deadlines rather than rigid schedules, enabling students to work autonomously. Interaction is pro-
moted through asynchronous forums that support meaningful discussions between students and
teaching staff. Digital inclusion is addressed by supporting students in developing the techno-
logical skills needed to participate in online education, including through an onboarding module
provided before the start of the program [87]. These principles ensure that learning remains acces-
sible, inclusive, and responsive to the needs of adult learners, qualities that are especially valuable
in fully online environments, as they accommodate students’ varied time constraints and commit-
ments [28].
Given the unique structure and benefits of asynchronous online learning, this research focuses
primarily on this educational format, which promotes flexibility and self-paced engagement. Al-
though the literature review included studies from broader contexts, such as synchronous or hybrid
learning environments, this was necessary due to the limited number of studies addressing all tar-
get themes in purely asynchronous settings. Nonetheless, the asynchronous modality remains the
primary lens through which the problem is approached, as it aligns closely with the goals of this
work [28].
2.1.8
Moodle Definition
Moodle is a free, open-source Learning Management System (LMS) widely used to support dig-
ital education across diverse institutional contexts. It provides educators with a flexible and cus-
tomizable platform grounded in pedagogical principles and benefits from a global community of
developers and educators who continuously enhance its features [58].
Moodle’s modular design supports a wide range of teaching strategies and learning activities,
and its functionality can be extended through a large ecosystem of plugins and third-party inte-
grations. It allows for deployment on institutional servers to ensure compliance with data privacy

regulations such as the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) [38], and integrates with ex-
ternal tools via protocols like Single Sign-On (SSO) and Learning Tools Interoperability (LTI),
promoting seamless access across systems [58].
In its latest release, Moodle LMS 4.5 introduces an AI subsystem that connects to multiple
providers, including open-source backends such as Ollama [59]. This subsystem primarily sup-
ports AI-driven features like content generation and summarization. However, these capabilities
focus on enhancing course materials and do not extend to personalized learning guidance. In con-
trast, the system developed in this dissertation focuses on personalized support for self-regulated
learning (SRL), offering tailored feedback based on Moodle data to guide learners through their
individual learning processes. This highlights a current gap in Moodle’s native AI support and
reinforces the originality of the proposed solution.
2.1.9
Retrieval-Augmented Generation Definition
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances Large Language Models
(LLMs) by incorporating external information retrieved at inference time, with the aim of improv-
ing response factuality, relevance, and accuracy. This section summarizes the results of a focused
technical literature review, particularly based on the taxonomy proposed by [29], which classifies
RAG approaches into three paradigms: Naive, Advanced, and Modular. These categories, ilus-
trated in Figure 2.3 reflect successive architectural refinements that address limitations such as
hallucinations, outdated content, and inefficient retrieval, offering a foundation for analyzing the
implementation choices made in this dissertation.
Figure 2.3: Comparison between the three paradigms of RAG [29]

[Vista original de la página 28](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-28.png)

![Página PDF 28, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-28.png)


<!-- PDF page 29 -->

## Página PDF 29

2.1 Exploratory Literature Review
15
2.1.9.1
Evolution of RAG Methods
Naive RAG
Naive RAG follows a straightforward three-step process: indexing, retrieval, and
generation. Documents are processed into chunks, embedded as vectors, and stored in a vector
database. When a query is issued, the system retrieves the most relevant vectors and constructs
a prompt for the LLM. This method allows external knowledge integration without extensive re-
training but suffers from retrieval inefficiencies, outdated information, and hallucinations [29].
Advanced RAG
Advanced RAG mitigates these issues through pre-retrieval and post-retrieval
optimizations. Pre-retrieval enhancements improve indexed data granularity using sliding window
chunking, metadata filtering, and dynamic embeddings, ensuring better query alignment. Post-
retrieval techniques, such as re-ranking and prompt compression, refine retrieved documents by
prioritizing relevance and reducing context noise. Re-ranking, used in frameworks like LangChain,
restructures retrieved content to maximize contextual relevance. Prompt compression, including
techniques such as Selective Context, mitigates issues like the ’lost in the middle’ phenomenon by
filtering unnecessary content [29].
Modular RAG
Modular RAG introduces a more flexible approach by incorporating additional
modules to enhance retrieval and generation. Search modules expand retrieval scope by querying
external corpora through LLM-generated code. Memory modules leverage the LLM’s internal
knowledge to refine retrieval. Extra generation modules mitigate redundancy by synthesizing
missing information. Additionally, task-adaptable and validation modules optimize RAG perfor-
mance across different applications. New retrieval patterns, such as iterative and adaptive retrieval,
allow models to refine queries dynamically. For example, the Rewrite-Retrieve-Read process em-
ploys reinforcement learning to optimize query formulations based on LLM performance, improv-
ing retrieval accuracy. The Demonstrate-Search-Predict framework treats context learning as an
explicit program, enhancing performance in knowledge-intensive tasks. These advances illustrate
how RAG systems can evolve beyond static retrieval methods to dynamic, self-improving mecha-
nisms [29].
2.1.9.2
Augmentation in RAG
Augmentation plays a crucial role in RAG by enriching retrieval process. It can occur in the
augmentation stage, through augmentation data, or in the augmentation process. The augmen-
tation data refers to the effectiveness of the data sources utilized, which can be categorized into
unstructured data, structured data, and LLM-generated content.
Augmented Unstructured data
Unstructured data includes textual corpora, fine-tuning prompts,
and cross-lingual datasets that support multilingual retrieval. Different granularities like tokens,
sentences, and paragraphs affect retrieval efficiency. Finer-grained retrieval units are particularly

[Vista original de la página 29](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-29.png)

![Página PDF 29, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-29.png)


<!-- PDF page 30 -->

## Página PDF 30

Scoping Review
16
effective for handling rare patterns and out-of-domain queries but incur higher computational costs
[29].
Augmented Structured data
Structured data, particularly knowledge graphs (KGs), provides
high-quality contextual information that reduces hallucinations and improves retrieval precision
[29]. RET-LLM, for example, constructs knowledge graph memories by extracting relational
triples from past dialogues, enhancing the contextual relevance of retrieved information [55]. Sim-
ilarly, SUGRE embeds subgraphs using Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to ensure that generated
responses reflect retrieved knowledge [39].
LLM-Generated Content RAG
LLM-generated content leverages internal model knowledge
to enhance retrieval. By synthesizing contextually relevant documents, this approach supplements
traditional retrieval methods, improving performance in downstream tasks. SKR, for instance,
classifies queries based on whether the LLM can answer them directly or requires external re-
trieval, ensuring efficient use of internal and external knowledge [92].
2.1.9.3
Enhancements in RAG Systems
This subsection is based on the survey by [103], which categorizes key enhancements in Retrieval-
Augmented Generation (RAG) systems to address challenges such as outdated knowledge, re-
trieval inefficiencies, and content quality. The taxonomy of these enhancements, as outlined in
[103], is illustrated in Figure 2.4.
Figure 2.4: Taxonomy of RAG Enhancements in AI-Generated Content [103].
Input Enhancement
Input refinement improves retrieval accuracy through query transforma-
tion and data augmentation. Query transformation techniques generate pseudo-documents to en-
rich retrieval by incorporating additional contextual information. Data augmentation enhances
document quality by removing irrelevant content, updating outdated records, and synthesizing
new data [103].

[Vista original de la página 30](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-30.png)

![Página PDF 30, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-30.png)


<!-- PDF page 31 -->

## Página PDF 31

2.1 Exploratory Literature Review
17
Retriever Enhancement
Optimizing retrieval mechanisms improves relevance and contextual
accuracy. Recursive retrieval iteratively refines queries using structured reasoning techniques, such
as Chain-of-Thought (CoT). Chunk optimization methods adjust retrieval granularity by selecting
contextually relevant segments. Hybrid retrieval combines sparse and dense retrieval techniques,
while re-ranking strategies reorder retrieved results to mitigate information loss from vector com-
pression [103].
Generator Enhancement
Generator optimization improves response coherence and faithful-
ness. Prompt engineering techniques refine LLM outputs, while decoding tuning adjusts hyper-
parameters to balance diversity and output precision. Generator fine-tuning aligns outputs with
retrieval by incorporating mechanisms such as chunked cross-attention [103].
By enhancing retrieval and generation processes, RAG systems achieve greater accuracy, ro-
bustness, and adaptability across diverse applications.
2.1.9.4
Evaluation of RAG Systems
Evaluation Methods
Evaluating RAG systems is essential to ensure reliability and effective-
ness. Evaluation methods are categorized into independent and end-to-end approaches. Indepen-
dent evaluation assesses retrieval and generation separately, using metrics such as Hit Rate, Mean
Reciprocal Rank (MRR), Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain (NDCG), and Precision to
measure retrieval quality. The generation module is evaluated based on the relevance of retrieved
documents to the query [29].
End-to-end evaluation assesses the final generated responses, ensuring alignment with re-
trieved knowledge. This can be performed manually or through automated methods using LLMs.
Key evaluation metrics include faithfulness (ensuring responses remain true to retrieved data), an-
swer relevance (measuring pertinence to the query), and context relevance (ensuring retrieved doc-
uments are accurate and appropriate). Additional capabilities such as noise robustness, negative
rejection (rejecting responses when retrieval lacks relevant data), and counterfactual robustness
(handling erroneous information) are also crucial [17, 29].
Evaluation Frameworks
Several frameworks have been developed to automate RAG evalu-
ation. RAGAS measures retrieval relevance, faithfulness, and generation quality through auto-
mated prompts and GPT-based assessments [25]. ARES refines evaluation by generating syn-
thetic datasets and fine-tuning models to assess response faithfulness and context relevance. Using
Predictive-Driven Reasoning (PDR), ARES provides statistical confidence in evaluation results,
ensuring a more reliable assessment [79, 29].
These evaluation frameworks employ LLMs as automated judges, providing scalable and effi-
cient methods for assessing RAG performance across various domains.
By integrating structured and unstructured data sources, refining retrieval mechanisms, and
employing robust evaluation methodologies, RAG systems continue to improve in accuracy, rele-
vance, and trustworthiness for complex knowledge-intensive tasks.

## Página PDF 32

Scoping Review
18
2.2
Mapping of Surveys on LLM-Based Chatbots for SCRL
This section presents an ongoing structured literature review, which began with an exploratory
phase and progressed to a mapping review, a review of existing reviews. This phased strategy
refines the problem scope and lays the foundation for a later systematic review.
The mapping review focuses specifically on surveys and literature reviews related to LLM-
based chatbots that support self- and co-regulated learning in asynchronous online software en-
gineering education. By building on validated secondary sources, this approach increases the
likelihood of identifying relevant findings and avoids the risk of conducting a new systematic re-
view without clear direction or value. It also enables a more informed understanding of prior
achievements, current limitations, and open research questions that can guide future design and
investigation.
2.2.1
Data Sources and Search Strings
The process of creating search strings was an interactive and experimental endeavor that involved
defining and grouping keywords to enhance the effectiveness of literature retrieval. Initially, key
concepts relevant to my research were identified, and a comprehensive list of keywords was gen-
erated. This included not only primary terms but also synonyms and variations to ensure a broad
capture of related literature. The keywords were then organized into thematic groups, allowing
for the strategic use of Boolean operators to combine them effectively. Multiple queries were con-
ducted iteratively, enabling refinement based on the relevance and quality of the retrieved articles.
This iterative process involved analyzing initial search results, identifying patterns, and adjusting
search strings to incorporate new insights. Ultimately, by combining the most effective keywords
from each thematic group, I developed a comprehensive search string that maximized the likeli-
hood of retrieving pertinent literature. This systematic approach ensured a thorough exploration
of the topic while documenting the search strategy for transparency and replicability.
2.2.1.1
Data Sources
The selection of data sources was made to ensure a comprehensive literature review by leveraging
accessible and reliable academic databases. This process began with an initial exploration on
Google Scholar, followed by a more refined search on Scopus for enhanced search capabilities.
• Google Scholar – Exploratory Review: The exploratory review, as detailed in Section 2.1.2,
in which key terms and conceptual frameworks were defined, was conducted exclusively
through Google Scholar. This platform was selected for its accessibility, breadth of cov-
erage, and user-friendly search capabilities. It enabled the identification of foundational
works and emerging concepts related to large language models, self-regulated learning, co-
regulation, and asynchronous education. Notably, [53] report that Google Scholar indexes
approximately 88% of all citations, making it a powerful starting point for exploratory in-
vestigations.

2.2 Mapping of Surveys on LLM-Based Chatbots for SCRL
19
• Scopus – Mapping of Surveys: For the subsequent mapping of literature reviews and sur-
veys, the Scopus database was used due to its advanced filtering and structured metadata.
Scopus includes only peer-reviewed publications, ensuring source quality. Its search inter-
face allows precise filtering by subject area, document type, and publication year, which
was instrumental in narrowing results to those directly relevant to LLM-based chatbots and
self- and co-regulated learning. Citation tracking features also helped to identify influential
works and thematic trends within the scope of this review.
This two-phase approach, starting with an open exploratory search and refining with a curated
academic database, allowed the review to balance breadth with depth, supporting both conceptual
clarity and methodological rigor.
2.2.1.2
Thematic Categories of Keywords
To ensure an effective and comprehensive search strategy, thematic categories of keywords were
defined based on the foundational concepts outlined in the previous sections. These categories
incorporate not only the core keywords as "Chatbot", "Large Language Model", "Self-regulation",
"Co-regulation", "Online asynchronous learning", and "Software Engineering", but also their syn-
onyms and closely related terms. The inclusion of synonyms and related words broadens the
scope of the search to capture more relevant literature while accounting for variations in terminol-
ogy used across studies. It is important to clarify that, throughout this work, the term "synonyms"
refers to both exact synonyms and terms that are closely related in context. These closely related
terms will be collectively referred to as "synonyms" for simplicity.
The thematic categories of keywords, along with their corresponding synonyms or related
terms, are shown below in Table 2.1. This table provides a comprehensive overview of the search
terms used to ensure that they are transparent.
2.2.1.3
Search Strings
Search strings were developed iteratively to maximize the retrieval of relevant literature while
minimizing noise. Initial testing was conducted on Google Scholar due to its accessibility, but
limitations such as a 256-character cap and lack of filtering led to thousands of inconsistent results
with low specificity.
To address these issues, the final search was conducted on Scopus, which offers advanced
features such as Boolean operators, field-specific searches, and filters by document type and pub-
lication year. These tools enabled the construction of precise queries that reflected the study’s
scope.
Keywords were grouped strategically to broaden the search scope and improve relevance. At-
tempts to restrict results strictly to online asynchronous education or software engineering yielded
too few papers, confirming the need to broaden terms. Techniques such as wildcards (*) were
used to capture both singular and plural forms, and proximity operators (W/2) were employed

to capture both "self-regulation of learning" and "self-regulated learning", enhancing the search’s

Given the recency of the topic, the search was limited to literature reviews published after
2020 to ensure the inclusion of only the most current and relevant developments. This cutoff was
justified by the observation that most pertinent publications emerged after this date, particularly
following the release of transformative technologies such as ChatGPT in 2022. This temporal
restriction aligns with the exploratory nature of this research, which aims to capture emerging
trends and innovations in the application of AI to education. To further refine the results, certain
document types, such as book chapters, editorials, notes, and discussion papers, were excluded
to maintain a focus on peer-reviewed literature reviews. Although no explicit language filter was
applied during the initial exploratory phase, the final search was limited to English-language pub-
lications to ensure consistency, accessibility, and alignment with international academic standards.

2.2.2
Eligibility
To determine study eligibility, inclusion and exclusion criteria were applied to ensure that selected
literature aligned closely with this research on the use of LLM-based chatbots to support self
and/or co-regulated learning in educational contexts. Papers were included if they were literature
reviews or surveys, published after 2020, and written in English, following the reasons laid out in
Section 2.2.1.3. Eligible papers also needed to provide relevant insights into at least one aspect
of the core concept, educational context, or perspective of the research. Excluded documents
included non-systematic sources that lack the structured, research-oriented approach needed for
a comprehensive review, as well as papers written in languages other than English. During the
screening process, papers were excluded if their titles or abstracts indicated a misalignment with
any of the defined concepts, context, or perspective. The criteria chosen is presented in Table 2.2.
Table 2.2: Inclusion and Exclusion Criteria
Type
Criteria
Inclusion
Is a literature review or survey.
Is relevant to at least one aspect of the concept, con-
text and perspective.
Is written in English.
Is published after 2020.
Exclusion
Is not a literature review or survey.
Is not relevant to at least one aspect of the concept,
context and perspective.
Is not written in English.
Is published before or in 2020.
2.2.3
Screening Process and Results
The screening process involved reviewing the titles and abstracts of the papers retrieved through
the search strategy. All papers were initially evaluated against predefined inclusion and exclusion
criteria.
In the first phase, quick eliminations were made based on title and abstract, removing papers
that were not literature reviews, not in English, or clearly unrelated to the topic. From the initial

18 results retrieved from Scopus, 5 papers were immediately excluded for not addressing any of
the key themes, leaving 13 papers for further analysis.
In the second phase, abstract screening revealed that 2 papers with unusual titles had captured
the search terms incorrectly. One referred to SRL as "Semantic Role Labeling" rather than "Self-
Regulated Learning" and another to CRL as "Collaborative Reinforcement Learning", rather than
"Co-Regulated Learning". This refinement reduced the list to 11 potentially relevant studies for
further analysis.
The final phase of exclusion involved a deeper reading of the abstracts of each study, elimi-
nating literature reviews that did not meet the criteria, such as those focusing on lower education
instead of higher education. Additionally, papers that discussed AI outside the context of conver-
sational agents and LLMs were excluded. Some papers appeared relevant but did not focus on self-
and co-regulation. Others, while concentrating on self and co-regulation, did not reference any of
the technologies of interest, such as LLMs or chatbots. As a result, 6 reviews were eliminated,
leaving a final selection of 5 relevant papers:
• How educational chatbots support self-regulated learning? A systematic review of the liter-
ature [30]
• A review of chatbot-assisted learning: pedagogical approaches, implementations, factors
leading to effectiveness, theories, and future directions [102]
• The influence of ChatGPT on student engagement: A systematic review and future research
agenda [52]
• Impact of Artificial Intelligence on Learning Management Systems: A Bibliometric Review
[89]
• Evaluating AI’s impact on self-regulated language learning: A systematic review [14]

Scoping Review
24
2.3
Thematic Analysis of Selected Reviews
Following the identification and selection of the five most relevant literature reviews, the next stage
of the literature review consisted of a detailed thematic analysis of these studies. This analysis
aimed to extract patterns, insights, and research gaps related to the use of LLM-based chatbots
in the context of higher education, particularly in support of self-regulated learning (SRL). The
methodology followed in this phase was based on the approach proposed by [88], enabling a
systematic organization of themes and subthemes across the selected articles.
2.3.1
Guiding Research Questions
To structure the thematic analysis conducted in this phase, a set of five Guiding Research Ques-
tions (GRQs) was formulated during the initial scoping phase. These questions were not only
instrumental in shaping the inclusion criteria but also helped identify key themes in the selected
literature and ensured alignment with the overall research focus of this dissertation:
• GRQ1: What are the current characteristics of LLM-based chatbots designed to support
SRL?
• GRQ2: What are the current techniques used for LLM-based chatbots designed to support
SRL?
• GRQ3: How are LLM-based chatbots utilized to support learners across the phases of self-
regulated learning (forethought, performance and self-reflection)?
• GRQ4: What are the design principles and processes that contribute to the effectiveness of
LLM-based chatbots in supporting all phases of SRL?
• GRQ5: What are the challenges and limitations that contribute to the ineffectiveness of
LLM-based chatbots in supporting all phases of SRL?
It is important to clarify that, from this point onward, the focus of the literature analysis is
exclusively on self-regulated learning (SRL). Although the initial mapping phase included both
self-regulation and co-regulation in its search strategy, the results revealed a significant gap in
the literature concerning co-regulated learning in the context of LLM-based chatbots in higher
education. Therefore, the thematic analysis presented here concentrates on SRL, in alignment
with the primary objective of this dissertation: to design and evaluate an LLM-based chatbot that
supports individual self-regulated learning in asynchronous online software engineering education.
2.3.2
Methodology
The thematic analysis followed involved systematically extracting, categorizing, and analyzing
qualitative data to identify patterns and themes.

[Vista original de la página 38](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-38.png)

![Página PDF 38, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-38.png)

## Página PDF 39

2.3 Thematic Analysis of Selected Reviews
25
Initially, articles were filtered based on their relevance to the research topic. Accessibility was
verified through institutional subscriptions, publicly available repositories, or by directly contact-
ing authors to request access. Articles that remained inaccessible were in unreadable languages,
or lacked peer-reviewed publication status were excluded from the analysis.
An Excel sheet was prepared to document the reasons for exclusion, the number of excluded
articles, and the final list of articles selected for detailed analysis. Each article was assigned a
unique identifier (e.g., A1, A2,...) for ease of reference throughout the study.
From the selected articles, relevant excerpts were extracted that directly addressed the research
objectives. Priority was given to using exact quotations to preserve the original context but lengthy
or overly detailed excerpts were truncated using ellipses.
The extracted elements were subjected to thematic analysis. Each element was systematically
grouped and refined to form broader themes through processes of classification, comparison, and
labeling. Themes were iteratively clarified to ensure their coherence and objectivity.
The themes were evaluated in relation to the research objectives. Relevant themes were in-
tegrated into the research framework, while irrelevant themes were excluded. Additionally, the
analysis highlighted areas not addressed by the identified themes, thus uncovering knowledge
gaps in the literature.
This structured methodology enabled a comprehensive exploration of the selected literature,
ensuring consistency with the research aims and providing a foundation for identifying future
research directions.
2.3.3
Overview of the themes identified
Themes were derived from relevant excerpts extracted and grouped based on conceptual similar-
ity, reflecting the main patterns related to LLM-based chatbots and Self-Regulated Learning. To
ensure coherence with this dissertation’s research goals and questions, only themes that aligned
with the defined objectives were retained and analyzed further. In particular, themes related to
chatbot benefits, implementation strategies, and SRL models are directly related to GRQ1–GRQ5
and help structure the interpretation of current practices, design approaches, and research gaps in
the field.
After completing the accessibility verification process, all five articles selected during the pre-
vious phase were made available for analysis. Four of these articles were accessed through insti-
tutional subscriptions provided by the University of Porto. The fifth article, initially inaccessible,
was successfully obtained by contacting the author via ResearchGate.
A preliminary review of the articles confirmed that they were all written in English and pub-
lished in peer-reviewed journals. This ensured that the selected works met the inclusion criteria
and provided a robust foundation for thematic analysis.
The articles have been assigned the following identifiers for reference throughout this docu-
ment:

This thematic analysis revealed a diverse set of themes across the selected articles, reflect-
ing both the opportunities and challenges associated with using chatbots in education and their
potential to support Self-Regulated Learning.
The most frequently discussed themes were "Chatbots benefits in education" (T1) and "Chat-
bots potential in education" (T2), with total frequencies of 33 and 24 occurrences, respectively.

[Vista original de la página 40](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-40.png)

## Página PDF 41

2.3 Thematic Analysis of Selected Reviews
27
These results underscore a prevailing optimism about the evolving potential of chatbots in ed-
ucation. They are frequently cited as tools that can enhance engagement, personalize learning
experiences, and provide scalable support for diverse learner needs. However, the theme "Chat-
bots limitations in education" (T3), with 16 occurrences, introduced critical perspectives. These
limitations primarily concerned practical challenges such as user acceptance, technical constraints,
and the need for robust implementation strategies to maximize their effectiveness.
Other themes explored the nuances of chatbot deployment in educational contexts. For in-
stance, "Chatbots current support" (T4), "Chatbots effective implementation" (T6) and "Chatbots
mixed results in education" (T7), with frequencies of 15, 5 and 4, respectively, indicated that while
chatbots have shown promise in providing immediate feedback and fostering engagement, their
impact is not universally consistent. Conversely, themes like "Chatbots ineffective implementa-
tion" (T9) and "Chatbots technology" (T8) were scarcely represented, suggesting that discussions
about the technical foundations and failed implementations of chatbots remain underdeveloped in
the current literature.
Themes related to SRL revealed a focus on foundational models and concepts. "SRL model by
Zimmerman" (T11) and "SRL definition" (T12), appearing 5 and 4 times, respectively, reflected
the theoretical grounding of SRL research. These themes were complemented by "SRL benefits"
(T13) and "SRL current support" (T14), which highlighted the potential advantages of integrating
SRL principles into chatbot applications in education.
The role of Learning Management Systems (LMS) emerged uniquely in article A4, where
"LMS definition and characteristics" (T17) was discussed with a frequency of 6. This theme
underscored the importance of chatbots integration into LMS platforms to enable efficient and
meaningful interactions in asynchronous educational settings.
"Future research" (T18) and "Gaps in the research" (T19) highlighted areas requiring further
investigation, appearing 15 and 12 times, respectively. These themes pointed to significant oppor-
tunities for advancing the field by exploring underdeveloped areas.
In summary, the thematic analysis of the 5 papers provides a structured overview of current
research on LLM-based chatbots and their relationship to Self-Regulated Learning in educational
settings. It highlights a strong emphasis on the perceived benefits and potential of chatbots, while
also revealing practical limitations and inconsistent outcomes across implementations. Although
SRL is theoretically recognized, its integration into chatbot systems is often limited to surface-
level references, with few articles explicitly aligning with established SRL models such as [104]’s.
As previously mentioned, the co-regulation of learning is also notably absent from the reviewed
literature. Additionally, the integration of chatbots into Learning Management Systems, particu-
larly within asynchronous online learning environments, is only briefly addressed. These findings
set the stage for the discussion that follows, which further examines their implications in light of
the research questions and identifies concrete opportunities for future work.
