## Página PDF 42

Scoping Review
28
2.4
Discussion
This section synthesizes the key findings from the thematic analysis of the 5 surveys and explores
their implications within the context of Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) and the use of LLM-based
chatbots in educational settings. It discusses emerging trends, current practices, and the integra-
tion of chatbots to support SRL. The section concludes by identifying critical research gaps and
proposing directions for future studies to advance the use of chatbots for SRL support in education.
2.4.1
Self-Regulated Learning
The state of the art revealed theoretical frameworks of SRL, explored its benefits, and discusses
the challenges that learners face in effectively employing SRL strategies. By examining these
elements, the aim is to understand the complexity of SRL and the importance of fostering self-
regulation in educational settings.
2.4.1.1
SRL Definition and Frameworks
As identified in Section 2.1.5.1, the surveys analyzed reinforced that Self-Regulated Learning
(SRL) is conceptualized as a dynamic and cyclical process in which learners set goals, em-
ploy learning strategies, monitor their progress, and adapt their approaches based on outcomes
[67]. Several prominent theoretical models describe the SRL process. Zimmerman’s Three-Phase
Model [105], was the most widely adopted framework. Additionally, Winne and Hadwin’s COPES
Model [97] was identified in the literature and emphasizes cognitive facets such as conditions, op-
erations, products, evaluations, and standards. It also describes the learning process as occurring
in four key phases: task definition, goal setting and planning, enacting study tactics, and adapting
future strategies. These frameworks underscore the complexity of SRL, highlighting its recur-
sive, goal-oriented nature and the multifaceted skills it involves. It is also noteworthy that other
theoretical frameworks, such as [96]’s and [74]’s, were referenced but not explicitly discussed or
explained.
Therefore, the Three-Phase Model by [105]’s, and its later refinement by [106], is re-affirmed
as the primary theoretical framework adopted in this dissertation, given its widespread acceptance
among researchers and its ability to systematically structure each key component of the SRL pro-
cess, as discussed in Section 2.1.5.1.
2.4.1.2
Benefits and Challenges of SRL
According to the papers, Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) has been shown to significantly improve
academic performance and support lifelong learning [19, 40]. Key advantages of SRL include
enhanced goal-setting, strategic planning, and metacognitive monitoring, which ultimately lead to
improved learning outcomes and self-efficacy. Furthermore, SRL promotes the transfer of skills

[Vista original de la página 42](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-42.png)

![Página PDF 42, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-42.png)


<!-- PDF page 43 -->

## Página PDF 43

2.4 Discussion
29
to new contexts, enabling students to apply strategies learned in one area to future learning chal-
lenges. This adaptability makes SRL crucial not only for immediate academic success but also for
fostering lifelong learners capable of navigating diverse learning environments.
However, despite these benefits, SRL remains a challenging skill set for many learners. Com-
mon difficulties include inadequate identification of relevant learning resources [48], struggles
with setting specific and attainable goals, and difficulty selecting effective strategies [54, 6]. Fur-
thermore, poor metacognitive monitoring and evaluation of progress often sets back the devel-
opment of SRL [104, 33]. These challenges highlight the need for scaffolding mechanisms that
support learners in developing the necessary skills for effective self-regulation.
2.4.2
LLM-based Chatbots
In exploring the state of the art on LLM-based chatbots, key insights regarding their definition,
characteristics, and relevance to educational contexts were identified, particularly in supporting
self-regulated learning. This section outlines these findings, starting with an overview of their
fundamental attributes.
2.4.2.1
Chatbots Definition and Characteristics
As previously discussed in the exploratory literature review (Section 2.1.3), chatbots have evolved
from early rule-based systems to more advanced generative models, particularly those leveraging
large language models (LLMs). Theme T5 from the thematic analysis further highlighted how
recent studies vary in their definitions and emphasis on key chatbot characteristics. This subsection
consolidates those insights, with a focus on the attributes most relevant to the role of LLM-based
chatbots in supporting Self-Regulated Learning (SRL).
Literature identified describes educational chatbots as interactive programs that leverage artifi-
cial intelligence (AI) and natural language processing (NLP) to simulate human-like conversations
via text or voice [30]. These tools are designed to dynamically and adaptively interact with learn-
ers, offering tailored responses and fostering engagement across diverse educational scenarios.
NLP-driven chatbots, in particular, exhibit robustness in interpreting user inputs, even when those
inputs are unclear or grammatically incorrect. This flexibility enables them to support a wide range
of learners, accommodating varied communication styles and enhancing accessibility.
In essence, chatbots represent digital tools capable of simulating human conversations, with
their foundational mechanisms rooted in NLP technologies [102]. Their application in educa-
tional settings highlights their potential to facilitate SRL by providing interactive, adaptive, and
personalized learning experiences.
2.4.2.2
Chatbots Current Support and Benefits in SRL
Research has shown that chatbots have demonstrated their ability to support self-regulated learn-
ing (SRL) processes by assisting learners in enacting essential strategies, monitoring their under-
standing, and improving cognitive efficiency. They enhance student engagement, foster the use of

[Vista original de la página 43](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-43.png)

![Página PDF 43, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-43.png)


<!-- PDF page 44 -->

## Página PDF 44

Scoping Review
30
effective learning strategies, and improve self-efficacy and critical thinking skills [30].
In educational contexts, chatbots provide a range of benefits. They deliver rich instructional
support tailored to individual learner needs, including personalized learning schedules and im-
mediate feedback that keeps learners engaged and helps them adapt during the learning process
[82, 46]. Additionally, chatbots assist in critical activities such as task orientation and metacog-
nitive monitoring, offering personalized feedback and prompts that help learners reflect on their
progress, set goals, and adjust strategies as needed [5, 75]. By offering these supports, chatbots
help learners manage their cognitive load, reducing the mental burden that often arises from com-
plex tasks, and promoting active engagement in the learning process [76, 34].
Furthermore, chatbots offer opportunities for repetitive practice to reinforce knowledge acqui-
sition and improve learning efficiency [12]. They also enhance the authenticity of the learning
experience by creating immersive, real-world scenarios that promote critical thinking and evoke
positive emotional responses [90, 86]. Pedagogically, chatbots play a vital role in fostering auton-
omy and engagement by presenting guiding questions and prompts that encourage self-regulation
[77]. They support collaborative academic activities, such as writing tasks, by providing feedback
and encouragement, while also serving as learning companions that offer emotional support and
foster feelings of enjoyment, security, and motivation [84].
Many learners using chatbots report entering a "flow state", characterized by heightened fo-
cus, positive emotions, and increased learning efficiency, aligning with flow theory principles of
[21]. While some studies highlight high self-efficacy and motivation facilitated by chatbots, oth-
ers report mixed results, indicating that outcomes may vary depending on the implementation and
context [42]. Chatbots equipped with SRL-oriented features provide significant advantages by
enabling learners to personalize their academic tasks [99], encouraging self-regulation through
guiding questions, and fostering autonomy in environments conducive to high levels of engage-
ment and interaction [21, 84].
Both [24] and [7] highlighted DialogFlow as a widely used Natural Language Processing
(NLP) platform for developing chatbots aimed at supporting Self-Regulated Learning (SRL). No-
tably, this was the only specific technology referenced for chatbot development in SRL across the
reviewed articles.
2.4.2.3
ChatGPT Role in Supporting SRL
ChatGPT aligns particularly well with SRL principles, which involve a cyclical process compris-
ing forethought, performance, and self-reflection [104]. During the forethought phase, ChatGPT
assists learners in analyzing tasks, setting goals, and devising strategies [2, 3]. In the performance
phase, it helps learners navigate challenges by providing tailored feedback and fostering a pro-
gressive learning journey [4]. In the self-reflection phase, ChatGPT facilitates critical evaluation
by offering personalized feedback on learning outcomes, encouraging learners to refine and adapt
their strategies [11]. By supporting these stages of SRL, ChatGPT helps learners monitor their
progress and make adjustments to their learning strategies.

[Vista original de la página 44](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-44.png)

![Página PDF 44, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-44.png)


<!-- PDF page 45 -->

## Página PDF 45

2.4 Discussion
31
In addition to supporting this SRL cycle, ChatGPT enhances learners’ ability to regulate their
pace and style of study, which improves their management of academic tasks [10]. It can assist
students in generating a personalized learning dashboard within a learning management system,
enabling them to reflect on their progress at various stages of their learning and identify areas for
improvement in an iterative cycle [16]. It also boosts self-efficacy by increasing learners’ confi-
dence in their ability to perform and succeed [98]. Furthermore, it fosters cognitive engagement
through guiding questions and prompts, encouraging active participation and critical thinking,
which are central to effective SRL [9]. These capabilities highlight ChatGPT’s potential as a
virtual tutor that helps students develop and apply SRL strategies effectively.
2.4.2.4
Chatbots in Learning Management Systems
LMS are widely used platforms designed to facilitate course delivery, resource management, inter-
action, and progress monitoring [89]. The integration of chatbots into LMS has significantly en-
hanced their functionality, enabling adaptive and personalized learning experiences. AI-powered
chatbots offer instant responses and real-time feedback, support self-directed learning, and pro-
vide intelligent tutoring capabilities. By dynamically adjusting to individual learners’ needs, these
tools foster engagement and improve learning outcomes.
The synergy between LMS and chatbots creates intelligent educational systems that amplify
teaching and learning activities. Chatbots enhance interaction between students and instructors, re-
ducing isolation in online environments and encouraging meaningful dialogue. They also leverage
learning analytics to provide personalized recommendations and optimize educational experiences
[89].
In the context of SRL, chatbots integrated into LMS foster autonomy by offering tailored
feedback and adaptive support. They promote self-regulation of learning through personalized
prompts and create interactive environments that align with SRL theories. These systems not
only improve learner outcomes but also address the challenges of maintaining engagement and
motivation in asynchronous online learning settings.
2.4.2.5
Challenges and Limitations of Chatbots in Education
Despite their potential, educational chatbots face several limitations. Chatbots frequently show
insufficient responsiveness to evolving learner conditions, such as motivation and self-efficacy.
While some studies report that chatbots can improve learning outcomes, the effects on learning
performance remain mixed, with some studies showing negligible improvements due to the nov-
elty effect or poor alignment with specific learning tasks [30]. Additionally, learners may struggle
to maintain sustained engagement with chatbots, possibly preferring direct interaction with in-
structors rather than a digital assistant.
In addition to the limitations of individual chatbot implementations, several broader challenges
exist in their adoption and effectiveness in educational settings. One such challenge is the limited
intelligence of current chatbots, which may not meet the complex needs of learners. Moreover,

[Vista original de la página 45](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-45.png)

![Página PDF 45, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-45.png)

## Página PDF 46

Scoping Review
32
many students remain unfamiliar with this learning method, which can hinder its acceptance and
effectiveness. The cognitive load associated with chatbot interactions can also be a barrier to
learning, as it may detract from the cognitive resources available for the task at hand [37]. Fur-
ther complicating matters, many chatbot implementations are short-term, providing little insight
into their long-term impact on learning. Additionally, chatbots often lack comprehensive support
across disciplines and educational contexts, limiting their ability to scale effectively in diverse
environments. While some studies report increases in self-efficacy and motivation as a result of
chatbot use, other research has found mixed results, highlighting the variability of outcomes [42].
When integrated into learning management systems (LMS), chatbots face additional chal-
lenges. Ethical concerns, particularly regarding privacy and data security, are a significant hurdle
that must be addressed for broader adoption [89]. Moreover, the technical limitations of AI inte-
gration, including the developing state of chatbot technologies and the lack of standardized imple-
mentations, make it difficult to establish robust and scalable systems. These challenges underscore
the need for further research and development to ensure that chatbot-enhanced LMS platforms can
reach their full potential in diverse educational settings.
2.4.3
Research Gaps
The current body of research on chatbot-assisted Self-Regulated Learning highlights several key
gaps that must be addressed to fully realize the potential of chatbots in educational contexts. One
major gap is the insufficient understanding of how chatbot implementations can offer holistic as-
sistance across all phases of SRL [30]. While some chatbots target specific phases of SRL, there
is limited research on how these technologies can offer comprehensive support across all stages
of SRL, as outlined by prominent SRL models such as those of Zimmerman [105] and Winne
and Hadwin [97]. Additionally, current implementations often lack the ability to collect real-time
data on learners’ internal conditions, such as their motivation, emotional state, or cognitive load,
which restricts the chatbot’s ability to provide adaptive and immediate feedback. This limitation
highlights the need for more advanced systems that can dynamically adjust to learners’ evolv-
ing needs. Furthermore, there is a lack of longitudinal studies evaluating the long-term impact
of chatbot-assisted learning on students’ SRL processes and overall academic performance [30].
While short-term studies have shown promise, more research is needed to determine the sustained
benefits and challenges associated with the integration of chatbots in SRL over extended periods.
Another gap is the limited exploration of the effectiveness of chatbots in fostering the trans-
fer of SRL skills across various contexts and disciplines. Most studies have focused on specific
subjects or narrow educational settings, with insufficient attention paid to the broader applicability
of chatbot-supported SRL [102]. Furthermore, the integration of advanced frameworks such as
SRL into chatbot systems is still in its early stages, with many implementations failing to reduce
cognitive load or enhance deep engagement [89]. This issue underscores the importance of refin-
ing chatbot designs to support the cognitive and emotional aspects of learning more effectively. In
addition, while some chatbots show potential in addressing specific SRL phases, the lack of clarity
regarding which phases benefit most from chatbot assistance leaves significant room for further

[Vista original de la página 46](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-46.png)

![Página PDF 46, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-46.png)

## Página PDF 47

2.5 Conclusion
33
investigation [30]. As chatbots are increasingly integrated into learning management systems,
the potential for privacy and data security issues also needs to be addressed, which complicates
broader adoption and implementation [89].
2.4.4
Future Research Directions
To bridge these research gaps and advance the integration of chatbots into SRL frameworks, sev-
eral key avenues for future research must be explored. First, there is a need for the development
of chatbots that can comprehensively support all phases of SRL [30]. By integrating advanced
generative AI technologies, chatbots can be made more adaptable and capable of responding with
greater accuracy to learners’ individual needs and learning contexts, as suggested by [30]. Fur-
thermore, to better understand the long-term effectiveness of chatbot-assisted SRL, longitudinal
studies are essential. These studies would provide valuable insights into the sustained impact of
chatbots on learning outcomes and self-regulation, allowing researchers to evaluate whether the
benefits of chatbot assistance persist over time or diminish after initial engagement.
Another critical direction for future research is the exploration of chatbots’ ability to dynami-
cally elicit and adapt to students’ internal conditions, such as emotional states or motivation, during
learning sessions. By incorporating emotion detection and adaptive response features, chatbots
could more closely mimic the guidance and support provided by human tutors [75]. Addition-
ally, expanding the application of chatbot-assisted SRL across diverse disciplines and pedagogical
scenarios is crucial. Chatbots should be tested in a variety of educational contexts to determine
their versatility and effectiveness in different learning environments. Moreover, more research is
needed on how SRL-oriented features within chatbot systems can reduce cognitive load and foster
deeper engagement with learning tasks [34]. Investigating how guiding questions and adaptive
feedback can more effectively support SRL strategies is also vital for improving the quality of
chatbot interactions and ensuring that they foster meaningful learning experiences.
Lastly, specific research on integrating SRL frameworks with AI-driven technologies such as
ChatGPT is necessary to explore the unique ways this tool can enhance self-regulation. [52] high-
light the importance of conducting longitudinal studies to assess the long-term effects of ChatGPT
on SRL processes. Furthermore, targeted strategies within ChatGPT that focus on enhancing spe-
cific SRL phases, particularly self-reflection and performance, should be developed to increase the
chatbot’s pedagogical value. By broadening the scope of research across various educational lev-
els and disciplines, the effectiveness and impact of chatbot-assisted SRL can be better understood,
leading to more robust implementations in educational systems.
2.5
Conclusion
The research gaps identified in this section are explicitly drawn from the reviewed literature, where
under-explored areas were mentioned by the authors of the articles, and future research directions
were explicitly stated in the literature reviews themselves. To organize these findings, the gaps
were categorized based on [104]’s self-regulated learning (SRL) phases and and complemented by

Based on the gaps and future directions identified in the previous sections, the choice of fur-
ther exploring the integration of chatbots that provide holistic support across all phases of Self-
Regulated Learning, in asynchronous online higher education was taken. This decision is moti-
vated by the recognition that, while chatbots have shown promise in supporting specific phases of
SRL, there is a significant gap in their ability to address the entire SRL cycle. [104]’s model high-
lights the interconnected phases of forethought, performance, and self-reflection, each of which
plays a crucial role in enabling learners to effectively set goals, engage in learning activities, and
reflect on their progress.
Existing research indicates that SRL requires a continuous and cyclical process that integrates
all three phases to enhance learner autonomy and foster deeper learning. However, current chatbot
implementations often focus on supporting only one or two phases of the cycle, leaving gaps
in their ability to offer holistic support throughout the SRL process. By focusing on chatbots
that offer comprehensive support across all phases of [106]’s model, I aim to address these gaps
and contribute to the development of more adaptive and effective chatbot systems. This focus
is consistent with emerging trends in AI-driven educational technologies, which are increasingly
capable of providing personalized and dynamic support tailored to the specific needs of learners
across all phases.

## Página PDF 49

2.5 Conclusion
35
The process of conducting a thematic analysis has been instrumental in guiding this research
and writing this state of the art. By systematically analyzing key research articles, I was able to
identify recurring themes and trends that informed my understanding of the current state of the art.
This process highlighted not only the strengths of current chatbot applications in SRL but also the
limitations and gaps in research. The insights gained from this process have therefore guided my
research to focus on how chatbots can fill these gaps and offer continuous support throughout the
entire SRL cycle, that will take place in Chapter 3.
While this thematic analysis offered valuable insights into the current research landscape, it
also presents certain limitations. First, the review was restricted to five articles due to its narrow
focus on recent literature reviews published after 2020. Although this aligns with the goal of cap-
turing recent developments, it may have excluded older studies with practical relevance. Second,
only English-language peer-reviewed articles indexed in Scopus were included, introducing po-
tential language and database bias. Third, as a thematic analysis, the emphasis was on identifying
and organizing themes rather than critically appraising the evidence, which limits the depth of
conclusions that can be drawn. Finally, despite being systematic, the thematic coding process in-
volves some subjectivity, especially when interpreting ambiguous or multi-faceted excerpts. These
limitations should be taken into account when interpreting the findings.

## Página PDF 50

Chapter 3
Systematic Literature Review
3.1
Introduction
"LLM-based chatbots that provide a comprehensive SRL support in asynchronous online higher
education" systematic literature review was conducted using the Parsif.al platform, following a
structured methodology. The process included defining the research protocol, importing relevant
studies, applying inclusion and exclusion criteria, extracting key data, and systematically reporting
the findings.
3.2
Objective
This systematic review aims to identify the current state of knowledge on the development of
LLM-based chatbots, examining various approaches, distinctive characteristics, and underlying
processes that can effectively support learners, in asynchronous online higher education, through-
out all phases of Zimmerman’s Self-regulated Learning cycle: forethought, performance and self-
reflection, mentioned in Section 2.1.5.1.
3.3
PICOC Framework
To guide this systematic literature review, the PICOC framework (Population, Intervention, Com-
parison, Outcomes, Context) was adopted. This framework provides a structured approach to
defining the scope of the review and ensuring relevance to the research objectives. Below is the
detailed application of PICOC:
• Population: The review focuses on students enrolled in higher education programs, partic-
ularly those participating in asynchronous online learning environments. This population is
relevant because the development of self-regulation of learning skills is crucial for success
in such contexts.
36

[Vista original de la página 50](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-50.png)

![Página PDF 50, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-50.png)

## Página PDF 51

3.4 Addressing the Research Questions
37
• Intervention: The primary intervention under investigation is the use of Large Language
Model-based chatbots designed to support Self-Regulated Learning. These chatbots lever-
age artificial intelligence to provide tailored feedback and guidance aimed at fostering self-
regulatory behaviors.
• Comparison: The study examines and compares the characteristics and techniques em-
ployed by LLM-based chatbots to support each phase of SRL as defined by Zimmerman’s
model (forethought, performance, and self-reflection). By identifying similarities and dif-
ferences, this review aims to highlight effective practices and potential gaps in the design
and implementation of such systems.
• Outcome: The desired outcome is to identify the characteristics and functionalities of LLM-
based chatbots that effectively support all phases of SRL. This includes assessing both their
theoretical alignment with SRL principles and their practical impact on learners’ abilities to
self-regulate.
• Context: The context of this review is higher education, specifically in asynchronous online
learning environments. These settings present unique challenges for SRL, such as the lack
of immediate instructor feedback. The role of LLM-based chatbots as potential facilitators
of SRL in this context is a central focus of the study.
This structured approach ensures that the scope of the review is clear and that the findings are
directly aligned with the research objectives of this dissertation.
3.4
Addressing the Research Questions
The research questions outlined in Section 2.3.1 serve as the guiding framework for this research,
shaping its methodological and analytical approach.
This systematic review investigates key
aspects of LLM-based chatbots designed to support Self-Regulated Learning (SRL), aiming to
provide a comprehensive understanding of their potential and limitations in asynchronous online
higher education.
The study examines the current characteristics and technologies underpinning LLM-based
chatbots, exploring how these systems operate and the technological frameworks and architectures
they employ (GRQ1-GRQ2). Particular attention is given to the methods used for data collection
and processing, which are essential for validating the functionality and adaptability of these chat-
bots (GRQ2).
A central focus of this investigation is on how LLM-based chatbots support learners across
the three phases of SRL: forethought, performance, and self-reflection (GRQ3). This includes
analyzing the strategies and approaches implemented to foster self-regulation in online learning
environments, addressing the specific needs of learners in each phase.

[Vista original de la página 51](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-51.png)

![Página PDF 51, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-51.png)


<!-- PDF page 52 -->

## Página PDF 52

Systematic Literature Review
38
Furthermore, this review seeks to identify the design principles and processes that contribute
to the effectiveness of LLM-based chatbots (GRQ4). By examining successful implementations,
it highlights features and approaches that align with the principles of SRL.
Finally, the research addresses the challenges and limitations associated with the development
and deployment of these systems (GRQ5). By identifying areas where LLM-based chatbots fall
short, it offers insights into their current weaknesses and provides recommendations for future
improvements.
Through this systematic literature review and critical analysis, the dissertation synthesizes
findings from the existing body of knowledge, contributing to the advancement of LLM-based
chatbot design for SRL.
3.5
Search String
This systematic literature review differs from the previous review conducted in Chapter 2 in both
purpose and methodology. While the review of reviews aimed to broadly explore the landscape
of LLM-based chatbots in education and identify general research directions, the current review
adopts a more rigorous and targeted approach to examine how these chatbots support all phases of
Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) in asynchronous online higher education.
The search string employed in this systematic literature review was carefully designed to en-
sure comprehensive yet targeted retrieval of relevant studies. The thematic categories of keywords
used in this search were validated in Chapter 2, where they were established as part of a broader
literature exploration. Minor adjustments were made to these categories, with some expansions to
better reflect the scope of the research while preserving their alignment with the research objec-
tives.
The search string was constructed based on the PICOC framework, focusing primarily on the
Intervention (LLM-based chatbots) and the Population (students in asynchronous online higher
education). Including other elements of the PICOC framework, such as Comparison or Outcomes,
would have excessively constrained the search results, yielding much fewer studies. This targeted
approach maximizes the likelihood of identifying pertinent literature while minimizing irrelevant
or overly broad results.
The search string was applied in the Scopus database, selected for its broad disciplinary cov-
erage and its reputation for delivering a variety of high-quality, peer-reviewed research results.
Scopus offers an extensive index of academic literature, ensuring that the review captures a wide
range of perspectives and studies related to the research objectives.
The finalized search string, presented below, integrates terms and phrases that encompass vari-
ations of keywords related to self-regulated learning, chatbots, large language models, and higher
education. Unlike the previous review-focused query, this search targeted original empirical pa-
pers, broadened the language scope to include Portuguese, and refined educational terms. Boolean
operators, truncation (*), and proximity search (W/2) were maintained to ensure both flexibility
and precision. Restricting the search to not only English but also Portuguese aimed to increase

39
coverage while ensuring that all selected studies could be properly understood and analyzed. Ad-
ditionally, only studies published after 2020 were analyzed to prioritize recent advancements in
the field and review articles, surveys, and conference papers were excluded to focus on original
and empirical studies.

3.6
Selection Criteria
To ensure the relevance and quality of studies included in this systematic review, a set of inclu-
sion and exclusion criteria was defined. These criteria were designed to align with the primary
objective of this research: exploring how LLM-based chatbots support Self-Regulated Learning
(SRL) across all phases, in online asynchronous higher education. However, due to the potential
variability in the availability of studies, the criteria were applied with some flexibility during the
screening, particularly when assessing titles and abstracts.
Table 3.1: Inclusion and Exclusion Criteria
Type
Criteria
Inclusion
Addressed more than one phase of SRL.
Addressed only one phase of SRL, if the study provided relevant insights or specific contributions to the topic.
Were conducted in a higher education environment.
Focused on the use of LLM-based chatbots.
Were written in English or Portuguese.
Were peer-reviewed.
Followed other SRL models for defining phases of self-regulation, if relevant to the study.
A study did not need to explicitly specify which SRL phases it addressed, as long as it was relevant to SRL or the implemen-
tation of LLM-based chatbots.
Exclusion
Addressed only one phase of SRL, unless the study was deemed relevant for inclusion during the initial screening process.
Used chatbots that were rule-based rather than LLM-based.
Did not mention chatbots, LLMs, or SRL in their scope.
Did not involve the use of chatbots or LLMs.
Were categorized as gray literature (e.g., non-peer-reviewed reports, preprints).
Were written in languages other than English or Portuguese.
Were not conducted in higher education environments.
Did not focus on SRL as a central concept.
Were inaccessible (e.g., due to paywalls or unavailability).

## Página PDF 54

Systematic Literature Review
40
3.6.1
Rationale for Criteria Flexibility
Given the scope of this systematic review, LLM-based chatbots that provide comprehensive sup-
port for SRL across all phases in asynchronous online higher education, it was necessary to antic-
ipate the possibility of limited search results. Therefore, studies addressing only one SRL phase
or lacking explicit mentions of supported phases were not automatically excluded. These studies
were assessed on a case-by-case basis during the screening process. By including studies with
partial or ambiguous coverage of SRL phases, this review aimed to capture as broad a perspective
as possible while remaining focused on its research objectives. Also, studies conducted in higher
education settings were included even if they did not explicitly focus on asynchronous online
environments to obtain more results.
This flexible approach allowed for the inclusion of studies that might contribute indirectly to
understanding comprehensive SRL support, particularly when the phases of SRL were not clearly
delineated or when studies supported two or three phases without explicitly stating so. Balanc-
ing these considerations ensured a robust and representative dataset for the analysis, even in the
presence of variability in study focus and reporting.
3.7
Screening Process and Results
3.7.1
Screening
The screening process was conducted in multiple stages to ensure the selection of relevant studies
for this systematic review. Initially, a total of 108 studies were retrieved from the Scopus database
based on the predefined search string. These studies underwent a rigorous selection process fol-
lowing the inclusion and exclusion criteria outlined in Table 3.1 with the process being described
in Figure 3.1.
In the first phase, the title and abstract of each study were reviewed to assess its relevance.
Studies were excluded if they clearly did not meet the inclusion criteria or explicitly matched any
of the exclusion criteria. This step resulted in the acceptance of 44 studies for further evalua-
tion, while 64 studies were excluded, the majority not involving the use of chatbots or LLMs, as
specified in the exclusion criteria.
The second phase involved verifying the accessibility of the selected studies and conduct-
ing a more in-depth reading to extract key information. The primary objective was to determine
whether the chatbot utilized in the study was LLM-based rather than rule-based and to identify
which phases of Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) were supported. This information was primarily
extracted from the methodology, discussion, and conclusion sections of each study. Following
this evaluation, 28 additional studies were excluded due to inaccessibility, absence of LLM usage
or insufficient focus on SRL, resulting in a final set of 16 studies, presented in Table 3.2, that
underwent full analysis.

3.7.2
Results and Categorization
In the final stage, the 16 selected studies were comprehensively analyzed and categorized based on
the number of SRL phases they supported. Studies were grouped into three categories: those ad-
dressing only one SRL phase, those supporting two phases, and those providing a comprehensive
SRL approach by covering all three phases (forethought, performance, and self-reflection). This
classification led to the final selection of studies from which relevant information was extracted to
answer the research questions. Although some studies did not explicitly mention the SRL phases,
their presence could be inferred through the underlying processes associated with each phase, as
detailed in Section 2.1.5.1. Figure 3.2 provides a visual summary of the SRL phase coverage
observed across 16 all studies.
As shown in the figure, the Performance phase was the most frequently supported, appearing
in all studies. The Forethought and Self-Reflection phases were less consistently addressed, with

Systematic Literature Review
42
Figure 3.2: SRL Phases Supported Across Studies
only 12 studies each including support for these phases. This suggests a current trend in the
literature toward emphasizing immediate learning activities over the preparatory and reflective
dimensions of SRL.
Additionally, the studies can be categorized by the number of SRL phases they supported:
• One SRL Phase addressed: 2 studies (A3, A7).
• Two SRL Phases addressed: 4 studies (A4, A6, A5, A13).
• Three SRL Phases addressed: 10 studies (A1-A2, A8-A12, A14-A16).
This systematic screening and categorization process ensured that the final selection of stud-
ies aligned with the research objectives. The resulting categorization supports the identification
of comprehensive implementations and highlights key findings that contribute to the discussion
on chatbot capabilities, SRL phase coverage, and the effectiveness of different implementation
approaches.
3.8
Discussion
This section presents and discusses the findings of the systematic literature review by addressing
each research question in light of the extracted data. Unlike the broader state of the art presented
earlier, this targeted review focuses specifically on empirical articles that describe concrete imple-
mentations of LLM-based chatbots in educational contexts. As such, it complements the previous
literature overview by providing grounded, practice-oriented insights into how these systems are
being designed and evaluated in real-world settings.

[Vista original de la página 56](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-56.png)

## Página PDF 57

3.8 Discussion
43
3.8.1
Characteristics of LLM-based Chatbots Designed to Support SRL
The reviewed studies found indicate that LLM-based chatbots for self-regulated learning (SRL)
are all cloud-based and rely on OpenAI’s GPT models, particularly GPT-3.5 and GPT-4.0, as can
be observed in Figure 3.3. [36, 64].
Figure 3.3: ChatGPT Model Distribution
These chatbots are mostly integrated into Learning Management Systems (LMS) interfaces,
such as Moodle or other online platforms, enabling seamless interaction with the platform’s func-
tionalities, as in [100, 26]. In addition to API integration, they are also sometimes directly accessed
through OpenAI’s website [85]. The integration with Moodle enhances user comfort by providing
a familiar interface, ensuring a more cohesive learning experience [64].
Some studies implemented Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) or similar techniques to
enhance chatbot responses with external knowledge, as shown in Figure 3.4 [64, 47, 36].
3.8.2
Techniques Used by LLM-based Chatbots in SRL
Several techniques are employed to improve chatbot effectiveness in Self-Regulated Learning
(SRL) contexts. Chatbots often incorporate structured scaffolding techniques, such as Mandala
Charts for organizing information for students [36] or guided learning logs to assist with learner’s
goal-setting and reflection [45]. In more sophisticated implementations, architectures that incor-
porate tools like LangChain, LlamaIndex, and vector databases such as Weaviate allow to enhance
the chatbot’s contextual understanding and data retrieval capabilities, to improve its effectiveness
in SRL support [64].
[64]’s MoodleBot provides insights into how to employs a robust architecture combinating
OpenAI’s pretrained models, LangChain agents, LlamaIndex, and Weaviate for seamless integra-
tion and functionality. The system sources its data from various formats, including lecture notes,

[Vista original de la página 57](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-57.png)

![Página PDF 57, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-57.png)


<!-- PDF page 58 -->

## Página PDF 58

Systematic Literature Review
44
Figure 3.4: RAG Implementation
slides, and exercise sheets. The raw data undergoes vectorization for efficient querying. Moodle-
Bot integrates directly with Moodle’s native chat interface and forums, ensuring users experience a
consistent environment. The LangChain agent leverages modular tools like a Question Generator
and Answer Generator to provide dynamic support. The Question Generator formulates relevant
questions from the content, while the Answer Generator taps into the vectorized data stored to
provide precise answers. This system, underpinned by ChatGPT, enables a more interactive and
responsive learning experience for students. Additionally, a MongoDB instance is used to track
costs and chat histories, maintaining transparency and allowing for future optimizations. These
advanced technologies combined ensure that MoodleBot effectively supports the self-regulated
learning process by providing personalized, on-demand assistance.
Other simpler implementations focus on direct chatbot integration into messaging platforms,
such as QQ or Discord, to provide real-time feedback [47, 36].
3.8.3
Evaluation Techniques of Current Solutions
To assess the effectiveness of LLM-driven solutions in supporting self-regulated learning, the re-
viewed studies employed a diverse range of quantitative and qualitative methods. The most com-
monly used quantitative approaches included pre and post-tests, standardized questionnaires, and
statistical analyses to measure learning gains and user perceptions. For instance, studies evalu-
ating chatbot-based interventions frequently utilized pre- and post-tests to assess improvements
in self-efficacy, self-regulation, and knowledge acquisition [36, 47, 45]. Similarly, validated SRL
measurement instruments such as the Self-Regulated Learning Perception Scale and the Online
Self-Regulated Learning Questionnaire (OSLQ) were administered to collect self-reported data
on students’ self-regulation skills and confidence in online learning [100, 22].

[Vista original de la página 58](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-58.png)

![Página PDF 58, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-58.png)


<!-- PDF page 59 -->

## Página PDF 59

3.8 Discussion
45
Questionnaires played a significant role in gathering user feedback and evaluating the effec-
tiveness of chatbots in personalized support and feedback generation. Studies investigating Chat-
GPT’s role in feedback and reflection processes leveraged structured surveys, such as those based
on the extended Technology Acceptance Model (TAM), to analyze factors influencing adoption,
usefulness, and learning outcomes [22, 91]. Additionally, self-regulated learning skills question-
naires, such as Barnard’s (2009) instrument measuring goal setting, environmental structuring,
time management, and self-evaluation, were utilized to assess chatbot effectiveness in facilitating
SRL behaviors [43].
Beyond self-reported data, learning analytics and observational methods were employed to
capture behavioral interactions between students and AI systems. Several studies collected trace
data in the form of navigation logs to analyze students’ engagement with chatbots [15]. Conver-
sational data, including text-based interactions between learners and AI, were examined to assess
the depth of engagement, feedback quality, and level of interactivity [85, 64].
Qualitative methods were also widely employed to gain deeper insights into students’ expe-
riences and the pedagogical impact of AI-based SRL interventions. Semi-structured interviews
were conducted to explore learners’ perceptions, challenges, and reflections on using AI chatbots
in education [36, 26]. Some studies required participants to maintain reflective learning journals
to document their experiences, which were later analyzed for recurring themes in self-regulation,
goal-setting strategies, and help-seeking behaviors [91, 15].
To ensure statistical rigor, studies employed ANCOVA, ANOVA, and Tukey’s HSD tests to
compare experimental conditions and assess whether the AI-driven SRL tools significantly im-
pacted students’ learning outcomes [26, 47]. Longitudinal studies collected weekly self-reported
motivation scores and chatbot conversation transcripts to track progressive changes in motivation
and engagement over time [49]. Moreover, automated fact-checking and manual instructor valida-
tion were used to evaluate the accuracy and reliability of chatbot-generated responses in academic
settings [64].
Overall, the combination of self-reported surveys, learning analytics, observational techniques,
and experimental designs provided a comprehensive evaluation of AI-driven SRL solutions. These
varied methodologies ensured a robust assessment of student engagement, learning effectiveness,
and the pedagogical value of AI-based tools in fostering self-regulated learning.
To evaluate my solution, adopting a combination of these established methodologies would
ensure a comprehensive assessment. Quantitative measures such as pre and post-tests, SRL ques-
tionnaires, or learning analytics will be utilized to evaluate learning gains, engagement, and self-
regulation development. Additionally, qualitative approaches, including user interviews or reflec-
tive journaling, would provide deeper insights into students’ experiences and perceptions. By
integrating both objective performance metrics and subjective feedback, the evaluation is able to
ensure a rigorous and multidimensional analysis of the effectiveness of the proposed intervention.

[Vista original de la página 59](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-59.png)

![Página PDF 59, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-59.png)


<!-- PDF page 60 -->

## Página PDF 60

Systematic Literature Review
46
3.8.4
Utilization of LLM-based Chatbots Across SRL Phases
The studies highlight varying approaches to supporting the three SRL phases, mentioned in Sec-
tion 2.1.5.1, comprehensively:
• Forethought: Chatbots assist students in goal setting, strategic planning, and motivation
regulation, which are crucial for initiating self-regulated learning. Several studies highlight
chatbot-supported goal setting through structured frameworks such as the Mandala Chart
method [36], explicit prompting strategies [13], and planning aids that help students or-
ganize study sessions [22]. Additionally, chatbots contribute to self-efficacy and outcome
expectations by guiding students in diagnosing knowledge gaps and structuring personal-
ized learning paths [15]. Some chatbots further promote metacognitive strategy activation,
such as reverse prompting, which encourages students to think critically about their learn-
ing processes [8, 22]. These interventions align with key forethought processes, enabling
students to approach learning tasks with a structured and motivated mindset.
• Performance: In the learning phase, chatbots play a key role in self-control and self-
observation, supporting learners in monitoring their progress and adjusting strategies dy-
namically. Studies demonstrate that chatbots provide real-time feedback and clarification,
helping students maintain focus and refine their understanding through interactive discus-
sions [64, 26, 15]. Other implementations encourage self-monitoring by prompting learners
to track their study habits and reflect on their learning progress, reinforcing cognitive en-
gagement [100, 85]. Some chatbots integrate task strategies, such as generating quizzes or
scaffolded discussions, to sustain engagement and reinforce knowledge retention [47]. Ad-
ditionally, AI learning companions enhance attention regulation by structuring interactions
with visual and textual aids to prevent cognitive overload [36].
• Self-Reflection: In the final phase, chatbots facilitate self-evaluation, causal attribution,
and adaptive responses, which help students refine their learning strategies. Chatbots sup-
port self-assessment through reflective questioning, allowing students to analyze their un-
derstanding and make necessary adjustments [13]. Some studies explore journaling and
progress tracking, where chatbots prompt learners to document their learning experiences
and compare feedback over time to identify trends in their academic development [85, 15].
Additionally, chatbots contribute to causal attribution by encouraging learners to reflect on
their mistakes, promoting adaptive learning strategies rather than defensive responses [47].
These interventions align with the self-reflection phase by fostering self-satisfaction and
metacognitive awareness, enabling learners to regulate their learning more effectively over
time.
While the studies comprehensively address various aspects of the Self-Regulated Learning
phases, certain processes described in Section 2.1.5.1 within the SRL cycle appear to be less
emphasized.

[Vista original de la página 60](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-60.png)

![Página PDF 60, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-60.png)


<!-- PDF page 61 -->

## Página PDF 61

3.8 Discussion
47
In the forethought phase, processes related to motivation could be further explored. Although
some chatbots assist in goal setting and strategic planning, there is less focus on how these tools
help students maintain intrinsic motivation and manage emotional states related to learning tasks.
Additionally, the aspects of task interest/value and goal orientation are not extensively covered.
Understanding how chatbots can help students align tasks with their personal goals and interests
is crucial for sustaining long-term engagement and resilience in learning.
In the performance phase, the self-control process, particularly in managing distractions and
maintaining focus over extended periods, is less discussed. While chatbots provide real-time feed-
back and support self-monitoring, there is a gap in addressing how students can develop strategies
to avoid procrastination and stay on task, especially in unstructured learning environments. Fur-
thermore, help-seeking behavior and interest enhancement are areas that could benefit from more
attention. Effective help-seeking is essential for students to overcome challenges independently,
and enhancing interest in the learning material is vital for maintaining engagement throughout the
learning process.
In the self-reflection phase, the causal attribution process is notably underrepresented. Al-
though some chatbots encourage learners to reflect on their mistakes, there is limited exploration
of how students attribute their successes and failures to specific factors, such as effort, ability,
or external circumstances. This attribution is essential for developing adaptive responses and re-
silience. Additionally, the adaptive inferences aspect, where learners adjust their future learning
strategies based on past performance, is not extensively covered. While some chatbots support
self-assessment and reflection, there is less emphasis on translating these reflections into concrete,
adaptive actions for future learning tasks. Moreover, managing self-satisfaction/affect is important
for helping students cope with emotional responses to feedback, which can impact their motivation
and persistence in learning.
Addressing these gaps when developing a solution could enhance the overall effectiveness of
the LLM-based chatbot in supporting the complete SRL cycle, ensuring that learners not only
reflect on their performance but also develop robust strategies for continuous improvement. By
focusing on these underrepresented processes, future development can create more holistic and
effective educational tools that support students throughout the entire self-regulated learning.
3.8.5
Effective Design Principles and Processes of LLM-based Chatbots for SRL
Based on the analysis of the reviewed studies, a set of recurring design principles was identified
that appear to enhance the effectiveness and adoption of chatbots in educational contexts. These
principles do not originate from a single source but rather represent a synthesis derived from
common patterns observed across multiple implementations.
One of the most crucial aspects is the integration with Learning Management Systems (LMS).
Embedding chatbots within existing educational platforms, such as Moodle, significantly enhances
usability by providing a seamless and familiar interface for students. This integration not only
facilitates access but also encourages regular interaction, thereby promoting continuous learning
and engagement [100].

[Vista original de la página 61](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-61.png)

![Página PDF 61, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-61.png)


<!-- PDF page 62 -->

## Página PDF 62

Systematic Literature Review
48
Another vital principle is scaffolding and feedback personalization. Effective chatbots incor-
porate structured support tools, such as Mandala Charts or guided interactions, to assist learners
throughout the Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) phases. These tools provide visual guidance and
interactive elements that enhance engagement and clarity, making the learning process more intu-
itive and accessible [36, 64]. Personalizing feedback to cater to individual learning needs further
amplifies the effectiveness of chatbot implementations. By tailoring responses to the specific
requirements and progress of each student, chatbots can offer more meaningful and impactful
support, fostering a deeper understanding of the material [93].
Task-specific prompts are also essential in optimizing chatbot interactions. Tailoring conver-
sations to particular tasks ensures more relevant and accurate responses. For example, instructing
chatbots to converse with learners in a friendly and constructive manner can enhance their effec-
tiveness [49]. This approach ensures that the chatbot’s responses are contextually appropriate and
aligned with the learning objectives, thereby improving the overall learning experience [15, 62].
Additionally, chatbots that engage students in structured dialogue, rather than providing direct
answers, promote deeper learning and critical thinking. This method encourages active learning by
requiring students to think through problems and formulate their own solutions, thereby enhancing
their problem-solving skills and comprehension [45, 43].
3.8.6
Challenges and Limitations of LLM-based Chatbots for SRL
Despite their potential, LLM-based chatbots face notable challenges that need to be addressed for
more effective implementation in educational settings. One significant issue is the over-reliance on
AI, which can lead to "metacognitive laziness". This phenomenon occurs when students become
too dependent on chatbots for answers, reducing their engagement in active learning and critical
thinking processes [26].
Another challenge is the generation of inaccuracies and hallucinations. Many chatbots struggle
with producing factually incorrect or overly verbose responses, which can mislead students and
hinder the learning process. To mitigate this, better validation mechanisms are necessary to ensure
the accuracy and relevance of the information provided [85, 91].
Moreover, while chatbots are effective in providing immediate feedback, they often fall short
in encouraging deeper metacognitive reflection. This limitation can result in superficial engage-
ment with the material, as students may not be prompted to think critically about their learning
processes. To address this, chatbots should be designed to foster sustained engagement and deeper
reflection, possibly through more sophisticated questioning techniques and reflective prompts
[15, 8].
Lastly, scalability and institutional integration remain significant hurdles. Many chatbot im-
plementations are tailored to specific academic settings, which limits their broader applicability.
For chatbots to be widely adopted, they must be adaptable to various educational contexts and
integrated seamlessly with existing institutional systems [36, 62].

[Vista original de la página 62](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-62.png)

![Página PDF 62, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-62.png)


<!-- PDF page 63 -->

## Página PDF 63

3.8 Discussion
49
3.8.7
Conclusion
This systematic review has provided valuable insights into the current implementations of LLM-
based chatbots supporting Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) in asynchronous online higher edu-
cation across all phases of Zimmerman’s cycle. The technological landscape is dominated by
OpenAI’s GPT models, particularly GPT-3.5 and GPT-4.0, with most implementations being
cloud-based and integrated into Learning Management Systems. Implementations incorporat-
ing Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) demonstrate the potential for enhanced contextual
understanding and personalized support across all SRL phases.
The review highlights that successful chatbot implementations share common design prin-
ciples, and that most implementations found can support forethought, performance and self-
reflection, comprehensively. This provides crucial guidance for developing comprehensive support
across the entire SRL cycle. Through this systematic analysis of current approaches, successful
implementations, and persistent challenges, this review has established a solid foundation for the
development of an effective LLM-based chatbot that can support all phases of self-regulated learn-
ing.
Based on these findings, my solution aims to address specific gaps in current implementations
and follow the identified design principles. It will leverage RAG to enhance the chatbot’s contex-
tual understanding and personalized support, integrating it into a Learning Management System
to provide a seamless and familiar interface for students.
My focus will be on developing features that encourage software engineering students to en-
gage in deep reflection on their learning strategies, particularly in diagnosing knowledge gaps
and structuring personalized learning paths. By using sophisticated prompts designed to stimu-
late metacognitive thinking, the chatbot will guide students in analyzing their understanding and
making necessary adjustments to their learning approaches. This focus on metacognition will help
students develop a deeper awareness of their learning processes, fostering adaptive learning strate-
gies and resilience. To tackle the gaps identified, it will be addressed how the chatbot can help
students maintain intrinsic motivation and manage emotional states related to learning tasks. This
will involve creating features that align tasks with personal goals and interests, thereby sustaining
long-term engagement and resilience. Self-control mechanisms should also be incorporated to
help students manage distractions and maintain focus over extended periods, particularly in the
demanding context of software engineering education.
By focusing on these areas, this solution aims to provide a specialized and effective educational
tool that supports software engineering students throughout the entire self-regulated learning cy-
cle. This systematic review directly answers the dissertation’s objectives by identifying proven
technological approaches and architectures for chatbot implementation, revealing successful de-
sign principles that can be incorporated into the proposed solution, and highlighting potential
challenges that need to be addressed in the development process.
These insights will guide the design and implementation of a chatbot that effectively promotes
self-regulated learning across all phases of the cycle, with an emphasis on enhancing metacog-

## Página PDF 65

Chapter 4
Methodology
4.1
Introduction
This chapter describes the methodology employed in this research, following the Design Science
Research Methodology (DSRM) of [73]. DSRM provides a structured, iterative approach to solv-
ing complex problems through the creation and evaluation of artifacts. In this project, the artifact
is not merely an application but a conceptual and methodological approach, a design for an LLM-
based chatbot aimed at supporting self-regulated learning (SRL) in asynchronous online higher
education. The iterative nature of DSRM ensures that the artifact evolves through continuous
evaluation and refinement until it meets the identified objectives or the project’s time constraints.
The methodology began with a problem-centered initiation, where the problem and motivation
are identified, creating the foundation for subsequent processes: defining objectives, design and
development, demonstration, evaluation and communication. This is an iterative process, where
each step is revisited as needed, allowing for improvements informed by intermediate results.
This chapter outlines the steps taken thus far and the plans for their completion following the
Design Science Research Methodology.
Figure 4.1: DSRM Process [73]
51

## Página PDF 66

Methodology
52
4.2
Problem Identification and Motivation
The research began with a problem-centered initiation, recognizing the challenges faced by stu-
dents and educators in asynchronous online learning environments. While self-regulated learning
is essential for student success, particularly in these settings, current technological solutions of-
ten fail to provide comprehensive, adaptive, and timely support throughout the entire SRL cycle.
Existing chatbot implementations tend to focus on isolated aspects of SRL without ensuring a co-
hesive and continuous learning experience across all three phases of Zimmerman’s: forethought,
performance, and self-reflection phases.
A review of the literature revealed that SRL is most effective when supported as a cyclical
process, where students continuously plan, monitor, and evaluate their learning. However, current
chatbot-based interventions do not sufficiently integrate these phases. This fragmentation prevents
students from receiving context-aware, personalized feedback that adapts to their evolving learning
needs, ultimately reducing the effectiveness of SRL support.
This study addresses this research gap by investigating how an LLM-based chatbot can pro-
vide dynamic, personalized, and contextually relevant support across all phases of SRL in asyn-
chronous online higher education. Unlike existing solutions that focus on individual SRL phases,
this research explores an integrated approach, aiming to develop an adaptive system that actively
facilitates and adjusts to students’ self-regulation needs throughout their learning journey.
To guide this investigation, research questions were formulated based on the problem context,
leading to the exploration of a technological solution capable of supporting all phases of Zimmer-
man’s SRL model. The motivation behind this project is grounded in the potential of LLM-based
chatbots to enhance the educational experience by providing dynamic, meaningful interactions
tailored to the needs of software engineering students in an asynchronous learning environment.
The initial stages of this research, including problem and motivation identification and defining
the problem importance and definition, have been largely completed. The ongoing investigation
into LLM-based chatbots that comprehensively support SRL will be finalized by the end of Jan-
uary, setting the stage for the subsequent phases of the methodology.
4.3
Objectives of a Solution Definition
The objectives of this research are both conceptual and methodological, aiming to address the
identified problem beyond mere technical specifications. This study seeks to design a structured
approach that enables an LLM-based chatbot to provide continuous and adaptive support across
all phases of Self-Regulated Learning, ensuring coherence between forethought, performance, and
self-reflection. It further aims to develop a chatbot capable of integrating external data from Moo-
dle, allowing it to deliver personalized and context-aware feedback tailored to students’ evolving
self-regulation needs.
To support these objectives, three overarching Research Questions (RQs) were formulated and
introduced in Chapter 1, Section 1.4, guiding the design and evaluation of the proposed solution.

4.4 Design and Development
53
These RQs are addressed in detail in Chapter 8, where the results of a comprehensive suite of
functional tests provide evidence regarding the chatbot’s context-awareness, alignment with Zim-
merman’s SRL subprocesses, and its potential to support students’ reflection on metacognitive
challenges. While no real students were involved in this iteration, the evaluation design simu-
lates realistic scenarios to assess how promising the chatbot’s responses are in this regard. This
limitation is acknowledged in Chapter 9 and proposed as future work.
Additionally, the foundation for this solution was informed by a preliminary scoping and sys-
tematic review, detailed in Chapters 2 and 3. These chapters were guided by five Guiding Research
Questions (GRQs) defined in Section 2.3.1, which shaped the thematic structure of the literature
analysis and helped identify key gaps and opportunities. The insights derived from this process
directly influenced the development priorities and design decisions for the artifact, ensuring its
relevance and grounding in current research.
Beyond development, this research also focuses on evaluating the effectiveness of the chatbot
in fostering self-regulated learning through empirical testing, assessing its usability, relevance,
and impact using both quantitative and qualitative criteria. It also aims to contribute to the broader
understanding of LLM-based educational technologies in asynchronous learning environments by
identifying best practices, limitations, and future research directions.
These objectives provide a guiding framework for the design and development phases, en-
suring that the proposed solution remains aligned with the overarching goal of enhancing self-
regulated learning in asynchronous higher education.
4.4
Design and Development
The design and development phases in the DSRM process are closely intertwined. As the artifact
is developed, the insights gained from this process continuously inform and refine the design. This
iterative approach ensures that the solution evolves to become more robust and effective.
The envisioned chatbot was an generative LLM-driven local conversational agent that utilizes
a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) architecture. The design involves integrating domain-
specific data from Moodle to enhance the relevance and context of interactions. The prompt of
the chatbot needs to be carefully designed to align with SRL processes, guiding students through
learning activities. Iterative prototyping plays a crucial role in refining the chatbot’s functionalities
based on practical insights gained during development.
4.5
Demonstration
This research was developed in the context of software engineering education, targeting asyn-
chronous online courses offered through the UAb Moodle platform. The intended audience in-
cludes students enrolled in the "Software Development Laboratory" course, who are predomi-
nantly working professionals and benefit from flexible, self-paced learning environments.

## Página PDF 68

Methodology
54
While the initial research plan included the possibility of involving real students in the demon-
stration phase, this was not carried out due to time constraints. Instead, the chatbot was tested in
a controlled environment through a local deployment of Moodle, populated with authentic course
contents from a previous iteration of the LDS course and realistic interaction scenarios drawn
from expert analysis. This setup enabled evaluation of the chatbot’s functionality, retrieval accu-
racy, and alignment with Zimmerman’s model of self-regulated learning, without requiring live
participants.
The current implementation replicates the technical and pedagogical context of the LDS course
and was developed with ease of deployment in mind. The system is fully prepared for future
integration into a real Moodle instance, where it can be activated with minimal configuration to
support and evaluate real student interactions after metacognitive activities or forum contributions.
This approach, ensures that the demonstration is both methodologically sound and practically
grounded, paving the way for future validation in authentic educational settings.
4.6
Evaluation and Communication
Evaluation is a fundamental component of the iterative DSRM process. Each cycle of development
is followed by testing, extracting conclusions, and refining the design to address any identified is-
sues. This iterative cycle continues until significant improvements are achieved or time constraints
are reached.
In this project, the evaluation focused on validating the chatbot’s ability to support self-
regulated learning (SRL) through a structured set of formal tests based on [106]’s SRL model.
Rather than relying on user studies or surveys, the artifact was evaluated using a comprehen-
sive suite of functional tests that simulate realistic student interactions. Each test was carefully
designed to represent a specific SRL subprocess across the forethought, performance, and self-
reflection phases. These tests verified whether the chatbot could accurately identify the user’s
intention, retrieve relevant context from the course data, and respond in a way that aligned with
the targeted subprocess. Additional evaluation dimensions included query refinement behavior,
context prediction accuracy, and prompt adherence.
At least two evaluation cycles were planned to ensure the artifact undergoes rigorous test-
ing and iterative refinement. Results from each cycle informed subsequent development phases,
enabling continuous alignment with research goals and user needs.
Communication occurred through two primary channels. First, this dissertation will serve as
a comprehensive documentation of the project, detailing the methods employed, design iterations,
evaluation results, and the rationale behind key decisions. This systematic approach to documen-
tation ensures that the research process and outcomes are accessible and transparent for future
studies and applications.
Second, a scientific article based on the findings was prepared and submitted to a peer-reviewed
journal or conference. This article focused on the methodology and results of this project, aiming
to contribute to the academic literature and foster discussions on the use of LLM-based chatbots in

4.8
Conclusion
This chapter outlined the methodology applied based on the DSRM framework, highlighting its
iterative nature and the alignment of its phases with the research objectives. The problem iden-
tification and motivation phase are near completion, with ongoing research to be finalized by the
end of January. Subsequent phases, including design, development, and evaluation, will build on
this foundation to produce a well-conceived artifact, aimed at addressing the identified challenges
through iterative refinement and evaluation.
The potential value of this project lies in its contribution to both academic literature and edu-
cational practice. By addressing a critical gap in the provision of personalized feedback in asyn-
chronous online learning environments, this research aims to enhance self-regulated learning for
software engineering students. Furthermore, the proposed approach and methodology can serve as
a model for similar contexts, offering scalability and adaptability. Future iterations of the artifact
will refine its capabilities and extend its applicability, guided by the insights and findings obtained
throughout this project.
