## Página PDF 71

Chapter 5
Development Process
This chapter describes the research and development process followed in creating the proposed
solution. It outlines the theoretical and practical considerations in selecting and implementing
the underlying technologies, including the choice between running models locally or using cloud-
based solutions while addressing the principles outlined in Regulation 2016/679 of the European
Parliament, commonly known as the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) concerns [38].
The iterative development process is documented, detailing how different models with different
parameter sizes were evaluated, distinguishing between model quality and reasoning capabili-
ties. Additionally, the impact of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is analyzed to determine
whether observed limitations stem from the model itself or the retrieval process. All testing is
conducted in Portuguese, reflecting the language used by students, with parallel tests in English
to identify potential language-related performance discrepancies. UML diagrams illustrate the
system’s architecture and workflows, and a local Moodle instance is leveraged for testing.
5.1
Requirement Elicitation and Objectives
5.1.1
Objectives
The development of the chatbot is guided by specific requirements derived from both theoretical
foundations and insights from the State of the Art Chapters 2 and 3. The primary objective is
to create a chatbot that interacts synchronously with students through a chat interface embedded
into Moodle. This chatbot is designed to support all three phases of Zimmerman’s self-regulated
learning cycle - forethought, performance, and self-reflection - by providing tailored guidance that
fosters self-regulation and ultimately enhances students’ academic performance.
Given the asynchronous nature of the LDS learning environment, where students do not have
direct contact with instructors, the chatbot must function as an adaptive learning assistant. To
ensure meaningful support, it must rely on Moodle’s course content and students’ activity data,
allowing it to generate responses that are contextually relevant and aligned with course objectives
rather than generic or disconnected from the learning process. Additionally, the chatbot must
be capable of addressing key self-regulated learning processes within each phase of Zimmerman’s
57

[Vista original de la página 71](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-71.png)

![Página PDF 71, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-71.png)


<!-- PDF page 72 -->

## Página PDF 72

Development Process
58
cycle. While supporting all processes would be ideal, it is essential to evaluate the potential impact
of each and determine which are most critical for improving student self-regulation in this context.
5.1.2
Requirements
The definition of these requirements was based on multiple elicitation techniques to ensure align-
ment with the needs of self-regulated learners in asynchronous online software engineering edu-
cation. The decision-making process was guided by introspection, involving a reflective analysis
of the context, as well as the constraints and expectations of SRL support in this learning environ-
ment. Expert interviews provided valuable insights into self-regulated e-learning, drawing on the
expertise of Prof. Leonel Morgado as an e-learning specialist and Prof. Daniela as an SCRL ex-
pert. Additionally, a case analysis was conducted by examining a previous year’s Moodle instance
of the Laboratório de Desenvolvimento de Software course, focusing on its content, structure, and
existing SRL support through metacognitive challenges. Prototyping further contributed to the
process, as early implementations tested various chatbot integrations to assess the feasibility, us-
ability, and effectiveness of different technologies. Finally, an analysis of existing solutions in
the state of the art further informed the definition of these requirements, revealing multiple design
alternatives that influenced key development choices.
These elicitation techniques led to several critical design decisions. One key choice was be-
tween implementing a synchronous or asynchronous chatbot. Asynchronous chatbots require less
computational power and allow delayed responses, making them suitable for hardware-constrained
environments. However, the advantages of synchronous interaction, particularly in fostering en-
gagement and providing immediate feedback, were deemed more beneficial for self-regulated
learning, ultimately justifying the decision to adopt a synchronous approach that allows students
to interact with the chatbot before, during and after the execution of Moodle activities.
Another decision was whether to integrate the chatbot into Moodle or have it function as a
standalone application. Embedding it within Moodle ensures seamless access for students and
allows the system to leverage course data effectively. This integration can be achieved through an
HTML inline frame (iFrame), which enables the chatbot to be placed anywhere within Moodle,
such as in a Moodle Block or any other section of the platform. Additionally, since the chatbot re-
mains as a standalone component, it can also be accessed independently outside Moodle, allowing
for broader adaptability across different web platforms. This approach ensures a cohesive learning
experience while maintaining flexibility in deployment.
Furthermore, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) was chosen over relying solely on di-
rect model responses to improve accuracy and relevance. By grounding responses in course ma-
terials and student interactions, RAG enhances the chatbot’s ability to provide meaningful sup-
port. Additionally, the choice between running the underlying language model locally or using an
online service was carefully considered. While the literature examined in the state of the art re-
lied on cloud-based models, recent advancements have made local deployment reliable. Running
the model locally offers advantages in GDPR compliance, as it prevents student data from being

transmitted to external servers, improving privacy and security while ensuring functionality even
in environments with restricted internet access.

Each requirement is assigned a unique identifier (e.g., F3 for functional requirement number 3 and
NF5 for non-functional requirement number 5) to facilitate reference and traceability throughout
the development and evaluation process.

5.2
Local Model Deployment Options
In the development of the chatbot, the ability to deploy Large Language Models (LLMs) locally
was a critical requirement. Running models on-premises ensures compliance with data protection
regulations such as the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) while also reducing reliance
on external services or costs. Two major options were considered for this purpose: Ollama and LM
Studio. The selection process focused on their suitability for integration into Moodle, their capac-
ity to support Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), and their ability to incorporate advanced
prompt engineering techniques to provide meaningful and context-aware interactions. Further-
more, the chatbot should be capable of supporting multiple agents with different specialized roles
if necessary, making flexibility in model customization an essential factor in the decision.

5.2.1
LM Studio
LM Studio provides a graphical interface for running LLMs locally and supports multiple oper-
ating systems, including Windows. This broad compatibility makes it an appealing option for
general-purpose use. It simplifies model discovery, downloading, and execution while integrat-
ing with frameworks such as TensorFlow and PyTorch. However, LM Studio is not open-source,
which limits the level of customization possible when adapting it to specific use cases. Addition-
ally, while its graphical interface makes it accessible to users with limited technical expertise, it
introduces a layer of abstraction that may reduce flexibility when deploying models. LM Studio
also lacks built-in features for advanced prompt engineering and multi-agent coordination, which
are essential for dynamically adapting chatbot responses to different phases of the SRL cycle. [51]
5.2.2
Ollama
Ollama, in contrast, is an open-source platform that allows efficient execution of LLMs through
a streamlined command-line interface. Initially designed for only macOS and Linux, Ollama has
since introduced native support for Windows, including GPU acceleration for both NVIDIA and
AMD Radeon hardware. This development makes it a versatile option for deploying models. The
open-source nature of Ollama ensures that it can be customized and extended as needed, making
it more suitable for deep integration with Moodle’s infrastructure [66].
A key advantage of Ollama is its flexibility in model instruction tuning through prompt en-
gineering. Since the chatbot must provide guidance across the forethought, performance, and

## Página PDF 75

5.3 Large Language Models Selection
61
self-reflection phases of SRL, it is necessary to fine-tune its behavior. Ollama allows for the
customization of system prompts, ensuring that the chatbot responds appropriately based on the
context of a student’s learning activities. Additionally, the ability to modify model behavior en-
ables it to better support RAG by ensuring that responses are generated based on relevant Moodle
course content.
Another important consideration was multi-agent support. The chatbot should interact with
students in various capacities, Ollama’s framework allows the use of multiple concurrent models,
which enables the implementation of specialized agents that can handle different types of queries.
This approach enhances the chatbot’s ability to provide adaptive and context-aware feedback, an
helpful feature for fostering self-regulated learning.
5.2.3
Platform Selection
After evaluating both platforms, Ollama was chosen as the foundation for deploying the chat-
bot’s LLM component. The decision was based on its adaptability, open-source framework, and
streamlined deployment model, which align with the project’s objectives. Since the chatbot must
dynamically retrieve and process Moodle course content using RAG, a solution that allows flexible
automation was preferred.
Moreover, the ability to implement advanced prompt engineering techniques and support mul-
tiple specialized agents was a decisive factor. The chatbot must function dynamically across
different phases of SRL, adapting its instructions to provide relevant and meaningful guidance.
Ollama’s design facilitates this level of customization, ensuring that students receive targeted sup-
port tailored to their learning progress. These capabilities reinforce its suitability for enhancing
the self-regulation skills of students in an asynchronous online learning environment.
5.3
Large Language Models Selection
The choice of Large Language Models (LLMs) for this project was guided by the need to balance
model performance with the computational limitations of the available hardware. The system
used for testing consisted of 16GB RAM, an Intel i7-9750H CPU at 2.6GHz, and an NVIDIA
GTX 1650 GPU with 4GB of dedicated memory. Due to the limited VRAM, all models were run
on the CPU, making processor performance the critical factor in determining feasibility.
5.3.1
Model Selection and Testing
The evaluation focused on balancing model size, inference speed, and retrieval performance within
these constraints.
The first model tested was Mistral 7B, which is optimized for multi-core CPU execution.
Official recommendations suggest at least a modern multi-core CPU with six or more cores and
a clock speed above 3.6GHz [20]. The CPU met these specifications, and the model ran without

[Vista original de la página 75](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-75.png)

![Página PDF 75, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-75.png)


<!-- PDF page 76 -->

## Página PDF 76

Development Process
62
issues. However, due to the lower number of parameters, it sometimes failed to provide relevant
answers.
Llama 3.1 8B was also evaluated for its balance of performance and language capability. The
recommended specifications include a CPU with at least eight cores and 16GB of RAM for rea-
sonable inference speed [56]. Since the test machine had only six cores, performance was slightly
slower compared to Mistral 7B. However, response quality was noticeably better, particularly
for complex queries, making it a strong candidate despite the longer processing time. Without
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), responses were nearly instant. However, enabling RAG
introduced a delay of approximately 30 seconds per query, which impacted usability.
One of the last models tested was DeepSeek 14B, a larger model that exceeded the capabilities
of the available hardware. DeepSeek was rejected due to its significantly longer inference time
compared to Mistral and Llama. The model required approximately five minutes to generate
a single response, even without RAG, making it impractical for real-time chatbot interactions.
This demonstrated that 14B models were unsuitable for local execution on the given hardware,
reinforcing the decision to use a smaller, more efficient model with fewer than 8B parameters.
In April 2025, the release of Gemma 3 introduced a highly efficient model that offered sig-
nificant improvements in performance, even on hardware with modest specifications. This break-
through enabled the testing of Gemma 3 12B locally, despite its larger parameter count. Surpris-
ingly, the model ran smoothly without RAG, and delivered the best overall results during evalu-
ation. It demonstrated a markedly better understanding of Portuguese and an improved ability to
follow prompt instructions precisely, outperforming both Mistral 7B and Llama 3.1 8B. These ad-
vantages, combined with its manageable inference time, made Gemma 3 12B the optimal choice
for supporting the chatbot’s requirements in a multilingual, SRL-focused academic environment.
Additionally, the Nomic-embed-text model was also tested using Ollama and chosen to employ
vector-based data ingestion, playing a crucial role in the RAG pipeline. While not a conversational
model, it facilitates the retrieval of relevant documents, ensuring that user queries are answered
with contextually appropriate information.
5.3.2
Hardware Limitations and Future Considerations
The evaluations revealed that while 7B and 8B models can run efficiently on the current hardware,
integrating RAG significantly increases response times. The primary limitation stems from CPU-
bound processing, which impacts token generation speed. To achieve real-time inference with
more advanced models or reduce response times for RAG-enhanced queries, a dedicated server
with high-performance hardware, including a GPU with at least 16GB of VRAM and a more
powerful CPU, would be necessary.
However, the release of Gemma 3 in late April introduced a significant improvement in local
model execution. Despite having 12 billion parameters, Gemma 3 12B was able to run effectively
on the same hardware used in previous tests, delivering superior results, especially in terms of Por-
tuguese language understanding and prompt adherence. This suggests that recent advancements
in model efficiency can partially mitigate hardware constraints, at least for certain tasks.

[Vista original de la página 76](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-76.png)

![Página PDF 76, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-76.png)

## Página PDF 77

5.4 Integration with Moodle
63
Despite these limitations, the system architecture allows for easy substitution of the underlying
model, meaning that upgrading to a more powerful system in the future would enable the use of
even larger models.
5.3.3
Testing with Different Models
Given the constraints of testing with only a single model, an approach involves testing the system
with more powerful LLMs with different numbers of parameters. By testing with models with 7
billion, 8 billion, 12 billion and 14 billion, it is possible to compare performance and accuracy
between different local execution. This comparison provides insights into whether chatbot perfor-
mance is limited by the model’s number of parameters or the RAG architecture implementation.
5.3.4
Model Selection
The final choice for local execution was Llama 3.1 8B, as it provided the best balance between
accuracy, inference time, and feasibility given hardware constraints. Mistral 7B was also a strong
candidate, but the broader knowledge coverage of Llama 3.1 8B made it the preferred choice.
Larger models with 14B parameters were deemed impractical due to excessive inference times,
making them unsuitable for real-time conversations.
However, the late-April release of Gemma 3 introduced a significant shift. Despite its 12B
parameter size, Gemma 3 ran efficiently on the same local setup and delivered the best results
overall. It demonstrated enhanced comprehension of Portuguese and a superior ability to follow
complex prompt instructions, outperforming previously tested models. It had not been used earlier
simply because it was not yet available, but once released, it was immediately adopted as the final
model for the evaluation phase, presented in Chapter 7.
Although the current hardware setup limits performance, the architecture allows for seamless
model substitution in the future, ensuring scalability as hardware improvements become available.
Furthermore, testing with local models with different levels of power provides and additional
benchmark for evaluating the capabilities of the chatbot to run under constrained conditions while
still allowing for allowing for high-performance execution in more powerful environments when
necessary.
5.4
Integration with Moodle
The development and testing of the chatbot prototype were conducted within a controlled Moodle
environment. A local instance of Moodle 4.5 was installed to serve as the development platform.
This instance was configured to replicate the operational environment where the final prototype
would be deployed. To facilitate testing, an older backup of the Software Development Laboratory
course (LDS) was restored. This course serves as the setting for e-SimProgramming activities and
represents the environment where the chatbot integration would ideally take place. The backup
excluded student information to ensure privacy and focus on the structural and functional aspects

Development Process
64
of the course. This setup allowed for comprehensive testing and refinement of the chatbot’s capa-
bilities before its final integration into the real Moodle environment. This process involved several
key steps, each contributing to the seamless integration of the chatbot within the Moodle platform.
5.4.1
Enabling Web Services
To facilitate communication between the chatbot and Moodle, web services were enabled. This
process began by accessing the Moodle site with administrator privileges and within the site ad-
ministration panel, enabling the Web Services option. Additionally, the REST protocol was en-
abled to support API interactions, ensuring compatibility with the chatbot’s requirements and fa-
cilitating secure data exchange.
5.4.2
Creating an External Service
An external service was created to define the set of functions that the chatbot could access. This
service was configured to include essential Moodle functions such as retrieving course content and
student data. The service was named and saved, and the necessary functions were added to it. This
configuration ensured that the chatbot had access to only relevant data for its operations, enabling
it to provide accurate and up-to-date information to students, while keeping a secure access to
Moodle’s database.
5.4.3
Generating an Access Token
An access token was generated to authenticate API requests from the chatbot to Moodle. This
token is created for a specific user with the necessary permissions to access the functions included
in the external service. This token was crucial for securing the communication between the chatbot
and Moodle, ensuring that only authorized requests were processed and maintaining the integrity
of the data exchange.
5.4.4
Retrieving Contents in JSON
The chatbot retrieved course data and other information from Moodle using the Web Services API,
which returned data in JSON format. The API endpoint was configured to use the generated token
for authentication, and HTTP requests were sent with the required parameters to retrieve course
contents, enrolled users activities and forum discussions. The retrieved JSON data was stored in
separated instances according to the content retrieved which facilitated easy access and retrieval
of the structured data.
5.4.5
Integrating the Chatbot Interface
To integrate the chatbot interface into Moodle, a custom Moodle Block inside the course was
added. This text block was modified to include the HTML code that embeds the chatbot interface
using an iFrame, resized to fit the block space appropriately. The block was added added to the

## Página PDF 80

Chapter 6
Proposed Solution
6.1
Architecture
This section presents the system architecture, drawing insights from the whitepaper on agents,
[94]. The design follows a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) paradigm, adopting an Agent
with Data Store approach to meet the project’s requirements.
6.1.1
Agent
Before discussing the architecture, it is essential to distinguish between an agent and a model.
A model is a computational entity that learns patterns from data to make predictions or generate
responses. In contrast, an agent is a more comprehensive system that interacts with its environ-
ment, incorporates decision-making processes, and executes actions based on inputs and goals.
While models serve as components within agents, the latter integrate additional logic, contextual
awareness, and tool interfaces to facilitate complex interactions.
[94] categorizes agents based on their capabilities, ranging from basic input-output process-
ing to more sophisticated systems that incorporate memory and external tools. A Simple Agent
operates with limited adaptability, executing predefined tasks without contextual awareness. An
Agent with Tools extends this capability by integrating external functions, enhancing performance
in more complex scenarios. The Agent with Data Store, as exemplified in Figure 6.1, builds upon
this foundation by maintaining and retrieving information dynamically, allowing for informed and
context-aware responses. Given the need for a system that can adapt to user interactions and
efficiently retrieve relevant information, this architecture was deemed the most suitable.
6.1.2
Data Storage and Retrieval
The architecture defined relies on a structured data storage and retrieval process to ensure efficient
access to relevant information. Since course-related data in Moodle is retrieved in JSON format
through the API, this data must be periodically fetched, stored, and embedded for future queries,
in order to keep the knowledge updated. To achieve this, a dual storage system is employed:
66

[Vista original de la página 80](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-80.png)

![Página PDF 80, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-80.png)


<!-- PDF page 81 -->

## Página PDF 81

6.1 Architecture
67
Figure 6.1: 1-to-many relationship between Agents and Data Stores with Data Sources of various
types [94].
• File System is used to store JSON files and relevant PDFs, including chat history, files re-
trieved from Moodle, and theoretical SRL materials. This allows organized and persistent
storage of learning activities, forums and discussions, quizzes (which include the metacog-
nitive challenges), quiz attempts, and prior chatbot interactions. In future iterations, this
local storage approach could be replaced with a more scalable database solution such as
MongoDB using GridFS, which supports efficient storage and retrieval of both structured
JSON and large binary files like PDFs.
• ChromaDB, an open-source vector database, is responsible for storing and querying em-
beddings of the retrieved files. By maintaining a vectorized representation of the course
data, the system enables semantic search and efficient retrieval of contextually relevant doc-
uments.
This combination ensures that Moodle data remains accessible and searchable while optimiz-
ing retrieval performance for chatbot interactions. As new data becomes available in Moodle, it is
periodically retrieved, processed, stored in MongoDB, and embedded in ChromaDB.
6.1.3
Implementation Framework
The system integrates several key technologies to implement this architecture:
• Ollama enables local execution of LLMs, ensuring efficient processing without reliance on
external cloud services [65].
• LangChain provides a structured framework for integrating LLMs with external memory
and tool usage, facilitating a flexible data-store based agent design [44].
• ChromaDB serves as the vector store, managing and querying embeddings for semantic
retrieval [18].
• Streamlit provides an interactive user interface, offering a streamlined environment for
chatbot interactions [83].

[Vista original de la página 81](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-81.png)

![Página PDF 81, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-81.png)


<!-- PDF page 82 -->

## Página PDF 82

Proposed Solution
68
6.1.4
Workflow and Reasoning Strategy
The chatbot was planned to follow a structured workflow to generate informed responses. When a
user submits a query, an embedding model first converts the query into a high-dimensional repre-
sentation. This vector is compared against stored embeddings in ChromaDB to retrieve the most
relevant information. The retrieved content is then combined with the original query and passed
to the LLM for response generation. By dynamically enriching the model prompt with contextual
data and instructional cues, this approach aims to ensure accurate and relevant responses while
maintaining adaptability and efficiency.
The system’s design was inspired by principles from LangChain Agents and the ReAct (Rea-
soning + Acting) framework, which promote reasoning steps interleaved with retrieval operations
to guide response planning. While the implementation did not rely on LangChain’s agent abstrac-
tion directly, the final prototype applies a similar reasoning pattern, prioritizing retrieval-based
context selection, structured prompt composition, and dynamic adaptation based on query seman-
tics.
6.1.5
Architectural Overview
To support learners throughout all phases of the self-regulated learning (SRL) cycle, the system
was designed with a modular, agent-based architecture focused on retrieval-first interactions. This
approach ensures that user queries are grounded in course-specific data and guided by SRL prin-
ciples.
The prototype integrates LangChain for agent coordination, ChromaDB for semantic retrieval,
and Ollama for running local LLMs. Course content, user activity data, and SRL theory materials
are stored and accessed through a structured file system, which proved sufficient for managing
the required resources. While components like MongoDB [57] or LlamaIndex [50] were initially
considered, they were ultimately excluded in favor of a simpler, more lightweight implementation.
Future iterations could explore these alternatives to improve scalability or expand functionality.
The overall architecture, depicted in Figure 6.2, illustrates the interaction between the agent,
data sources, and retrieval mechanisms. This design serves as the foundation for the concrete
implementation described in the following section.
6.1.6
Implemented System Architecture
Following the initial architectural vision presented in the overview, this section details the concrete
implementation of the system through a series of architectural diagrams. These diagrams reflect
the modular composition, data flow, and functional behavior of the final version of the SRL-
supporting chatbot, based on the developed python scripts and their interactions with external
components and APIs.
The first diagram introduces the component-level architecture, identifying each core module
and external system involved, as well as the services they provide and consume. It serves as a

6.2
Prototype: Design Choices and Challenges
This section presents the design decisions and challenges encountered during the development of
the prototype, focusing on the retrieval of Moodle data and the refinement of context selection
strategies. The discussion follows an incremental approach, detailing the initial methodology
for retrieving raw Moodle information and the subsequent improvements aimed at optimizing its
processing for natural language understanding within a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG)
framework. This section also explores different strategies for presenting contextual information to
the model, beginning with an approach that sends the entire context at once, evolving to approaches
with progressively more scalable retrieval methods.
Throughout the development process, the model used for local testing was llama3.1:8B,
as mentioned in Section 5.3.4. At the time, the released gemma3:12B model, later shown to
outperform others in comprehension and prompt alignment, had not yet been made available. As
such, llama3.1:8B was used for the prototyping present in sections below, while gemma3:12B
was only incorporated during the final evaluation phase described in Chapter 7.
6.2.1
Extracting, Structuring, and Preprocessing Moodle Data
The first step in development involved extracting relevant data from Moodle, which is essential
for enabling personalized and context-aware chatbot responses. Initial access to raw Moodle API
data returned extensive but unstructured content, including student interactions, course materials,
forum discussions, and quiz responses. This required preprocessing to convert it into structured
JSON files suitable for embedding and retrieval.
The extraction process used various Moodle API functions: course details were retrieved using
core_course_get_courses_by_field and core_course_get_contents; enrolled stu-
dents via core_enrol_get_enrolled_users; quizzes and their attempts using mod_quiz
_get_quizzes_by_courses, mod_quiz_get_user_attempts, and mod_quiz_get
_attempt_review. Forum metadata was accessed through mod_forum_get_forums
_by_courses.
However, the API lacked a direct method to retrieve entire forum discussions. To overcome
this, a custom process was implemented: for each student, mod_forum_get_discussion_
posts_by_userid retrieved their contributions, and mod_forum_get_discussion_posts
fetched the full thread to preserve discussion hierarchy. This allowed associating forum threads
with specific users, enabling personalized feedback based on individual participation.
Following extraction, data was cleaned and structured to improve retrieval efficiency. Three
key optimizations were applied:
• Metadata reduction – redundant fields like system-generated IDs and timestamps were
removed, reducing JSON file size significantly (e.g., from over 6,000 lines to under 1,000).
• Course structure alignment – materials were grouped by module/section to reflect how
students navigate the course.

6.2 Prototype: Design Choices and Challenges
77
• Student-centric organization – forum activity, quiz responses, and progress tracking were
grouped per student to enable personalized interaction.
Figure 6.10 illustrates this structure, showing how the processed data is divided and stored to
reflect both course organization and individual student activity.
This refined structure was embedded using the nomic-embed-text model and stored in
ChromaDB. The initial embedding of large blocks of course data had resulted in excessive and
irrelevant retrievals, while the new design prioritized chunk-level granularity, ensuring only the
most relevant and concise information was retrieved.
Figure 6.10: Organization of processed Moodle data, categorized per student for each course.
In summary, the extraction and structuring pipeline enabled the system to move from raw,
unstructured Moodle data to a modular, semantically organized format optimized for retrieval.
This foundation was essential for supporting context-aware SRL feedback in later stages of the
chatbot’s pipeline.

## Página PDF 92

Proposed Solution
78
6.2.2
Naive RAG: Unfiltered Context Retrieval
6.2.2.1
Concept
The initial approach to information retrieval involves providing the model with the entire retrieved
context in a single prompt. This method focuses on comprehensive information inclusion, ensuring
that all potentially relevant data is made available to the model during the reasoning process.
The underlying concept is that by providing complete information, the model has access to all
necessary details to formulate accurate and informed responses without the risk of missing critical
context.
6.2.2.2
Implementation
This approach relies on vector-based similarity search, using the function similarity_search
_with_score from ChromaDB to retrieve the most relevant documents, which are then con-
catenated and structured into a single prompt before being passed to the language model. The
implementation uses Llama 3.1 with 8 billion parameters, which has a relatively limited context
window. Retrieved documents are ordered by relevance score originated by its similarity to the
query and injected into the prompt without additional categorization mechanisms, operating under
the assumption that similarity-based retrieval alone in a single collection will provide sufficient
contextual support to select the most relevant documents to answer the user’s query.
6.2.2.3
Tests and Results
During initial tests with a small dataset consisting of fewer than five short PDFs for SRL and JSON
files containing course content and forum discussions, this method yielded relatively coherent and
relevant responses. The model successfully processed the limited number of documents without
overwhelming its context window, leading to accurate and context-aware outputs, as illustrated in
Figure 6.11.
However, when scaling the approach to incorporate all retrieved Moodle data, performance
significantly deteriorated. The excessive volume of retrieved text exceeded the model’s capacity,
causing responses to become less relevant and often failing to address the user’s query effectively.
Several specific challenges emerged. Essential details were often buried within excessive con-
tent, leading to dilution of relevance. The limited context window of Llama 3.1 (8B) was quickly
exhausted. Key portions of retrieved documents were frequently truncated and the model could
not effectively distinguish between different document types. This led to responses becoming
unfocused as the model struggled to determine relevance.
6.2.2.4
Conclusions
Due to its simplicity, the unfiltered approach proved unsuitable for use with comprehensive course
content. While effective for small document collections, the method’s inability to scale with in-
creasing data volume highlighted the need for more sophisticated retrieval mechanisms. However,

[Vista original de la página 92](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-92.png)

![Página PDF 92, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-92.png)

6.2 Prototype: Design Choices and Challenges
79
(a) Original
(b) Translated
Figure 6.11: Chatbot interaction using RAG with a limited dataset of five short PDFs and JSON
files, demonstrating coherent and relevant responses.
it established a valuable baseline against which more refined methods could be compared, con-
firming that similarity-based retrieval alone is insufficient for complex applications.
6.2.3
Advanced RAG: Thematic Filtering for Context Retrieval
6.2.3.1
Concept
To address the limitations of unfiltered context injection, a refined approach was introduced by
structuring the retrieval process into distinct thematic sections. This concept aimed to organize
information systematically into three predefined categories - self-regulated learning strategies,
course-related content, and student-specific data - rather than treating all retrieved documents as
a single undifferentiated block. The fundamental principle is that thematic organization would
improve response relevance by ensuring content from appropriate domains is incorporated.
6.2.3.2
Implementation
Each thematic category was stored in separate ChromaDB collections, ensuring retrieval was per-
formed in a structured manner rather than drawing from a single large database. The implementa-
tion involved preprocessing documents to assign them to appropriate thematic collections during
the ingestion phase. During query processing, the system performed parallel retrieval from all
three collections, assembling a comprehensive but thematically organized response context.
6.2.3.3
Tests and Results
Testing for this approach involved comparing response quality and relevance against the previous
method. Tests were simple prompts designed to evaluate whether thematic organization improved

[Vista original de la página 93](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-93.png)

![Página PDF 93, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-93.png)


<!-- PDF page 94 -->

## Página PDF 94

Proposed Solution
80
the chatbot’s ability to provide contextually appropriate guidance across different query domains
without mixing all the information.
This implementation improved the relevance of retrieved documents compared to the previ-
ous method, as it ensured each response incorporated context from multiple relevant perspectives
rather than relying on potentially unrelated documents. However, it introduced a major challenge,
since the retrieval process now pulled from three distinct collections simultaneously, the total num-
ber of retrieved documents tripled. Given Llama 3.1 (8B)’s constrained context window, this led
to rapid exhaustion of available space, causing unpredictable truncation of input data.
The critical issue stemming from this overload was the model’s tendency to ignore explicit
prompt instructions. Instead of selectively integrating only relevant data retrieved to answer the
query, the model attempted to incorporate all available information at once, leading to incoherent
responses where the question of the user is completely ignored and the intended query structure is
not followed.
6.2.3.4
Conclusions
While thematic filtering introduced meaningful improvements in structuring information, it also
highlighted the constraints of models with limited context capacity. This approach demonstrated
that organizing information without addressing volume concerns was insufficient unless the model
could handle a large context window. Effective retrieval requires not just better organization but
also more intelligent selection mechanisms to manage context window limitations.
Although this approach laid the foundation for more context-aware responses by retrieving
and structuring multiple relevant sources simultaneously, the model constraints combined with
the increased volume of retrieved data ultimately overwhelmed the system, necessitating further
refinements in the retrieval strategy.
6.2.4
Advanced RAG: Two-Stage Retrieval for Context Optimization
6.2.4.1
Concept
Building on the thematic filtering approach, the retrieval process in this prototype evolved to em-
ploy a two-stage mechanism that aligns with principles of prompt-guided retrieval augmentation
(PGRA), as discussed by [31]. The core concept is to implement a hierarchical selection pro-
cess that first retrieves documents based on general query relevance, and then applies a secondary
filtering layer to identify the most pertinent thematic category for the specific query context.
6.2.4.2
Implementation
In the first stage, documents are fetched from the three previously established collections based
on their semantic similarity to the user query. This initial retrieval resembles the task-agnostic
retriever in the PGRA framework, which builds a shared static index to efficiently select relevant
document candidates.

[Vista original de la página 94](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-94.png)

![Página PDF 94, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-94.png)


<!-- PDF page 95 -->

## Página PDF 95

6.2 Prototype: Design Choices and Challenges
81
Rather than injecting all retrieved content from these three collections directly into the model,
an additional filtering mechanism addresses the context window challenges identified earlier. This
filtering process evaluates the relevance of each category in relation to the specific query, selecting
only the collection that contains the most important documents.
The filtering decision is made through a context selection prompt, where the Large Language
Model agent, prior to answering the student’s final query, determines which context is most ap-
propriate: SRL content, course information content, or user-specific content based on the original
query and a set of descriptions. This approach mirrors the prompt-guided re-ranker in the PGRA
framework, which re-ranks evidence based on task-specific relevance.
The LLM agent identifies the most appropriate context category, which determines which
retrieved documents are selected. By identifying only the most relevant category, the chatbot se-
lectively incorporates only the most critical information into its final response prompt, effectively
addressing the context window limitations while enhancing both efficiency and response accuracy.
6.2.4.3
Tests and Results
Testing focused on comparing response quality, relevance, and generation speed against both pre-
vious approaches. Tests included diverse query types designed to assess whether the system could
correctly identify the most appropriate category of context to answer the user’s query, while main-
taining response accuracy and coherence.
This approach significantly improved efficiency and response quality compared to the previous
methods. By limiting the input context to only the most relevant data, the chatbot maintained co-
herence and eliminated the occurrence of hallucinations that resulted from context overload. This
selective strategy prevented the model from exceeding its context window prematurely, ensuring
that all included information was effectively processed. An example showcasing this approach
is illustrated in Figure 6.12, which demonstrates a chatbot interaction where the model success-
fully selected only the most relevant retrieved information to generate a precise and context-aware
response.
However, testing also revealed several challenges. In certain cases, the chatbot correctly iden-
tified the relevant context but the retrieved embeddings from ChromaDB were not aligned with
the expected ones, leading to inaccuracies in the responses. Additionally, the model exhibited
difficulties in distinguishing between different types of forum-related content, such as the forums,
posts, discussions, and replies, occasionally leading to incorrect information being provided de-
spite the presence of the correct answer within the retrieved context. Another observed issue was
the chatbot’s tendency to confuse user identities, in some instances it would fail to recognize the
user’s name correctly. These findings highlight areas requiring further refinement to improve both
retrieval accuracy and response coherence.

Figure 6.12: Chatbot interaction using RAG with the complete dataset, demonstrating coherent
and relevant responses by selecting the SRL context for the first question and course content
context for the second.
6.2.4.4
Conclusions
Despite its advantages, this method introduced a trade-off between precision and completeness.
In cases where relevant information is distributed across multiple thematic sources, strict filtering
criteria exclude secondary yet useful contextual elements. However, in future scenarios where
the model employed has extended context capacity, this filtering mechanism could be further op-
timized to balance structured guidance with comprehensive information retrieval. For instance,
it could retain self-regulated learning strategies while simultaneously integrating detailed course
content, offering more holistic personalized guidance without requiring excessive pruning.
Moreover, the issues identified during testing suggest several areas for improvement. One key
enhancement would be providing the chatbot with a clearer explanation of the organization of
JSON files, ensuring a more structured retrieval process and reducing confusion between different
types of retrieved content. Additionally, to mitigate errors related to user identity recognition,
the system should be provided with the user ID and name, before processing queries, thereby
providing consistency in user identification and personalization.

[Vista original de la página 96](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-96.png)

## Página PDF 97

6.2 Prototype: Design Choices and Challenges
83
With this solid foundation, further testing is also necessary to refine the chatbot’s performance.
A more comprehensive evaluation framework should be established, incorporating a well-defined
list of complex and typical student queries based on course content. This would enable a more sys-
tematic analysis of response accuracy and the chatbot’s ability to handle diverse student inquiries
effectively. Additionally, ongoing experimentation with new optimization techniques should be
pursued to enhance retrieval efficiency. Possible improvements include generating new categorical
structures, refining data processing, and enhancing memory mechanisms to improve the chatbot’s
ability to maintain context and continuously learn from interactions.
Ultimately, this two-layer RAG strategy represented an effective retrieval mechanism devel-
oped within the prototype’s current constraints. By prioritizing contextual precision over volume,
it enhanced response accuracy while mitigating the risks associated with unfiltered context injec-
tion. Furthermore, it established a scalable foundation for future improvements, ensuring that as
the system evolves, the retrieval mechanism can be adapted to leverage larger context windows
while maintaining structured topic integration, aligning with the goals of the PGRA framework.
6.2.5
Modular RAG: LightRAG Framework
6.2.5.1
Concept
Building upon the two-stage retrieval mechanism discussed earlier, which addressed context op-
timization through hierarchical selection and thematic filtering, this prototype further explores
a more advanced and modular approach to Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) by imple-
menting the LightRAG framework. Developed by [32], LightRAG introduces a graph-based text
indexing paradigm combined with a dual-level retrieval system, aiming to overcome the limita-
tions of context window size, latency, and modularity present in previous retrieval strategies. By
incorporating graph structures into the retrieval process, LightRAG enhances the system’s capac-
ity to capture complex interdependencies among entities, fostering a more nuanced understanding
of relationships and enabling coherent, contextually enriched responses.
The framework’s dual-level retrieval system is designed to extract both low-level information,
such as specific entities and their interrelations, and high-level information, encompassing broader
topics and thematic groupings. This layered approach ensures comprehensive and balanced infor-
mation retrieval, capable of addressing both specific and abstract queries. Furthermore, LightRAG
includes an incremental update mechanism that allows timely integration of new data, ensuring the
system remains accurate and responsive in dynamic environments. By combining these features,
LightRAG significantly enhances retrieval accuracy, efficiency, and adaptability, making it a robust
foundation for modern RAG systems.
6.2.5.2
Implementation
The implementation of the LightRAG framework in this prototype follows a structured integra-
tion of graph-based text indexing and dual-level retrieval into the existing system. The process
begins with the construction of a knowledge graph, where documents are segmented into smaller,

[Vista original de la página 97](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-97.png)

![Página PDF 97, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-97.png)


<!-- PDF page 98 -->

## Página PDF 98

Proposed Solution
84
manageable chunks. These chunks are then processed using a Large Language Model (LLM) to
extract relevant entities (e.g., names, dates, locations) and their relationships, which are repre-
sented as nodes and edges in the graph. This structure enables efficient retrieval of both granular
(entity-specific) and abstract (thematic) information, supporting the generation of comprehensive
and contextually accurate responses.
To adapt LightRAG to the specific requirements of this environment and hardware constraints,
the dataset was replaced with the processed JSON dataset retrieved from Moodle, departing from
the original design, which assumes a single large text source, such as a book. The implementa-
tion employed the Ollama framework instead of an OpenAI API key, utilizing the recommended
Gemma 2B model for low RAM GPUs, which is a lightweight and efficient LLM. While the
framework is ideally suited for GPUs with at least 6GB of VRAM, it was successfully tested on
a 4GB VRAM GPU. The model’s context window was manually configured to 26,000 tokens to
accommodate the dataset’s complexity and the need for detailed retrieval across multiple levels.
The system was configured to perform both low-level and high-level retrieval, ensuring flex-
ibility in handling a wide range of query types. Additionally, the incremental update algorithm,
which allows for seamless integration of new data, was tested to verify the system’s ability to
remain up-to-date and responsive to evolving content. The knowledge graph was generated from
the custom dataset by extracting and linking entities and their relationships, thereby modeling the
internal structure and dependencies present in Moodle’s educational content. This setup allowed
the dual-level retrieval engine to support both focused and thematic queries effectively.
6.2.5.3
Tests and Results
To evaluate the integration of the LightRAG framework within this prototype, the test was con-
ducted using the processed Moodle dataset with objective to assess both the graph construction
process and the system’s retrieval capabilities.
The graph construction phase took approximately 60 minutes to complete. During this pro-
cess, the system extracted low-level and high-level keywords and established a small number of
relationships within the knowledge graph. A few connections were correctly identified, for in-
stance, between professors and the courses they lecture, among student interactions, and themes
of forum discussions, demonstrating LightRAG’s potential to support hybrid retrieval combining
entity-level detail and thematic information. However, despite the number of entities processed,
the final graph revealed only a minimal set of meaningful connections, suggesting limited success
in uncovering deeper inter-dependencies within the dataset. Additionally, the system included
connections from the example prompt provided in the framework, which did not belong to the
actual dataset. The result of this test, including the sparsity of the generated knowledge graph, is
illustrated in Figure 6.13.
Regarding retrieval performance, the system returned responses in approximately 30 seconds.
However, the output consisted mostly of invisible characters or blank content. This behavior points
to incompatibilities with structured JSON data, which is not natively supported by the current

[Vista original de la página 98](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-98.png)

![Página PDF 98, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-98.png)


<!-- PDF page 99 -->

## Página PDF 99

6.2 Prototype: Design Choices and Challenges
85
Figure 6.13: Sparse knowledge graph generated by LightRAG using the processed Moodle dataset.
Only a few relationships were established among many detected entities, and some nodes originate
from unrelated example data.
LightRAG design. As such, the framework appears limited to only suit linear text input, such as
books or single-document corpora, rather than datasets composed of multiple structured files.
6.2.5.4
Conclusion
Although the LightRAG framework offers a theoretical robust approach to Retrieval-Augmented
Generation by combining graph-based indexing with dual-level retrieval, its application in this
prototype yielded limited results. The graph construction process did not produce a dense or
informative network of relationships. Only a few connections emerged among dozens of entities,
failing to reflect the richness of the underlying educational dataset.
Moreover, the framework’s inability to handle multiple structured JSON files significantly
impaired its effectiveness. The generation of blank or invisible responses during testing further
highlights the challenges in adapting LightRAG to structured and modular data formats.
Despite these setbacks, the experiment demonstrates that LightRAG could still hold promise
in educational contexts, provided that it is adapted to process structured datasets more effectively.
Future work should focus on extending the framework itself to natively support graph construction
from hierarchical data sources such as Moodle exports.

## Página PDF 100

Proposed Solution
86
6.3
Prototype: Final Iteration
This section presents the final iteration of the prototype, focusing on key enhancements made
to the Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework. Building on previous development
cycles, this version introduces substantial improvements in data processing, retrieval organiza-
tion, and response generation. The transformation of structured JSON data into natural language
content within the same format significantly increased the relevance of retrieved information. Re-
trieval was further refined by restructuring the database into multiple thematic collections and
implementing a two-step context selection mechanism: one step filters SRL-relevant embeddings,
and the other retrieves complementary course-related content. Additionally, targeted prompt engi-
neering was applied to improve the overall quality and coherence of the chatbot’s responses. An
overview of initial results and insights is also provided, highlighting the system’s evolution until
this point and suggesting paths for future development.
6.3.1
Data Preprocessing
The data preprocessing stage was crucial to optimize the chatbot’s RAG performance in Por-
tuguese while handling complex, nested, structured data from Moodle in English. To improve
upon previous approaches, this step focused on transforming the preprocessing of the JSON input
even further, into a semantically meaningful format suitable for efficient embedding and retrieval,
rather than relying solely on the model’s capabilities to understand the semantic structure of the
retrieved JSON files.
To achieve this, following data extraction, the raw Moodle data was transformed into struc-
tured JSON files. Each data type, including course metadata, contents, enrolled users, forums,
discussions, completion status, quizzes, and quiz attempts, was processed into a standardized
format comprising two fields: a content field containing a natural language summary, and a
metadata field preserving the original structured attributes, both in Portuguese. This dual rep-
resentation supports both semantic retrieval and precise traceability to the source data. HTML
content was cleaned and converted to plain text, file attachments were preserved with their names
and URLs, and forums and quiz interaction logs were reconstructed in a readable format. The
processed files were organized by category and user, enabling selective embedding and retrieval
during the RAG pipeline described in the following sections.
This separation allows the agent to focus on the most relevant natural language content during
retrieval while retaining access to full metadata for filtering and validation, if necessary. It also
enables verification of whether the content accurately reflects the original structure.
The result was a standardized, language-consistent dataset optimized automatically for the
RAG pipeline. This structure aims to improve both retrieval accuracy and chatbot responses by
presenting data in a form that the LLM can understand more easily than structured input, which
often lacks explicit context or relational cues.

[Vista original de la página 100](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-100.png)

![Página PDF 100, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-100.png)


<!-- PDF page 101 -->

## Página PDF 101

6.3 Prototype: Final Iteration
87
6.3.2
Data Embedding
The data embedding phase is a critical step in the final iteration of the prototype, focusing on the
efficient storage and retrieval of preprocessed Moodle data. This phase involves converting the
structured JSON data into a format suitable for embedding in the ChromaDB vector database. The
goal is to ensure that the chatbot can quickly and accurately retrieve relevant information during
interactions, enhancing its performance and responsiveness.
The embedding process begins by loading the preprocessed JSON files, which have been trans-
formed into a standardized format comprising content and metadata fields. These files are
then converted into Document objects, a format compatible with ChromaDB. Unlike the pre-
vious approach, which embedded entire documents for automatic chunking, this refined method
embeds the content of each object, such as forum posts, discussions, or quiz attempts, as separate
document chunks. This ensures that each chunk retains complete semantic meaning and is not
arbitrarily split, which could otherwise result in incomplete or fragmented information.
Specifically, each content item is treated as an individual document. If the content exceeds the
800 character chunk size, it is split into smaller, coherent segments that preserve the integrity of
the information. This approach enhances the semantic significance of each document, ensuring
that every chunk contains meaningful and complete information. However, this method still does
not apply to Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) data, which consists of PDFs containing theoretical
foundations and strategies. The entire PDF content is still embedded arbitrarily, while maintaining
its coherence.
The calculate_chunk_ids function generates unique identifiers for each document chunk,
ensuring that each piece of data can be uniquely identified within the database. Additionally, the
flatten_metadata function ensures that the metadata is compatible with ChromaDB by con-
verting the nested dictionaries and lists into a flat structure.
The embedding process is divided into several stages, each focusing on a specific type of data.
Course information and content, such as module and section names, are loaded and added to their
own ChromaDB collection named course_data. SRL PDF documents are loaded using the
PyPDFDirectoryLoader and converted into Document objects, which are added to a separate
ChromaDB collection named srl_data. For each user, the embedding process loads forum dis-
cussions, quiz attempts, and completion status data. This data is enriched with module information
to ensure that each chunk makes sense even when isolated from the rest of the content. The en-
riched data is then converted into Document objects, with each user’s data added to separate Chro-
maDB collections named user_forum_data_<user_id>, user_quiz_data_<user_id>,
and user_progress_data_<user_id>.
The process_and_add_to_collection function handles the splitting of documents into
chunks, calculating unique IDs, and adding them to the specified ChromaDB collection. The
collections created, compared to the original organization presented in Section 6.2.1, in Figure
6.10, can be visualized here in Figure 6.14. This function ensures that the data is efficiently stored
and retrievable, even for large datasets. The embedding process is automated and can be reset

[Vista original de la página 101](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-101.png)

![Página PDF 101, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-101.png)


<!-- PDF page 102 -->

## Página PDF 102

Proposed Solution
88
using a flag, to clear the existing ChromaDB directory before populating it with new data. This
makes sure that the database is always up-to-date and free from outdated or irrelevant information,
when necessary.
By converting the preprocessed JSON data into a format suitable for embedding in ChromaDB,
the embedding phase enhances the chatbot’s ability to retrieve relevant information quickly and
accurately.
Figure 6.14: Collections embedded from processed Moodle data, categorized per student for each
course
6.3.3
Data Retrieval
The data retrieval phase is crucial for the chatbot’s ability to provide contextually relevant and
accurate responses. This phase involves querying the ChromaDB vector database to fetch the

[Vista original de la página 102](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-102.png)

6.3 Prototype: Final Iteration
89
most relevant documents based on the user’s input. The retrieved documents are then used to con-
struct the chatbot’s response, ensuring that the information provided is useful to answer student’s
questions.
6.3.3.1
Additional Context Selection
The selection of additional context is a dynamic process that determines the most relevant supple-
mentary information from the dataset to support the chatbot’s response. This process is essential
for enhancing the chatbot’s ability to provide tailored and informative answers.
The predict_context function is central to this process. Similar to previous approaches,
it takes the user’s query and the conversation history as inputs and uses a Large Language Model
(LLM) to predict the most relevant additional context type from a predefined set of options corre-
sponding to each collection. The function uses a prompt template that lists the following context
types: general course data, user-related forum data, user-related quiz data, and user progress data.
The model then predicts which of these contexts is most relevant to the user’s query following this
prompt:
1
Pergunta do Estudante: {question}
2
3
Qual destes contextos é o MAIS relevante para responder à pergunta do estudante?
4
[User-Forum-Context]: Fóruns e participações do estudante nos fóruns.
5
[User-Quiz-Context]: Questionários/Desafios e tentativas do estudante nos questioná
rios ou desafios metacognitivos.
6
[Course-Context]: Informações gerais sobre o curso e seus conteúdos (como está
estruturado ao longo de cada semana (etapa), quais os módulos disponíveis,
estudantes inscritos, etc).
7
[User-Progress-Context]: Estado de conclusão/progresso do estudante nos módulos.
8
9
Responde APENAS com o nome do contexto entre parênteses retos (exemplo: [User-
Progress-Context]).
10
11
Histórico do diálogo anterior à pergunta, se necessário para compreender a pergunta
:
12
{history}
For each context type, the top five most relevant documents are retrieved from ChromaDB,
for a total of 20 documents. The similarity of the embeddings is calculated using a cosine simi-
larity measure, which determines how closely the embeddings of the original user query and the
documents match, ensuring that the most semantically similar documents are retrieved.
The prediction is made using a language model, which generates a response based on the
prompt template, containing only the name of the context selected. The response is then parsed
to extract the predicted context type. This context type is used to retrieve only the five most
relevant corresponding documents from the ChromaDB selected collection, instead of all 20. The

90
retrieved documents are subsequently used to enrich the chatbot’s response, providing additional
information that is directly relevant to the user’s query.
6.3.3.2
SRL Context Retrieval
Unlike the previous approach where the Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) context was one of sev-
eral optional retrievals, the current implementation establishes SRL support as a required and
non-negotiable part of the data retrieval pipeline. In earlier prototypes, if the model did not ex-
plicitly select SRL as the most relevant context, it could be excluded from the response, leading
to inconsistent guidance on learning strategies. Now, SRL content is always included, ensuring
that students receive structured, pedagogically grounded support aligned with [106]’s model of
self-regulated learning, as well as strategies specific to support those phases of the model and to
provide feedback on metacognitive challenges.
A major improvement in this version is the use of enhanced SRL materials rather than re-
lying solely on [106]’s model framework, or solely on the LLM’s internal knowledge when the
model does not select this specific context. The SRL data used is derived from chapters authored
by Daniela Pedrosa, a domain expert in SRL and metacognition, from a pedagogical handbook
produced by Universidade Aberta [69]. Specifically, Chapters 2.2, “Autorregulação e Corregu-
lação das Aprendizagens”, and 7.1, “Desafios Metacognitivos: Como o Docente Pode Analisar e
Intervir”, were used, as they provide both theoretical insights on all SRL phases (forethought, per-
formance and self-reflection) and subprocesses while providing practical strategies for addressing
those processes and for providing feedback on metacognitive challenges. These chapters were part
of a single PDF, and were selected by recommendation of the instructors. Since the PDF contains
comprehensive SRL information covering all three phases, it ensures that the retrieved content
remains aligned with Zimmerman’s model, regardless of which phase the student’s query relates
to. This is achieved through semantic similarity, allowing the system to retrieve relevant strategies
and concepts associated with each phase based on the query’s meaning.
The retrieval process begins with the refine_for_srl_search function, which uses a
prompt-guided query rewriting approach. A Large Language Model (LLM) evaluates the student’s
original question and rewrites it to target either general SRL strategies or pedagogical feedback
for metacognitive challenges. This refinement is key to increasing the semantic relevance of the
retrieved documents during the similarity search of embeddings. The prompt used, that includes
terms better aligned with SRL theory, is as follows:
1
Pergunta do estudante: "{question}"
2
Decide qual das seguintes situações se aplica com base no conteúdo da pergunta:
3
4
1. Se a pergunta for sobre um desafio metacognitivo (refere-se a reflexões,
dificuldades, autoavaliações ou resultados de um desafio metacognitivo), então
reformula a pergunta de forma a facilitar a recuperação de **orientações de
feedback pedagógico**.
5

The model responds with a single reformulated sentence that incorporates these cues. This
refined query is then used to search the SRL collection stored in ChromaDB. From this search,
only the top three most relevant documents are retrieved, given that these texts are preprocessed
into 800 character chunks and tend to be dense and comprehensive enough to support high-quality
answers without overwhelming the model context window.
This improvement contributes for the reliability and quality of the SRL integration by ground-
ing the chatbot’s responses in institutionally validated materials and by ensuring alignment with
both theoretical and practical dimensions of SRL. It ensures that all three phases of Zimmerman’s
cycle, forethought, performance, and self-reflection, are properly addressed based on real peda-
gogical intent, not just LLM estimations.

6.3.4
Chatbot Interaction Flow
This chatbot interaction system seamlessly integrates data retrieval processes to deliver person-
alized, context-aware responses that blend course content with self-regulated learning support.
The system architecture ensures efficient context management while maintaining responsiveness
within model constraints.
When a user submits a query, the chatbot executes a structured workflow. First, the system
retrieves and formats the student’s recent conversation history (identified by username), ensur-
ing that context remains relevant without overwhelming the model’s context window. Next, the
chatbot analyzes the query and conversation history to determine the most appropriate supplemen-
tary context type from available options: general course data, user-specific forum activity, quiz
performance data, or progress tracking information. The user interface designed to support this
interaction is shown in Figure 6.15, which includes the login screen and settings screen used to
configure the chatbot’s behavior.
(a) Login screen
(b) Settings screen
Figure 6.15: Overview of the user interface screens.
Two parallel retrieval operations then occur: the system retrieves the five most relevant doc-
uments for the primary question context from the selected collection; simultaneously, it refines
the user’s query specifically for SRL purposes and retrieves the three most relevant SRL strategy
documents. This careful selection of only eight documents in total ensures the context remains
manageable within the model’s context window constraints, a significant improvement over pre-
vious prototype iterations where context overflow was a issue.
The retrieved documents are integrated into a structured prompt template, which guides the
large language model to generate a comprehensive and contextually appropriate response, one
that addresses both the student’s question and provides relevant support based on self-regulated
learning strategies. The conversation history is updated after each interaction, with an option for
users to reset their history when desired.

6.3.5
Prompt Design Engineering
This section documents the iterative refinement of Catmming’s system prompt as a designed arti-
fact. Each prompt version reflects a cycle of conceptualization, development, demonstration, and
evaluation, in line with the Design Science Research Methodology (DSRM). The process began
with a heavily structured initial prompt, which was later refined in response to observed limitations
during testing and analysis.
Prompt engineering emerged as a critical factor influencing the assistant’s ability to deliver
personalized, pedagogically sound, and contextually grounded support for self-regulated learning.
This section describes the two main prompt versions developed, their structural differences, and
how they reflect the evolving design priorities of the system. Both prompts were used without
modifying the remaining system components and were tested under identical conditions to enable
a comparative evaluation of their impact on the chatbot’s performance, as presented in Chapter 7.
6.3.5.1
Intent of the Prompt
The system prompt establishes the behavioral boundaries and communicative goals of the LLM
behind Catmming. In this case, the assistant is expected to operate exclusively in European Por-
tuguese, act as a personalized SRL support agent grounded in Zimmerman’s cyclical model of
Forethought, Performance, and Self-Reflection, interpret user queries carefully, provide concrete
strategies when possible, and request clarification when necessary. Additionally, it must leverage
relevant context, such as SRL theory, course content, and user-specific interactions and reflections,
without hallucinating information or providing generic advice unnecessarily.
Given the complex nature of SRL support, special care was taken to instruct the assistant on
how to efficiently draw from the various contextual sources provided at runtime in order to deliver
accurate, relevant, and actionable responses.
6.3.5.2
Initial Prompt Version
The first prompt version was heavily structured, with clearly labeled sections such as Contexto de
Autorregulação e Feedback (SRL) and Contexto Adicional, as well as explicit instructions on how
to link the student’s input to relevant SRL strategies. It included strict response guidelines, which
directed the assistant to interpret the user’s input based on Zimmerman’s model, avoid generic
advice when contextual data is available, speak in European Portuguese at all times, maintain a
respectful, pedagogically appropriate tone, personalize responses using user-specific data when
available and ask for clarification if the query is ambiguous. These guidelines emphasized conver-
sational clarity and encouraged direct references to past reflections or learning difficulties.
This version also attempted to enforce personalization by referencing the user ID and stating
explicitly that the assistant should speak directly to the student. However, despite these instruc-
tions, the model would misinterpreted the context and referred to the student in the third person,

[Vista original de la página 107](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-107.png)

![Página PDF 107, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-107.png)


<!-- PDF page 108 -->

## Página PDF 108

Proposed Solution
94
sometimes suggesting that the student engage in dialogue with themselves, as if they were a sep-
arate entity. This behavior exposed limitations in how the model handled identity resolution and
prompted further refinement in the next iteration.
Although effective in many scenarios, this version proved overly rigid in tone and prone to
overloading the model with formatting metadata, which at times surfaced in responses. This led
the agent to repeat or reference internal section titles not visible to the user, thereby breaking the
illusion of a natural conversational interface.
These challenges provided critical feedback for evaluating the effectiveness of the initial de-
sign and directly informed the development of a revised prompt.
6.3.5.3
Final Prompt Version
The final prompt version aimed to retain the pedagogical rigor of the initial design while improving
conversational flow, reducing formatting artifacts, and minimizing the likelihood of hallucinations.
One of the most significant changes involved removing explicit section titles from the prompt
content. These were replaced with more natural contextual cues and explanatory phrases, making
the structure more fluid and less likely to surface in the assistant’s responses. Each context section
was rewritten using descriptive language instead of rigid headings, which made the instructions
more interpretable by the model and provided clearer guidance on how to apply the contextual
data effectively.
Another major improvement addressed how the assistant should interpret entries linked to the
user ID. In the initial prompt, the model occasionally failed to associate the user with data retrieved
from Moodle, such as forum posts, quiz answers, or responses to metacognitive challenges, and
instead referred to the student in the third person. At times, it even encouraged the user to address
themselves for help, as if they were a separate person. To correct this, the final prompt explicitly
instructed the assistant to treat all data associated with the current user ID as belonging to the
person it was directly speaking to. This eliminated ambiguity and helped ensure that the assistant
understood the meaning of the retrieved content and communicated naturally in the second person,
maintaining a personalized and coherent tone.
In addition, this version adopted a slightly more conversational style. The tone was softened
to reduce excessive formality while maintaining academic credibility. Rather than enforcing rigid
response patterns, the prompt encouraged the assistant to apply SRL strategies flexibly, based on
the nature of the retrieved data and the specific needs expressed by the student. This shift aimed to
strike a balance between structural control and dialogue adaptability, improving both personaliza-
tion and pedagogical intent. The revised version thus reflects a new conceptual understanding of
the assistant’s role, translated into concrete changes in the prompt’s structure and language. These
modifications were then demonstrated through the assistant’s use in real interactions and prepared
for evaluation under the same conditions as the original version, setting the stage for a comparative
analysis:
