6.3.5.4
Prompt Comparison and Testing Rationale
Both prompt system versions were evaluated using the same implementation of the system, ensur-
ing that only the prompt design varied across test conditions. No changes were made to the re-
trieval architecture, or contextual data sources, allowing for an isolated assessment of how prompt
formulation influenced the assistant’s behavior for this architecture.
This comparative setup enabled a controlled investigation into the impact of these prompt
design choices on the assistant’s ability to support self-regulated learning through a natural con-
versation. Each prompt was tested against the same functional SRL evaluation suite, described
in Section 7.6, which covered all subprocesses of Zimmerman’s cyclical model. The assistant’s
responses were analyzed according to their alignment with self-regulation support, the person-
alization achieved, and their ability to interpret context meaningfully. The resulting insights are
presented in Chapter 7, forming the basis for reflecting on the strengths and limitations of each
design iteration and informing further refinement.
6.3.6
Preliminary Testing and Insights
Initial testing conducted on the final iteration of the system using Llama3.1:8b, still prior to im-
plementation of the sophisticated prompt engineering explained in the previous section, already
revealed notable improvements in response quality, contextual understanding, and alignment with
Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) support goals. Compared to earlier prototypes, the updated ver-
sion of the chatbot consistently generated more complete and pedagogically meaningful responses,
especially in terms of offering concrete strategies to support students’ self-regulated learning.
One key enhancement was the improved selection of relevant context embeddings. This was
achieved through a more granular and logically separated collection structure within the vector
database, as well as a refined selection logic that more effectively matched user queries to relevant

![Página PDF 110, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-110.png)

cessfully prioritized quiz interaction logs over unrelated content, allowing the chatbot to generate
more precise and context-aware responses.

Similarly, Figure 6.17 shows an example where the chatbot correctly predicted the User-Forum-

These preliminary qualitative results indicate that the proposed system improvements en-
hanced both the technical retrieval precision and the pedagogical relevance of the responses. Fur-
ther evaluation with a broader range of queries and user interactions will quantitatively assess the
system’s effectiveness across all SRL subprocesses, in Chapter 7.
6.3.7
Conclusion
This final implementation represents a significant advancement over previous prototypes through
several key innovations. By transitioning from structured JSON to natural language content, we’ve
substantially improved context utilization efficiency within the model’s constraints, while provid-
ing more semantic meaning to the data from Moodle. The mandatory inclusion of SRL context,
rather than treating it as an optional selection, ensures that every interaction provides valuable
learning strategy guidance alongside subject matter content, addressing student needs as a co-
regulated assistant for self-regulation.

(a) Original

scenario.
The dual-context approach, carefully limited to eight precisely selected documents, strikes an
optimal balance between comprehensive information and technical constraints, enabling responses
that address immediate questions and long-term learning. This architectural decision prevents
context window overflow while maximizing relevance.
Perhaps most significantly, this refined context selection mechanism, which now distinguishes
between general course data, forums with student’s forum participation, quizzes with student’s
quiz performance, and activity progress tracking, represents a major improvement over earlier
prototypes that relied on just three broad collection categories (general course contents data, in-
cluding forums and quizzes, student activity data and SRL data). This granular approach enables
more precise information retrieval tailored to the specific nature of each query, dramatically im-
proving response relevance.
Finally, by maintaining personalized conversation history and context for each student, the
system delivers truly individualized support that adapts to each learner’s unique journey, chal-
lenges, and progress patterns. Together, these enhancements aim to create an intelligent virtual
assistant for Self-Regulated Learning that provides relevant, and pedagogically sound assistance
while managing the technical limitations inherent in large language model applications.

## Página PDF 113

Chapter 7
Evaluation
7.1
Introduction and Scope of the Evaluation
This chapter presents the design and instrumentation of the evaluation methods used to assess
the effectiveness of the LLM-based chatbot prototype developed in supporting Self-Regulated
Learning (SRL). It aligns with the Design Science Research methodology adopted throughout this
project by evaluating the concrete functional and non-functional goals previously defined, that
lead to the design of artifacts and that were evaluated iteratively to refine them.
This chapter does not present or discuss the results fully, which are outlined in the next chapter,
Chapter 8. Instead, it details the structure of the test suite executed, the configuration of the models
under test, the metrics captured, and the logging mechanisms used during evaluation.
The goal is to demonstrate how the evaluation process was designed to enable rigorous, trace-
able comparison between the last iterations of the assistant, with different large language models
used, and prompt engineering strategies.
7.2
Evaluation Objectives and Test Environment
The evaluation conducted in this chapter aimed to assess the system’s capacity to support all
subprocesses of Zimmerman’s model of self-regulated learning (SRL), examine the pedagogical
impact of different prompt designs, and explore how model size influences the quality and align-
ment of generated responses. These goals were pursued by executing a controlled test suite across
multiple conditions, isolating key variables related to prompt engineering and language model
capabilities.
To this end, a series of 21 structured tests were conducted using two local large language
models: Llama 3.1 with 8 billion parameters and Gemma 3 with 12 billion parameters. Both
models were run in non-quantized form, on the same hardware environment to ensure consistency,
using the identical conversational pipeline and retrieval logic throughout. The assistant’s access to
contextual data was the same for all runs, in order to better compare the models used in the same
conditions, with only the reliance in the same SRL-related PDFs and JSON-formatted Moodle data
99

[Vista original de la página 113](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-113.png)

![Página PDF 113, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-113.png)


<!-- PDF page 114 -->

## Página PDF 114

Evaluation
100
from the course. The chatbot also had access to the two previous interactions with the student, the
last two pairs of messages, so each test was executed in a fixed sequence. This allowed for the
observation of whether the chatbot leveraged the message history to provide a more personalized
and coherent conversation, and whether it used prior questions to establish a connection between
different steps of the self-regulated cycle.
Each test instance was executed under one of two distinct prompt configurations, either the ini-
tial structured prompt or the refined final version described in the previous chapter, Section 6.3.5.
This approach allowed for a comparative analysis of how prompt formulation affected the assis-
tant’s ability to interpret context, apply SRL strategies, and generate accurate and pedagogically
relevant outputs. Importantly, the only varying factors across test batches were the model size and
prompt version, enabling a more reliable interpretation of their individual contributions to system
performance.
This evaluation environment thus provided a consistent, reproducible basis for analyzing the
assistant’s behavior across SRL subprocesses, while enabling insight into whether observed im-
provements stemmed from better model architecture, better prompt engineering, or a combination
of both. The results of these tests, as well as performance metrics and behavioral observations, are
presented in detail in Chapter 8.
Figure 7.1: Test Execution Cycle
7.3
Compliance with Evaluation Methodologies
The evaluation strategy developed was designed to align with the established methodologies for
evaluating Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems, as described in the literature pre-

[Vista original de la página 114](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-114.png)

![Página PDF 114, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-114.png)


<!-- PDF page 115 -->

## Página PDF 115

7.4 Evaluation of Functional and Non-Functional Requirements
101
sented in Section 2.1.9.4 [29, 17]. In particular, this work draws from the dual approach of in-
dependent and end-to-end evaluation methods. The independent evaluation is enabled through
detailed logging of retrieval scores and associated metadata for both SRL and forum-related con-
texts. These logs support post hoc calculation of standard Information Retrieval metrics such as
Hit Rate, Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR), and Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain (NDCG),
which are commonly employed to assess retrieval quality [29].
End-to-end evaluation is addressed through a test set of manually crafted user queries that
target specific subprocesses of self-regulated learning. This approach ensures that each final an-
swer can be examined with respect to its faithfulness to the retrieved content, its relevance to the
original user intent, and the contextual appropriateness of the supporting documents, in line with
the criteria outlined by [17]. The evaluation logs capture all required components for this process,
including the original and refined queries, retrieved document chunks, relevance scores, predicted
context types, and final generated responses.
Although frameworks such as RAGAS [25] and ARES [79] offer automated mechanisms for
evaluating faithfulness and generation quality using synthetic data and LLM-based judgment, this
work adopts a more controlled evaluation process. Rather than generating synthetic inputs, which
can introduce variability and reduce control over targeted subprocesses, this dissertation uses de-
liberately constructed test queries (Tables 7.4, 7.5, 7.6) that are mapped to Zimmerman’s self-
regulated learning model. This strategy ensures both theoretical rigor and interpretability in as-
sessing the chatbot’s behavior.
Therefore, while automation tools were not employed directly, the evaluation system and test
design remain fully compliant with the principles and metrics found in current RAG literature. The
log structure presented in Section 7.7 also leaves open the possibility of integrating LLM-based
assessment or synthetic benchmarking, thus ensuring scalability and extensibility of the evaluation
framework.
7.4
Evaluation of Functional and Non-Functional Requirements
To assess the effectiveness and robustness of the developed chatbot, both functional and non-
functional requirements, defined in Section 5.1, were systematically evaluated. The evaluation
strategy aimed to measure the agent’s ability to support all phases of the Self-Regulated Learning
(SRL) cycle, while also ensuring technical performance, reliability, security, and usability, in a
realistic educational setting.
Taken together, the evaluation of these non-functional requirements, whether through logging,
prototyping, or technical inspection, ensures that the chatbot meets a robust set of operational
criteria necessary for deployment in asynchronous higher education contexts. These assessments
complement the functional tests, providing a holistic view of the system’s readiness for real-world
application.

## Página PDF 116

Evaluation
102
7.4.1
Evaluation of Functional Requirements
To assess the effectiveness of the developed chatbot in comprehensively supporting all phases of
the Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) cycle, a structured set of functional Self-regulated Learning
test cases was created. These tests directly address the functional requirements F5 to F9 listed
in Table 5.1, focusing on the agent’s ability to provide pedagogically relevant guidance before,
during, and after learning activities (F5), leverage Moodle activity data for tailored responses
(F6), dynamically retrieve course-specific information (F7), recognize common SRL challenges
(F8), and communicate naturally in Portuguese (F9).
Additionally, the remaining functional requirements F1 to F4 were also taken into account
during development and are assessed as follows. Real-time feedback (F1) is verified through the
inspection of system response times as logged during chatbot usage. Requirement F2, regarding
accessibility as a standalone web application, was fulfilled during the deployment phase by hosting
the chatbot as a browser-accessible interface independent of Moodle, yet still capable of being used
in moodle through a Moodle Block. Requirement F3, the use of Retrieval-Augmented Generation
(RAG) for grounding responses, is a core feature of the system and was implemented from the
outset to ensure that answers are context-aware and content-aligned. Finally, requirement F4,
involving the storage and retrieval of each user interaction history, was implemented to allow the
agent to reference prior exchanges and support more adaptive and personalized interactions.
7.4.2
Evaluation of Non Functional Requirements
In parallel with the functional evaluation, the non-functional requirements defined in Table 5.2
were also taken into account throughout the design, development, and testing phases of the chat-
bot. These requirements address critical aspects such as performance, reliability, portability, main-
tainability, and data privacy, which influence the feasibility, usability, and long-term viability of
the system beyond its pedagogical capabilities.
7.5
Non-Functional Evaluation
Several non-functional requirements were either directly or indirectly evaluated through the struc-
tured logging system detailed in Section 7.7. Specifically, performance-related metrics such as
response time (NF1) were captured for each major step of the interaction pipeline. This includes
the time required for query refinement, SRL context retrieval, prediction and retrieval of the addi-
tional context type, generation of the final answer, and the total duration of the response process.
These logs enabled the identification of performance bottlenecks and provided empirical data to
validate that response times remained within acceptable thresholds during normal usage.
Likewise, reliability (NF5) was partially assessed by analyzing the consistency and relevance
of the chatbot’s responses across the 21 functional SRL tests. Each test case in the log includes the
final answer generated, associated context chunks, and metadata such as model type and context
sources, which allow the detection of system errors, irrelevant answers, or missing content. This

[Vista original de la página 116](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-116.png)

![Página PDF 116, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-116.png)


<!-- PDF page 117 -->

## Página PDF 117

7.6 Functional Evaluation
103
information facilitates qualitative and quantitative judgments about the system’s ability to perform
reliably under realistic academic conditions.
Scalability (NF2) was not tested in a full production Moodle environment with live concurrent
users, which would be necessary for a comprehensive evaluation. However, informal scalability
tests were conducted during the development of the prototypes by gradually increasing the size
of the dataset used for retrieval (e.g., number of forum discussions, metacognitive challenges, and
course documents). These tests ensured that the system could handle a growing amount of contex-
tual data without significant degradation in response time or retrieval accuracy. Nevertheless, full
scalability validation remains as future work to be addressed under real deployment conditions.
Some non-functional requirements were validated through technical implementation and hands-
on integration tests. For instance, requirements related to system architecture, such as portability
(NF3), compatibility with Moodle (NF4), and availability in a university Moodle environment
(NF7), were confirmed by deploying the chatbot as a standalone web interface and embedding it
within a locally hosted Moodle instance, through a Moodle Block. This verifies that the chatbot
runs on major browsers and operating systems and integrates without requiring any modifications
to Moodle’s internal database structure.
Maintainability (NF6) was ensured by adhering to modular programming principles and ac-
companying the code of documentation for each component. The system was designed to facilitate
future updates and extensions by other developers or researchers. Security (NF8) was also priori-
tized from the outset since all student data and interaction logs were processed and stored locally,
ensuring full compliance with GDPR and institutional data privacy standards, with no data trans-
mitted to external servers.
Finally, usability (NF9) was qualitatively assessed through introspective testing during devel-
opment. The chatbot interface, being similar to existing alternatives, was designed to be intuitive
and accessible for non-technical users, with natural language prompts and simple visual feedback.
Although no formal usability testing was conducted, internal feedback from prototype testing
confirmed that the interface was easy to use and required no technical background to interact
effectively with the chatbot.
7.6
Functional Evaluation
As detailed in Subsection 2.1.5.1, the chatbot was designed to align with [106] cyclical model of
SRL, which breaks down learning into the Forethought, Performance, and Self-Reflection phases,
each with its own processes and subprocesses. To verify this alignment in practice, each functional
test simulates a realistic student query and explicitly targets a specific SRL subprocess, such as
goal-setting, self-instruction, or causal attribution.
Each test consists of a plausible student query presented in both English and Portuguese, along
with a thematic classification, the expected SRL phase(s), process(es), and subprocess(es) in-
volved, and a description of the ideal response behavior the chatbot should exhibit. The chatbot

[Vista original de la página 117](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-117.png)

![Página PDF 117, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-117.png)


<!-- PDF page 118 -->

## Página PDF 118

Evaluation
104
is expected to reference course-specific data it has access to (e.g., Moodle forum discussions or
metacognitive challenges), while still relying on SRL strategies to guide the student.
To ensure that the tests were pedagogically valid and theoretically grounded, an iterative devel-
opment process was followed. Initially, a preliminary set of functional test queries was authored
and manually mapped to the SRL framework from [106]. However, to reduce interpretive bias
and obtain expert-like feedback at scale, this theoretical framework was presented to ChatGPT-4o
along with contextual information about the course structure, goals, and the RAG agent’s tech-
nical capabilities. The LLM was asked to classify each test scenario in terms of SRL phase(s),
process(es), and subprocess(es), and to describe the optimal chatbot behavior to support effective
learning regulation in that context.
The results from this automated classification were used to generate a provisional expert ver-
sion of the SRL test set. This version was subsequently reviewed and validated in person by
Professor Daniela Pedrosa, a domain expert in self-regulated learning, who provided critical feed-
back to ensure alignment with SRL theory and educational best practices. Based on this feedback,
several tests were revised, merged, or replaced to eliminate redundancy and improve subprocess
coverage. The final test set offers a comprehensive and structured evaluation of the chatbot’s
ability to support SRL through natural language interactions grounded in course context.
For clarity and ease of analysis, both the initial and final versions of the functional test set
are presented in parallel, with the changes between them summarized in Table 7.7. The original
version is shown across Table 7.1, Table 7.2, and Table 7.3, while the revised version is depicted
across Table 7.4, Table 7.5, and Table 7.6. These tables include the test ID, student query, SRL
phases, processes, subprocesses targeted, and expected behavior. The relevant course context
associated with each test is listed separately in Table 7.8, providing a detailed framework for
evaluating the chatbot’s pedagogical functionality.

## Página PDF 125

7.6 Functional Evaluation
111
This final test set provides a structured and theoretically grounded framework for evaluating
whether the chatbot’s behavior aligns with the subprocesses of the SRL model by [106]. By sys-
tematically covering all phases and subprocesses, these tests allow for a fine-grained assessment
of the chatbot’s pedagogical capabilities, highlighting both its strengths and areas for future im-
provement. This ensures that the functional evaluation is not only comprehensive but also directly
tied to the educational objectives of supporting self-regulated learning in asynchronous higher
education.
7.6.1
Revision and Validation of the SRL Tests
After submitting the first version of the SRL test tables for feedback, several important changes
were implemented in response to the expert’s suggestions. The feedback emphasized the need for
greater precision in the application of the model by [106], particularly regarding the categorization
of subprocesses and the dynamic relationships between phases, processes, and subprocesses. One
of the main recommendations was to ensure a stricter and more consistent alignment between
each student query and the specific subprocesses it was intended to support. This led to a thorough
revision of the mappings in each test, with changes in some queries following the expert’s feedback
on why each query should correspond to a particular subprocess.
Additionally, some test cases were flagged as ambiguous, lacking a clearly delineated subpro-
cess to be tested. As a result, these tests were either removed, rewritten to simplify and clarify
their scope, or redistributed to better reflect distinct subprocess targets. For instance, several tests
were consolidated or reworded to better clarify their intent and eliminate redundancy between
subprocesses.
To further improve coherence, all test IDs were reorganized to follow the sequential order
of Zimmerman’s three phases of self-regulation: Forethought, Performance, and Self-Reflection.
This restructuring ensures that the final test set presents a clear and complete distribution across
all subprocesses, with at least one test case corresponding to each.
A detailed summary of these changes, including which test cases were modified, removed,
added, or kept unchanged, is presented in Table 7.7. This mapping supports traceability between
the original and final test versions and helps justify the rationale behind each modification.
The revised and final version of the test suite, presented in Table 7.4, Table 7.5 and Table 7.6,
also includes improvements in the Expected Behavior column, particularly in areas where feed-
back pointed out the need for clarification. These changes ensure that Catmming does not merely
respond passively, but also actively promotes deeper self-regulation. Overall, the final version
of the test tables presents a more accurate, theory-driven, and pedagogically robust framework
for evaluating the chatbot’s comprehensive support of all subprocesses in Zimmerman’s Self-
Regulated Learning cycle.

7.6.2
Context Mapping and Retrieval Evaluation
To evaluate the chatbot’s ability to correctly retrieve relevant information and select the most
appropriate context from the ChromaDB-based retrieval system, each SRL-oriented test case was
mapped to a single optimal context and an expected type of retrieved content. This ensures that
both the retrieval module, responsible for locating the most relevant embedded chunks from the
Moodle data, and the context selection logic, which dynamically decides which specific source to
query (quizzes, forums, student progress, course structure, etc.) are adequately tested.
Given that the chatbot can only operate with one additional context at a time, this mapping
plays a critical role in validating whether it can identify and use the most relevant source of infor-
mation for each self-regulated learning scenario. In doing so, it also reflects how well the system
interprets the intent behind a student query and aligns it with the corresponding data source.
Table 7.8 presents this mapping in detail. For each test case, the table identifies the chosen
context, justifies its selection based on the nature of the query and the underlying SRL subprocess,
and describes the expected content that should be retrieved to support the student’s self-regulation
needs.

## Página PDF 128

Evaluation
114
7.7
Logging System Structure
To evaluate the chatbot’s ability to support Self-Regulated Learning (SRL), a structured logging
system was implemented. Each log entry captures the full interaction pipeline for a single test
case, including user inputs, system outputs, processing metadata, and timing information. This
architecture ensures both technical transparency and pedagogical traceability, enabling rigorous
analysis of how effectively each component of the RAG agent’s user query flow supports specific
SRL subprocesses across the different phases of learning.
Each log corresponds to the result of a functional SRL test (T1–T21) and includes a wide
array of parameters that characterize the complete workflow, from the user’s original query to the
final system response. This logging mechanism supports both functional validation (e.g., whether
the chatbot addressed the correct SRL subprocess) and technical debugging (e.g., performance
bottlenecks, failure points in context retrieval). By preserving all intermediate steps and inputs,
the log ensures the evaluation is both reproducible and interpretable by third-party reviewers.
7.7.1
User Interaction Data
Each log begins with basic user information, including the unique user identifier (user_id) and
the query submitted by the user (user_query). These fields serve to contextualize the interaction
and link it to the specific query being evaluated.
7.7.2
Processing Times
The logging system records the duration (in seconds) of each major processing step. The field
srl_refinement indicates the time taken to rephrase the initial query for SRL alignment.
srl_context_retrieval captures the duration of retrieving SRL-relevant context. The time
spent predicting the most suitable type of context is logged under context_type_prediction,
while additional_context_retrieval accounts for the time used to obtain supplementary
resources to help answer the question. The field final_answer_generation represents the
time used by the language model to generate the final response. Finally, the total time consumed
by the entire pipeline is recorded under total.
7.7.3
SRL-Related Data
Following the SRL refinement stage, the transformed query is stored in refined_query_for_srl.
The system also logs an array of retrieved SRL content chunks retrieved_srl_chunks. Each
chunk includes the textual content, a relevance score, and detailed metadata. These fields help
trace how specific content informed the chatbot’s final response, as well as assess whether the
chatbot accurately identified the subprocesses inherent in the user’s query.

[Vista original de la página 128](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-128.png)

![Página PDF 128, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-128.png)


<!-- PDF page 129 -->

## Página PDF 129

7.7 Logging System Structure
115
7.7.4
Additional Context Retrieval
In addition to SRL-specific content, the system may retrieve additional contextual chunks, retrieved_
additional_chunks, such as forums, quizzes, course content or user progress. Each of these
chunks is logged with its content, relevance score, and metadata. The predicted type of context,
(predicted_context_type), is also recorded to indicate the LLM chosen context category
(e.g., “User-Forum”, “User-Quiz”, “Course-Content”, “User-Progress”), or fallback response.
7.7.5
Final Output and Document IDs
The final answer generated by the model is recorded under final_llm_answer. To enhance
transparency and reproducibility, the system also logs the IDs of all retrieved documents, retrieved_
document_ids_summary, grouped by category: SRL content (srl) and additional contents
(user-forum, user-quiz, course-content or user-progress).
7.7.6
Log Metadata
Each log entry is uniquely identified by a log_id. It also includes information about the language
model deployed (model_used) (e.g., llama3.1:8b, gemma3:12b) and two timestamps mark-
ing the beginning and end of the log entry in Coordinated Universal Time (timestamp_start_utc
and timestamp_end_utc).
7.7.7
Conclusion
Altogether, this logging schema enables detailed monitoring, analysis, and validation of each test
case. It ensures reproducibility of results and supports systematic evaluation aligned with the
literature on RAG evaluation, facilitating both component-wise and end-to-end assessment of the
chatbot’s support for SRL.

## Página PDF 130

Chapter 8
Results and Discussion
8.1
Introduction
This chapter presents and analyses the results obtained from the structured evaluation process de-
fined in Chapter 7. The primary aim is to assess the chatbot’s ability to support the subprocesses of
Self-Regulated Learning (SRL) as defined by Zimmerman and Moylan’s framework. A total of 21
SRL-aligned test cases (T1–T21) were executed under controlled conditions using two different
large language models, Llama 3.1 (8B) and Gemma 3 (12B), and two distinct prompt formula-
tions, enabling a comparative study of how model scale and prompt engineering affect system
performance.
Thanks to the structured logging system described in Section 7.7, each test execution gener-
ated a comprehensive record of the full interaction pipeline, including the refined SRL-aligned
query, the types and sources of retrieved context, intermediate timings, the final answer, and all
supporting metadata. This infrastructure ensured not only reproducibility, but also the ability to
trace how specific technical choices influenced the functional success or failure of the chatbot in
addressing the target SRL subprocess.
The results of the comprehensive test suite are presented, structured by model (Llama 3.1 and
Gemma 3) and prompt version, followed by an analysis across three key dimensions.
First, the empirical findings are detailed, outlining the chatbot’s performance across the SRL-
aligned test cases, with particular attention to both quantitative metrics, such as success rates,
and computational efficiency, and qualitative observations, including the system’s adaptability to
distinct SRL subprocesses and its consistency in handling varied queries.
Building upon these results, the discussion interprets their implications, elucidating why cer-
tain configurations succeeded or failed in addressing specific requirements. This analysis consid-
ers factors such as model scale, prompt formulation, and system retrieval, providing insights into
the underlying mechanisms that drive effective SRL support.
Finally, the chapter synthesizes these findings into a broader reflection on the system’s align-
ment with its conceptual framework, assessing the extent to which the empirical outcomes validate,

8.2 Test Execution
117
challenge, or refine the original research proposal. By integrating results, interpretation, and con-
clusions, this chapter not only evaluates the chatbot’s performance but also contributes to a deeper
understanding of how conversational AI can be designed to foster self-regulated learning.
8.2
Test Execution
The tests were organized into four sets of 21 structured SRL queries, as defined in Section 8. Each
set combined a specific language model, either Llama 3.1 (8B) or Gemma 3 (12B), with one of
two system prompt versions: the initial baseline or the refined prompt described in Section 6.3.5.
This matrix allowed for a controlled comparison of model and prompt influence across the same
SRL-aligned scenarios.
Each set simulated a realistic student progression through Zimmerman’s three-phase SRL
model, executing the test prompts (T1–T21) in a fixed sequence for a single synthetic student
profile. This sequential execution preserved conversational continuity by maintaining the two
most recent user–assistant message pairs as context, enabling evaluation of how well the chatbot
adapted over time and leveraged dialogue history for more coherent and personalized responses.
Execution was fully automated using a custom Python script that emulated the application’s
runtime environment via a mocked Streamlit session state. The script cleared previous conversa-
tion history at the start of each test set, systematically invoked the query_rag() function per
prompt, and stored the interaction using save_conversation_history(). Internal logging
mechanisms captured key metadata for retrieval evaluation and qualitative analysis.
Although large language models exhibit some variability in output, repeated runs during vali-
dation confirmed that core behaviors, such as SRL subprocess alignment and context selection, re-
mained consistent. Minor differences in phrasing did not affect the system’s intended pedagogical
behavior, supporting the reliability of the recorded outputs as representative of each configuration.
8.3
Functional Evaluation Results
8.3.1
Additional Context Prediction
This section presents the results of evaluating the system’s ability to predict the most appropriate
context type for responding to student queries, focusing specifically on the context selection com-
ponent of the pipeline. A subsequent analysis (Section 8.4.2) will assess whether the similarity
search step successfully retrieved relevant documents based on these predictions.
The context selection component was tested using both LLMs with the updated prompt respon-
sible for determining which type of contextual data (e.g. course structure, user quiz history, forum
participation) should be retrieved. Crucially, while the main system prompts differed between the
four experimental configurations, the context selection prompt remained identical across all runs.
This deliberate consistency isolates the influence of the underlying model (Llama vs. Gemma) on
context prediction accuracy, eliminating confounding variables introduced by prompt variations.

[Vista original de la página 131](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-131.png)

![Página PDF 131, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-131.png)


<!-- PDF page 132 -->

## Página PDF 132

Results and Discussion
118
Each test case had a predefined expected context type, as described in Table 7.8. For each
model, the predicted context was compared against the expected one, and the outcome was cate-
gorized as follows:
• Correct: The predicted context exactly matches the expected one, which was manually
selected as the most pedagogically appropriate source of information.
• Valid: The predicted context does not match the expected one, but still contains information
that is reasonably relevant and pedagogically useful for answering the query. For example,
when a student asks about the goals of a quiz, the ideal context might be the quiz activity
itself, where these goals are explicitly defined. However, if the system instead selects the
general course context, which lists all activities, including quizzes, along with brief descrip-
tions, it still includes the quiz in question and can provide a reasonably helpful response.
This category acknowledges the flexibility and potential overlap between contexts.
• Incorrect: The selected context is not pedagogically useful or relevant to the query. This
includes two undesirable cases: when the fallback General-Context is selected, which
means no specific documents are retrieved and the model relies solely on its internal knowl-
edge, offering no personalization; and when a specific but unrelated context is chosen (e.g.,
retrieving quiz content in response to a query about forum participation). In the second
case, while some documents are retrieved, they are not aligned with the student’s query and
provide little to no pedagogical value. Both outcomes limit the chatbot’s ability to deliver
grounded, personalized support aligned with SRL processes.

Gemma 3 (12B) showed superior performance with 71.4% correct predictions (15/21) and
only one 4.8% incorrect predictions (1/21). Notably, the larger model completely avoided the
General-Context fallback mechanism across all test cases. While most predictions were accurate,
the model produced one clear error in T12, predicting User-Forum-Context for a quiz motivation
related query. In the other 23.8% of mismatches (5/21), Gemma selected valid alternative contexts
that could still support the query, such as choosing course context for progress-related questions.
8.3.1.2
Discussion
The results clearly demonstrate that model capacity plays a decisive role in the accuracy and
reliability of context type prediction, an important step for this approach of delivering grounded
and personalized SRL support. Gemma 3 (12B) outperformed Llama 3.1 (8B) across all metrics,
achieving a notably higher rate of correct predictions (71.4% vs. 57.1%) and completely avoiding
the undesirable fallback to General-Context, which Llama defaulted to in nearly a quarter of test
cases.
This consistent avoidance of fallback predictions is particularly significant. When the sys-
tem fails to retrieve a specific context and instead selects the general fallback, it cannot supply

[Vista original de la página 133](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-133.png)

![Página PDF 133, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-133.png)


<!-- PDF page 134 -->

## Página PDF 134

Results and Discussion
120
tailored support based on user activity, or course data, undermining the principles of SRL guid-
ance. Gemma’s ability to identify specific, relevant contexts in 20 of the 21 test cases suggests
a high level of contextual understanding and instruction-following, enabling robust grounding of
responses.
In contrast, Llama 3.1 showed difficulties in mapping user queries to the appropriate context
format, especially for questions involving motivational or quiz-related dimensions. Although some
misclassifications were considered valid alternatives, the model failed five times, each resulting in
a fallback response. These limitations likely reflect not only its smaller parameter size but also
its reduced capacity to interpret nuanced academic queries and in aligning them with the expected
context format.
Interestingly, model performance was not strictly proportional to scale in every case. The
anomaly in test T12 illustrates this well, since Gemma predicted User-Forum-Context for a mo-
tivation related quiz question, which consists of a clearly incorrect choice, as forum participation
is irrelevant for assessing quiz-related self-regulation. In a separate run of the same test, with an
identical component configuration, Gemma still predicted incorrectly, choosing User-Progress-
Context, although this choice would at least provide supporting data relevant to the learner’s gen-
eral engagement. Notably, Llama 3.1, despite its lower overall accuracy, correctly predicted User-
Quiz-Context for this particular case. This divergence highlights that even larger models can be
inconsistent on edge cases and that prediction quality may depend on the nature of the query rather
than model size alone.
Nonetheless, the overall findings validate a key design assumption of the proposed architec-
ture: that large language models, particularly when paired with a stable and well-engineered
prompt, can reliably infer the appropriate context type needed to ground pedagogical support.
The results from Gemma 3 (12B), with 95.2% of predictions being either correct or valid, confirm
the technical viability of this approach and justify the continued use of automated context selec-
tion in SRL-supportive systems in order to optimize retrieval by filtering the contents into multiple
smaller collections.
While perfect accuracy is impossible to achieve, model selection is critical for this solution.
To further enhance robustness, targeted improvements in motivational query handling should be
prioritized, potentially through prompt engineering that better distinguishes between similar yet
distinct Moodle data types.
8.3.2
Query Refinement
The query refinement task aimed to evaluate whether LLMs can rephrase original student queries
in ways that better align with the subprocesses defined in [106]’s SRL model. To test this, both
Llama 3.1 and Gemma 3 were prompted to automatically reformulate each original query (i.e.,
plausible questions a student might ask the chatbot). These refined versions were generated by
the LLMs themselves following the prompt presented in Section 6.3.3.2. The goal was to assess
each model’s ability to enhance the pedagogical clarity and SRL alignment of student interactions.

## Página PDF 135

8.3 Functional Evaluation Results
121
This helps determine whether LLMs can support students in better articulating their self-regulation
needs.
An analysis of the refined queries generated by each model reveals significant variation in
both linguistic quality and pedagogical alignment with self-regulated learning (SRL) principles.
Tables 8.3, 8.4, and 8.5 present a comparative evaluation of the reformulations across all test
cases, produced by the LLMs under identical prompting conditions and assessed via a standardized
rubric.
To rigorously evaluate SRL alignment, queries were scored on a five-point Likert scale de-
signed to prioritize fidelity to relevant subprocesses for each test scenario (e.g., Goal Orientation
for T5). The scoring criteria were defined as follows:
• 1 (Fully Misaligned): No relevant SRL content added.
• 2 (Misaligned): Mentions SRL subprocesses, but they do not align with student context or
are not completely clear.
• 3 (Partially Aligned): Addresses some expected subprocesses superficially and can include
off-topic elements.
• 4 (Mostly Aligned): Focuses on expected subprocesses but includes minor deviations.
• 5 (Fully Aligned): Directly and cohesively targets all expected subprocesses.
This scale ensures that incidental SRL content (e.g., discussing Strategic Planning when Goal
Orientation is relevant) is systematically penalized, while precise alignment is rewarded. By re-
solving ambiguity in borderline cases, it enables granular comparison between models’ perfor-
mance.

## Página PDF 139

8.3 Functional Evaluation Results
125
The comparative analysis of query refinement performance between Llama3.1 and Gemma 3 re-
vealed distinct patterns in SRL alignment. Gemma 3 achieved fully aligned responses (score 5) in
33.3% of cases (7 out of 21 test queries), substantially outperforming Llama3.1’s 4.8% rate (1/21).
Conversely, Llama3.1 produced more misaligned responses, with 42.8% scoring between 1 and 2
(9/21) compared to Gemma 3’s 4.8% (1/21). Notably, Gemma 3 avoided fully misaligned outputs
entirely (0% of score 1), while Llama3.1 generated two such instances of it (9.5%).
Significant differences in how each model processed the refinement task were identified. No-
tably, Llama3.1 frequently deviated from prompt instructions by including explanatory introduc-
tions before presenting the refined query, rather than providing only the reformulated sentence as
requested. This behavior occurred in 17 of 21 test cases (81%), often prefacing responses with
phrases like "Reformulação da pergunta para facilitar..." or "A pergunta reformulada seria:". In
contrast, Gemma 3 consistently adhered to the instruction format, delivering the refined query
directly all cases.
Key observations emerged regarding model capabilities. Both systems demonstrated stronger
performance when addressing structured SRL subprocesses like Goal Setting (T5-T6) and Time
Management (T7-T8), where Gemma 3 particularly excelled with five fully aligned responses.
However, performance declined for nuanced subprocesses - Causal Attribution (T20) and Interest
Incentives (T12) proved challenging for both models, with neither achieving full alignment in
these cases. Gemma 3 maintained more consistent performance across test cases, with 71.4% of
responses scoring 3 or higher (15/21) versus Llama3.1’s 57.1% (12/21).
Common behavioral patterns emerged despite these differences. Both systems occasionally
overgeneralized SRL concepts and struggled with contextual continuity in multi-turn interactions.
The shared tendency toward broader SRL terminology rather than targeted subprocess alignment
was particularly evident in complex metacognitive scenarios. Llama3.1’s additional explanatory
text, while not explicitly requested, sometimes introduced relevant SRL concepts that were never-
theless misaligned with the specific subprocess being assessed.
8.3.2.2
Discussion
The experimental findings confirm that the system’s bifurcated query refinement architecture, dis-
tinguishing between metacognitive challenges and general SRL strategy queries, was especially
effective when paired with more instruction, compliant language models. The prompt logic explic-
itly guided models to reformulate queries using educational vocabulary aligned with Zimmerman’s
SRL framework, with output constrained to a single-sentence format optimized for vector-based
retrieval. These design choices proved crucial in differentiating model behavior and retrieval po-
tential.
Gemma 3’s superior performance in both alignment and formatting compliance highlights the
advantage of larger, instruction-tuned models in this context. Its consistent adherence to the single-
sentence constraint and precise use of SRL-aligned vocabulary directly supports the system’s goal:
to transform informal student queries into effective, pedagogically grounded search statements. In
contrast, Llama3.1’s frequent insertion of explanatory introductions violated prompt instructions

[Vista original de la página 139](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-139.png)

![Página PDF 139, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-139.png)


<!-- PDF page 140 -->

## Página PDF 140

Results and Discussion
126
and degraded downstream performance. These unnecessary tokens dilute the semantic core of
the query, weaken the embedding representation, and ultimately reduce retrieval accuracy. In
vector-based search pipelines, even small deviations from the intended format can introduce noise
that impairs the matching of queries to relevant SRL literature, especially when precision at the
conceptual level is critical.
A key insight is that performance was not solely dependent on language fluency or model
size but also on the model’s ability to interpret prompt logic and incorporate precise vocabulary.
Gemma 3’s reformulations reflected closer alignment with the intended subprocess in most SRL
categories, especially those involving structured strategies like goal setting or time management.
However, both models struggled with queries targeting more abstract or infrequent subprocesses
such as causal attribution or interest incentives, suggesting a limitation in how well general models
grasp nuanced pedagogical distinctions.
Interestingly, even with correctly formatted conversational history, both models misinterpreted
context in 38% of the cases. This suggests that the current approach to include the most recent
conversation history may be counterproductive for search-focused refinement. For SRL query re-
formulation, where precise conceptual mapping is critical, relying on stateless input or selectively
extracting relevant past turns may yield more accurate results in a future iteration.
Finally, the results point toward several paths for system improvement. The inclusion of un-
derrepresented subprocesses could be reinforced via prompt augmentation with more targeted ex-
amples, through fine-tuning on SRL-aligned training data or by providing specialized few-shot
demonstrations. Additionally, implementing a lightweight post-processing validation layer to en-
force format compliance could increase system robustness before query submission to the retrieval
component.
In summary, the system architecture effectively channels powerful models like Gemma 3 to-
ward educationally meaningful reformulations, validating the value of prompt-engineered query
refinement. While limitations remain, particularly in handling conversational context and abstract
SRL constructs, the findings support further development of this approach as a practical bridge
between student questions and the retrieval of relevant self-regulated learning related literature.
8.3.3
SRL Support Analysis
This section presents the results of functional tests designed to evaluate whether each model
Llama3.1 (8B) and Gemma 3 (12B) with the newest prompt can support learners across all sub-
processes of Zimmerman’s SRL cycle: Forethought, Performance, and Self-Reflection. The eval-
uation consists of the results of the 21 tests (T1–T21), each aligned with one or more SRL subpro-
cesses which are presented in Table 8.6, Table 8.7 and Table 8.8 in comparison with the expected
behavior presented in Tables 7.4, 7.5 and 7.6.

Across the 21 SRL-focused tests, Gemma 3 (12B) consistently aligned more closely with expected
behaviors than Llama 3.1 (8B). Gemma demonstrated a stronger ability to personalize feedback,
connect student actions to self-regulated learning subprocesses, and use course-specific data effec-
tively. In contrast, Llama often relied on generic strategies, lacked follow-up prompts, and occa-
sionally hallucinated data. While both models showed competence in general SRL support, only
Gemma consistently provided metacognitive prompts, motivational strategies, and task-specific

Results and Discussion
130
guidance tailored to individual student inputs.
The subsections below reflects on the observed behaviors of the tested models, Llama 3.1 (8B)
and Gemma 3 (12B), through the lens of [106]’s three-phase model of self-regulated learning:
Forethought, Performance, and Self-Reflection. Each phase is examined with reference to the
subprocesses evaluated in the 21 formal test cases (T1–T21), providing a comprehensive view of
how the chatbot versions supported or failed to support each aspect of SRL. Examples from the
test set are used throughout to illustrate differences in performance.
8.3.3.2
Forethought Phase
The Forethought phase encompasses Task Analysis and Self-Motivation Beliefs. In this phase,
Gemma consistently delivered responses that showed pedagogical awareness of SRL goals, while
Llama’s support often lacked specificity, alignment with the subprocesses, or motivational fram-
ing.
For instance, in T1 (Strategic Planning), Llama offered generic advice on time use, while
Gemma provided a personalized plan based on the student’s current module status and progress,
breaking it into manageable study steps. Similarly, T2 (Goal Setting) showed that Llama struggled
to guide students in setting measurable learning goals, whereas Gemma helped the student identify
concrete milestones related to course activities.
Gemma also outperformed in supporting Self-Motivation Beliefs. In T3 (Outcome Expecta-
tions), it clearly articulated realistic academic outcomes tied to course engagement, while Llama
failed to meaningfully connect effort to consequences. In T4 (Task Interest/Value), Gemma re-
framed the student’s disinterest by highlighting the personal and academic benefits of a challenge
activity, contrasting with Llama’s vague encouragement. This pattern repeated in T5 and T6 (Goal
Orientation), where only Gemma helped the student focus on learning for the sake of understand-
ing and personal growth rather than simply trying to avoid failure.
In the hybrid case of T7 (Self-Efficacy / Time Management), both models attempted to of-
fer encouragement, but Gemma did so while proposing specific time-blocking techniques and
chunking strategies, reflecting a stronger alignment with both Self-Control and Self-Motivation
processes. Llama, on the other hand, repeated abstract affirmations without actionable follow-up.
8.3.3.3
Performance Phase
The Performance phase includes Self-Control (T10–T15) and Self-Observation (T16–T17). Gemma
consistently exhibited better task awareness, clearer feedback loops, and more structured guidance
than Llama, particularly in tasks requiring nuanced metacognitive support.
In T10 (Task Strategies), Llama offered vague advice, whereas Gemma provided a more struc-
tured, step-by-step approach tailored to the quiz scenario, including how to locate relevant docu-
mentation and tackle each part of the task. T11 (Self-Instruction), however, revealed a common
limitation in both models: neither explicitly encouraged the use of verbal or mental cues to guide

[Vista original de la página 144](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-144.png)

![Página PDF 144, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-144.png)


<!-- PDF page 145 -->

## Página PDF 145

8.3 Functional Evaluation Results
131
students during task execution. While both suggested helpful metacognitive strategies, like break-
ing tasks into smaller steps or using the Pomodoro technique, they fell short of supporting this
specific subprocess as defined by Zimmerman and Moylan.
T12 (Interest Incentives / Self-Consequences) highlighted one of the clearest contrasts: Llama
merely encouraged the student to keep going, whereas Gemma explored the student’s intrinsic val-
ues, suggested a self-reward plan, and connected completion to personal progress, thus addressing
both motivation and behavior regulation.
Gemma also successfully handled T13 (Imagery), helping the student visualize and structure
the task through analogies and progressive diagrams, along with reflective prompts to support
comprehension and monitoring. Llama also addressed this subprocess, providing a solid analogy
and recommending tools for visual representation, though without the deeper reflective scaffolding
Gemma provided.
In T14 (Environmental Structuring), both models addressed how to minimize distractions,
with Llama suggesting general strategies like turning off notifications and finding a quiet place.
However, Gemma provided a more comprehensive response, integrating both environmental and
motivational strategies, such as organizing the space, using Pomodoro cycles, journaling, and
encouraging reflection and peer support.
In T15 (Help-Seeking), both models encouraged the student to seek clarification through fo-
rums or instructors, framing help-seeking as an appropriate and effective strategy. Llama em-
phasized the importance of clearly communicating doubts and aligning help requests with learn-
ing goals, while Gemma added a reflective dimension by prompting the student to analyze prior
choices, journal uncertainties, and view help-seeking as part of a metacognitive process. Although
Gemma’s approach was slightly more comprehensive, both models demonstrated solid support for
this subprocess.
For Self-Observation, Gemma again excelled. In T16 (Self-Recording), it suggested concrete
ways to track learning progress, such as journals or completion logs, while Llama only repeated
the importance of keeping track without practical methods. In T17 (Metacognitive Monitoring),
Gemma addressed the importance of knowing what is slowing the student down and suggested
that the student check their progress against the course plan, actively prompting reflection.
8.3.3.4
Self-Reflection Phase
The Self-Reflection phase comprises Self-Judgment (T18–T20) and Self-Reaction (T21). This
phase is essential for adaptive learning, as it involves evaluating one’s performance and attributing
causes to success or failure.
In T18 (Task Strategies / Self-Evaluation), Gemma was able to help the student understand
why a specific quiz question was wrong, connect the error to a misunderstood concept, and propose
revised strategies. Llama only proposed general strategies without helping the student evaluate
their mistake, failing the Self-Judgment criteria.
In T19 (Self-Evaluation), Gemma offered a detailed analysis of the student’s challenge re-
sponses, highlighting specific strengths like logical thinking and adaptability, and linked them to

[Vista original de la página 145](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-145.png)

![Página PDF 145, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-145.png)


<!-- PDF page 146 -->

## Página PDF 146

Results and Discussion
132
course competencies. It encouraged self-reflection and targeted self-improvement. Llama, how-
ever, provided a more generic interpretation, suggesting general improvements and further self-
assessment without the depth or personalized feedback of Gemma’s response. This difference
highlights Gemma’s more detailed and tailored approach to fostering self-evaluation and reflec-
tion.
In T20 (Causal Attribution), Gemma effectively supported causal attribution by providing de-
tailed analysis of how forum participation aids learning, connecting specific behaviors like ex-
pressing doubts and receiving feedback to self-regulated learning strategies with concrete exam-
ples. It suggested actionable steps like initiating topics and reflecting on feedback quality. Llama
provided only general explanations of forum benefits without specific examples or detailed con-
nections to self-regulated learning, lacking the depth needed for effective causal attribution sup-
port.
Finally, in T21 (Self-Satisfaction / Adaptive vs Defensive Reactions), Gemma effectively sup-
ported this subprocess through detailed, empathetic responses that encouraged strategic reflection
and offered concrete improvement suggestions like task breakdown and module review. This
approach fostered adaptive rather than defensive reactions. Llama, while normalizing slower
progress and encouraging reflection, provided only general advice without specific, actionable
strategies needed to help students adapt their learning approaches and focus on self-satisfaction.
8.3.3.5
Discussion
To complement the detailed analysis provided in the previous subsections, Table 8.9 consolidates
the support quality assessment for each subprocess within Zimmerman’s Self-Regulated Learning
cycle. This comprehensive overview enables a systematic comparison of how Llama 3.1 (8B)
and Gemma 3 (12B) perform across all SRL subprocesses, providing a quantitative framework for
understanding their respective pedagogical capabilities and coverage.
The assessment employs a three-point Likert scale to rate each model’s support quality for
individual subprocesses:
• Rating 1 (Minimal Support): Indicates responses that provide only generic advice with-
out subprocess-specific guidance. These responses typically lack personalization, concrete
strategies, or clear alignment with SRL principles. The model may acknowledge the gen-
eral topic but fails to deliver targeted support that would effectively facilitate the specific
self-regulatory process.
• Rating 2 (Partial Support): Represents responses that address some aspects of the subpro-
cess but demonstrate limitations in depth, specificity, or complete alignment with SRL re-
quirements. While these responses show awareness of the subprocess, they may lack the
comprehensive guidance or personalized strategies necessary for optimal learning support.
• Rating 3 (Strong Support): Characterizes responses that provide comprehensive, person-
alized guidance directly targeting the subprocess with concrete strategies and clear SRL

[Vista original de la página 146](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-146.png)

![Página PDF 146, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-146.png)

alignment. These responses demonstrate deep understanding of the subprocess’s role within
the broader learning cycle and offer actionable, contextualized support that effectively facil-
itates self-regulated learning.
This rating system was not adapted from existing scales but was purposefully developed to
meet the specific analytical needs of this study. While subjective in nature, it was designed to re-
flect an expert-informed perspective, grounded on the SRL subprocesses defined in [106]’s frame-
work. This approach provides a structured and replicable method for assessing response quality,
tailored to the specific goals and context of this functional evaluation. This rating system enables
a granular analysis of each model’s pedagogical effectiveness, revealing patterns in their respec-
tive strengths and limitations across the three phases of Self-Regulated Learning: Forethought,
Performance, and Self-Reflection.

The consolidated ratings reveal a stark performance difference: Gemma 3 achieved strong
support (Rating 3) on 19 out of 20 subprocesses, while Llama 3.1 showed minimal support (Rating
1) on 12 out of 20 subprocesses. Notably, both models struggled equally with Self-Instruction
(Rating 1), reflecting a shared limitation in supporting verbal and mental cue strategies during task
execution, although the SRL data document presented contains reference to this behavior it failed
to be identified and applied by the chatbots.
Llama 3.1 (8B) frequently relied on static templates and offered advice without using context.
Even when it correctly named key SRL subprocesses, it rarely connected them to the student’s
actual learning activities or offered meaningful follow-up prompts. Its responses lacked person-
alization, diagnostic questioning, and engagement with the course structure. Moreover, Llama

[Vista original de la página 147](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-147.png)

![Página PDF 147, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-147.png)


<!-- PDF page 148 -->

## Página PDF 148

Results and Discussion
134
often generated messages with awkward Brazilian expressions or grammatically incorrect Euro-
pean Portuguese, which occasionally hindered comprehension and reduced the authenticity of the
feedback.
In contrast, Gemma 3 (12B) demonstrated dynamic and pedagogically aligned behavior across
all three SRL phases. It adapted its responses based on context, showed awareness of course
structure and student progress, and addressed both cognitive and emotional dimensions of learn-
ing. A critical differentiator was Gemma’s ability to transform abstract learning challenges into
actionable strategies, evident in its consistent Rating 3 performance in both Task Analysis and
Self-Motivation Beliefs. It frequently offered practical strategies, asked clarifying questions, and
encouraged reflection. Importantly, Gemma displayed genuine dialogic behaviors: it sought to
understand the student’s needs, adjusted tone and content accordingly, and scaffolded both moti-
vational and self-regulatory practice.
Additionally, Gemma appeared to leverage the accumulated conversation history more effec-
tively than Llama. It referred back to earlier messages in a coherent and contextually appropriate
manner, maintaining a sense of dialogue continuity that supported more natural and personalized
interaction. This was particularly evident in responses such as T2, where Gemma built on its prior
reply (T1) to reinforce earlier advice or refine its recommendations. In contrast, Llama 3.1 often
ignored conversational history, responding as if each prompt were isolated, which led to repetitive
advice, missed references to prior messages, and reduced pedagogical coherence across multi-turn
interactions.
Despite these strengths, both models exhibited a notable limitation when responding to queries
about student progress or course timelines. Specifically, in tests like T16 (“What progress have I
completed in this course so far?”) and T21 (“I feel like I’m not progressing as fast as others. Should
I be worried?”), the chatbot failed to identify the learner’s current phase or prioritize completed
and relevant activities. Instead, it selected arbitrary modules, resulting in inaccurate feedback.
This issue stems not from the models themselves, but from limitations in the system’s retrieval
mechanism, which lacks access to a reliable indicator of the student’s current status or pacing
relative to Moodle timeline affecting final answer generation. While this does not directly impact
the chatbot’s SRL subprocess alignment in these cases, it does undermine its capacity to deliver
the most relevant personalized guidance.
Gemma also produced significantly fewer grammatical errors in European Portuguese and ap-
peared to better understand language nuances. This linguistic advantage likely contributed to the
more coherent and pedagogically relevant responses. The combination of better inference capabil-
ities and stronger adherence to system prompts suggests that Gemma’s superior performance may
also stem from its larger model size, which enables deeper understanding, better contextualization,
and more effective integration of instructional cues.
This summary underscores that Gemma 3 provides broader and deeper support for self-regulated
learning than Llama 3.1. Its responses exhibit stronger alignment with Zimmerman’s theoretical
framework, particularly in scaffolding student thinking, integrating personal goals, and fostering
reflective behavior. Notably, Gemma 3 maintains conversational coherence by referencing earlier

[Vista original de la página 148](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-148.png)

![Página PDF 148, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-148.png)


<!-- PDF page 149 -->

## Página PDF 149

8.3 Functional Evaluation Results
135
exchanges, reinforcing connections across SRL phases and simulating a more natural tutor-student
dialogue. In contrast, Llama 3.1 behaves more like a general-purpose question-answering system,
with little sensitivity to the metacognitive or motivational dimensions essential for effective SRL
support. These findings suggest that Gemma 3 is a more promising foundation for implementing
an SRL-focused educational virtual assistant in asynchronous online learning environments.
8.3.4
Prompt Adherence and Hallucinations
Initial testing with the earlier version of the prototype described in Section 6.3.5 revealed signif-
icant issues with system prompt design that led to context confusion, inappropriate information
disclosure, and user identity misunderstanding. These problems highlighted the critical impor-
tance of careful prompt engineering for educational chatbots operating with student data.
8.3.4.1
Context Confusion and Information Disclosure
The earlier prompt version caused the model to inappropriately reference system-level information
in user-facing responses. For instance, in T14, the model explicitly mentioned "De acordo com o
contexto de SRL", exposing internal system terminology that should remain hidden from students.
Similarly, in T2, the model referenced o "contexto", as if the student had direct access to this
system-provided information. The model also incorrectly attributed automatically retrieved stu-
dent data to direct user submissions. In T5, it stated "No contexto do teste que submeteste", when
the student had not actually submitted anything, the context is retrieved from Moodle through the
system pipeline. This created confusion about data sources and potentially undermined student
trust.
8.3.4.2
Student Identity Misrecognition
Perhaps most critically, the earlier prompt led to systematic failures in recognizing the student’s
identity within the provided context. System prompt indicated the student’s ID. Not only that but
the model frequently confused different users or treated the student as a third party. In T13, the
model hallucinated another person’s identity: "o desafio metacognitivo do estudante com ID 1",
when discussing with the actual user who had ID 3. More problematically, the model recom-
mended that students seek help from themselves, failing to recognize they were already talking to
the target student. In T16, it suggested: "podes procurar o utilizador com ID 3", essentially telling
the student to find themselves for help. Similar confusion occurred in T17, where the model re-
ferred to "o utilizador com ID 3" as if they were a separate person who could provide insights to
the actual student they were addressing.
8.3.4.3
Technical Term Exposure
The earlier prompt also allowed inappropriate exposure of internal system terminology. In T18, the
model referenced technical grading labels directly: "a questão número 2 que classificaste como

[Vista original de la página 149](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-149.png)

![Página PDF 149, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-149.png)


<!-- PDF page 150 -->

## Página PDF 150

Results and Discussion
136
gradedwrong". The term represents internal system coding that should be translated into user-
friendly language rather than exposed directly to students.
8.3.4.4
Improvements in the Refined System Prompt
The final prompt version described in Section 6.3.5 successfully addressed these critical issues
through targeted structural and instructional changes. The removal of explicit section titles like
"Contexto de SRL" and "Contexto Adicional" eliminated the source of inappropriate system-level
references that were surfacing in user responses. These rigid headings were replaced with natural
contextual cues and descriptive language, preventing the model from exposing internal prompt
organization to students. Most importantly, the refined prompt included explicit instructions to
treat all data associated with the current user ID as belonging to the person being directly ad-
dressed. This clear directive resolved the identity confusion that led to third-person references
and recommendations for students to seek help from themselves. The prompt now unambiguously
established that retrieved forum posts, quiz answers, and metacognitive reflections belonged to
the conversational partner, ensuring consistent second-person communication. Additionally, the
final version adopted more flexible response patterns rather than rigid formatting requirements,
reducing the likelihood of technical terminology leakage while maintaining pedagogical appropri-
ateness.
These targeted modifications eliminated the systematic errors observed in the initial testing
while preserving the prompt’s core educational objectives and SRL alignment. This demonstrates
how prompt engineering not only affects linguistic tone but can fundamentally alter the model’s
capacity to preserve trust, contextual accuracy, and alignment with SRL principles.
8.4
Non-Functional Evaluation Results
To complement the functional evaluation of the chatbot’s capacity to support SRL subprocesses, a
non-functional evaluation was conducted to assess the performance of the system under different
configurations.
8.4.1
Computational Performance
Four test sets were executed using an automated pipeline, and the average processing time per
query was measured. The evaluation considers three sequential stages of the chatbot pipeline:
the self-regulated learning query refinement time, the context type prediction time, and the final
answer generation time.
The self-regulated learning query refinement time represents the duration spent analyzing
the user query and refining it to include terms relevant to SRL, in order to improve the retrieval of
documents from the self-regulated learning collection. The context type prediction time refers to
the time taken to classify the type of content being asked about and to select the appropriate context
(e.g., forum post, quiz, student progress) that best contains the information needed to answer the

[Vista original de la página 150](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-150.png)

![Página PDF 150, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-150.png)


<!-- PDF page 151 -->

## Página PDF 151

8.4 Non-Functional Evaluation Results
137
user’s question. Finally, the final answer generation time is the time required by the language
model to generate a complete response after all context has been retrieved.
8.4.1.1
Results
Table 8.10 presents the average duration (in seconds) for each stage and the total end-to-end re-
sponse time, from the moment the question is received by the system until the final answer is
returned to the user, across all 21 test queries per configuration.
