The performance analysis revealed significant timing differences across model configurations.
Using the old prompt formulation, Llama3.1 (8B) completed end-to-end processing in 214.37
seconds on average, while Gemma 3 (12B) required 765.50 seconds, approximately 3.6× longer.
The new prompt reduced total response times by 19.6% for Llama3.1 (to 172.48 seconds) and
4.7% for Gemma 3 (to 729.23 seconds), with these improvements coming exclusively from the
answer generation stage.
Component-level analysis showed stable performance in non-prompt-affected stages. For SRL
refinement, Llama3.1 averaged 38.53 seconds versus Gemma 3’s 173.06 seconds (4.5× slower),
with less than 1% variation between prompt versions. Context prediction times similarly remained
stable, with Llama3.1 averaging 19.14 seconds and Gemma 3 averaging 139.95 seconds across
both prompt versions (±2% variation).
The answer generation stage showed the most significant improvements from prompt opti-
mization, while remaining the dominant computational cost (61.5% of total time for Llama3.1,
60.9% for Gemma 3). The new prompt reduced answer generation latency by 19.6% for Llama3.1
(from 131.81 to 105.91 seconds) and 4.7% for Gemma 3 (from 466.31 to 444.26 seconds). These
improvements directly accounted for the total time reductions observed.
8.4.1.2
Discussion
The timing results demonstrate that prompt optimization specifically benefits the answer genera-
tion stage without affecting upstream components. Replacing the old prompt with a more concise
and structured version led to measurable improvements, reducing total processing time by 19.6%
for Llama3.1 (from 214.37s to 172.48s) and 4.7% for Gemma 3 (from 765.50s to 729.23s). The
more significant gains in the smaller model reinforce that prompt engineering can enhance both
responsiveness and quality, particularly when computational resources are constrained, a critical
consideration for real-time academic assistance systems.

[Vista original de la página 151](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-151.png)

![Página PDF 151, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-151.png)


<!-- PDF page 152 -->

## Página PDF 152

Results and Discussion
138
The stability of SRL refinement and context prediction times (±2% variation) confirms these
components operate independently of the answer generation prompt, as expected in the system
architecture. The inherent trade-offs between model size and performance become evident in
Gemma 3’s consistently slower processing (4.5× across all stages), highlighting that while larger
models offer superior linguistic capabilities, they do so at substantial latency costs. For time-
sensitive applications, these results suggest smaller models on capable hardware with optimized
prompts may provide the optimal balance.
These findings suggest that focused prompt engineering for generation stages yields mea-
surable benefits without requiring architectural changes. The consistent 60-61% time allocation
to answer generation clearly indicates where future optimizations could have most impact, par-
ticularly for deployments using larger models where even marginal improvements carry greater
absolute value. For practical implementation, this supports a strategy where prompt refinements
concentrate on the generation stage while model selection weighs both quality thresholds and la-
tency requirements, with parallel processing of previous components potentially mitigating the
throughput limitations of larger models like Gemma 3. Ultimately, the demonstrated improve-
ments validate that careful prompt engineering can meaningfully enhance system responsiveness
without compromising the quality advantages offered by more capable models.
For NF1-compliant deployments, the system requires hardware upgrades to meet the 30 sec-
ond response threshold. The current 4GB VRAM configuration should be replaced with GPU-
accelerated hardware featuring minimum 24GB memory (e.g., NVIDIA RTX 3090/4090) to prop-
erly accommodate medium-sized models. A three-stage optimization protocol could be imple-
mented: First, comprehensive prompt efficiency optimization across all pipeline components,
building on the demonstrated 19.6% possible latency reduction capabilities. Second, deploy-
ment of a hybrid architecture that strategically allocates models by computational demand, uti-
lizing Gemma 3 (12B) exclusively for answer generation while employing smaller models for
SRL refinement or context prediction. Third, application of hardware-level optimizations by us-
ing Gemma 3 4-bit quantized model version, which typically reduces memory requirements while
maintaining most of the original model accuracy [23]. This tiered approach would systemati-
cally address latency bottlenecks while preserving response quality, with projected performance
bringing most queries under the 30 second threshold when combined with parallel processing of
pipeline stages.
8.4.2
Document Retrieval
The retrieval system comprises two independent components: srl_context_retrieval and addi-
tional_context_retrieval. The former retrieves guidance chunks from a SRL document (PDF),
using a refined version of the user query, for the similarity search, and fixed-size segments (800
tokens). The latter retrieves course-related materials, such as forum discussions, quiz attempts,
and content descriptions from Moodle derived JSON files. These chunks are generated during
database population and vary in size and structure, with a soft limit of 800 tokens but usually
shorter due to preprocessing and segmentation logic applied.

[Vista original de la página 152](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-152.png)

![Página PDF 152, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-152.png)


<!-- PDF page 153 -->

## Página PDF 153

8.4 Non-Functional Evaluation Results
139
Each test scenario (T1–T21) was executed twice using two models (Llama 3.1 and Gemma 3),
each with two different system prompts. However, retrieval is unaffected by these prompt varia-
tions: both context selection and query refinement occur before the final answer generation stage,
and identical inputs lead to identical retrievals. Consequently, evaluating retrieval performance
once, under optimal conditions, provides sufficient insight. This evaluation focuses on the ad-
ditional_context_retrieval component for the most capable model, Gemma 3, which consistently
selected more accurate contexts and formulated better queries. In contrast, srl_context_retrieval
is excluded from metric-based evaluation because both models consistently produced answers
grounded in self-regulation concepts when using this source, suggesting acceptable reliability.
Following established RAG evaluation practices [29], two metrics are used to assess the quality
of the retrieved Moodle content: Hit Rate @5 and Precision @5. These metrics evaluate the
system’s ability to retrieve at least one relevant chunk (recall) and to avoid including irrelevant
content (precision), as presented in Section 2.1.9.4.
Hit Rate @5 evaluates whether at least one relevant document exists among the top-5 retrieved
chunks. For each test case, a binary score is assigned based on whether any chunk matches the
ground-truth content specified in Table 7.8. Since Gemma never selected the General-Context, it
never yields zero-retrieval scenarios. Precision @5 measures the proportion of relevant chunks in
the top-5 results, calculated only when Hit Rate @5 succeeds. A chunk is considered relevant if it
matches the expected content type (e.g., forum discussions for T15 or quiz descriptions for T6).
The evaluation proceeds in three phases. First, the predicted context type is verified against the
expected context using results from Section 8.3.1. Then, for cases with correct context selection
and available retrievals, Hit Rate @5 and Precision @5 are computed. This approach ensures
systematic coverage while excluding edge cases that do not involve actual chunk retrieval (e.g.,
incorrect contexts or fallback behaviors).
8.4.2.1
Results
The retrieval performance results for the additional_context_retrieval component using the Gemma
3 model are summarized in Table 8.11. The evaluation covers all 21 test cases, measuring both
Hit Rate @5 and Precision @5 based on alignment with the expected ground-truth content out-
lined in Table 7.8. These metrics allow us to assess the system’s ability to locate and prioritize
course-relevant materials when responding to student queries in realistic SRL scenarios.
The retrieval performance results demonstrate mixed effectiveness across the 21 test cases,
with notable variations in both Hit Rate @5 and Precision @5 metrics.
The system achieved an overall Hit Rate @5 of 81.0% (17 out of 21 test cases), indicating
that the correct context type was successfully retrieved within the top 5 results for the majority of
queries. The overall Precision @5 was 47.6%, suggesting that while relevant content was often
found, approximately half of the retrieved chunks were not directly relevant to the specific query
requirements.
User-Quiz-Context queries showed the strongest performance, with perfect hit rates across all
relevant test cases (T3, T4, T6, T9, T10, T12, T18, T19) and notably high precision scores for T3,

T4, T9, and T19 (0.8-1.0). This suggests the system excels at retrieving quiz-related content and

User-Progress-Context queries demonstrated variable performance. While T7 and T9 achieved
perfect scores (Hit Rate @5 = 1.0, Precision @5 = 1.0), other progress-related queries like T16
and T20 failed completely (0.0 for both metrics). T14, T17, and T21 showed successful retrieval
but with lower precision (0.2-0.6).
Course-Context queries exhibited the most inconsistent results. While T13 performed well
(Hit Rate @5 = 1.0, Precision @5 = 0.6), several course-context queries failed entirely, including
T5, T11, and T12 (0.0 for both metrics). T2, T8, T15, T18, and T21 showed successful retrieval
but with notably low precision (0.2-0.4).
User-Forum-Context queries showed perfect hit rates for T1 and T15, with T1 achieving high
precision (0.8) while T15 had moderate precision (0.4). However, T20 failed completely.
8.4.2.2
Discussion
The retrieval performance results reveal several important insights about the system’s capabilities
and limitations in supporting SRL scenarios. The complete failures observed in test cases T5,
T11, T12, T16, and T20 represent different categories of retrieval challenges. Cases T5 and T12
demonstrate upstream context selection errors where the previous system component incorrectly
identified the required context type, though in T5 the selected context was valid but retrieved
information remained useless for personalization. T11 and T20 show correct context identification
but retrieval of inappropriate content granularity, where the system found quiz-related content
instead of concrete examples for task orientation (T11) or general forum descriptions rather than
specific interaction patterns (T20). T16 represents a temporal limitation failure where correct

[Vista original de la página 154](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-154.png)

![Página PDF 154, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-154.png)


<!-- PDF page 155 -->

## Página PDF 155

8.4 Non-Functional Evaluation Results
141
context selection could not overcome the system’s inability to determine the student’s current
timeline position.
The pattern of high hit rates coupled with low precision scores (T2, T6, T8, T10, T15, T17,
T18, and T21) demonstrates successful context identification but poor content prioritization within
that context. This temporal contextualization challenge affects multiple test cases (T7, T12, T16,
T21), as the retrieval system cannot determine which week the student is currently in, preventing
retrieval of time-appropriate activities and personalized recommendations based on their current
learning phase.
The superior performance in User-Quiz-Context scenarios (T3, T4, T9, T19) reflects the sys-
tem’s strength in handling structured, user-specific data with well-defined formats and clear rela-
tionships. Conversely, Course-Context queries presented significant challenges, with inconsistent
performance between concrete technical content like MVC materials (T13) and abstract pedagog-
ical concepts (T5, T11, T12), where the lack of specific terms hindered relevant material retrieval.
Several technical improvements could address these limitations. The current vector-based
similarity search could be enhanced with hybrid retrieval strategies that combine semantic un-
derstanding with rule-based filtering and temporal constraints through a multi-stage pipeline. To
address the lack of temporal awareness, contextual metadata, such as timestamps reflecting student
progress, could be incorporated to enable temporal filters in similarity scoring. For pedagogical
content retrieval, implementing a compatible knowledge graph approach for course structure and
learning objectives could allow for explicit modeling of relationships between concepts, activities,
and learning outcomes, enabling more sophisticated retrieval based on educational dependencies.

## Página PDF 156

Chapter 9
Conclusions and Future Work
9.1
Introduction
This final chapter presents the conclusions drawn from the research conducted in this disserta-
tion and outlines potential directions for future work. It revisits the research aims and questions,
summarizes the key findings, and highlights the theoretical and practical contributions made. The
limitations of the study are then discussed, followed by actionable recommendations for future
research. The chapter concludes with a brief recapitulation of its main points.
The structure is as follows: Section 9.2 summarizes the key findings, while Section 9.3 details
the research contributions at both theoretical and practical levels. Section 9.4 presents the study’s
limitations, and Section 9.5 proposes avenues for future research. Section 9.6 closes the chapter
with final remarks.
9.2
Summary of Key Findings
This study aimed to investigate how an LLM-based chatbot could support the development of self-
regulated learning (SRL) in asynchronous higher education, using the Laboratório de Desenvolvi-
mento de Software course at Universidade Aberta as a case study. Specifically, it explored whether
a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) chatbot, grounded in Zimmerman and Moylan’s (2009)
SRL model, could deliver guidance across all three SRL phases and their subprocesses.
A key outcome of the project was the successful design and implementation of an end-to-end,
modular system that connects real-world course data to an interactive chatbot via similarity search
and natural language generation. This system consists of several interdependent components:
• Data Extraction and Structuring: The system begins by collecting data from Moodle
using a suite of API calls. These gather course metadata, enrolled users, quiz contents and
attempts, forum discussions, and module completion statuses. Special routines were added
to recover full forum threads and user-specific participation histories.
142

[Vista original de la página 156](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-156.png)

![Página PDF 156, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-156.png)


<!-- PDF page 157 -->

## Página PDF 157

9.2 Summary of Key Findings
143
• Preprocessing Pipeline: The raw JSON output from Moodle is transformed into seman-
tically structured files. Each entry contains a natural language summary (in Portuguese)
and a metadata field for traceability. HTML is cleaned, attachments preserved, and forum
hierarchies reconstructed. The data is then grouped by category and per student to support
personalization.
• Vector Embedding and Storage: The preprocessed content is divided into coherent chunks
and embedded using the Nomic-embed-text model. Each chunk is stored in ChromaDB,
with separate collections for course-wide materials, SRL theory PDFs, and user-specific
data (e.g., forums, quizzes, progress).Structured chat logs and raw files are stored, comple-
menting ChromaDB’s semantic retrieval capabilities.
• User Interaction and Retrieval: The chatbot interface is built with Streamlit, offering a
browser-accessible front-end. Upon receiving a user query, the system first refines it for
SRL alignment. A context prediction model selects which ChromaDB collection is most
relevant and retrieves the most semantically similar chunks. These are then combined with
the original query to form a prompt passed to an LLM running locally via Ollama (e.g.,
Llama 3.1 or Gemma 3).
• Response Generation and SRL Support: The LLM responds by integrating the user ques-
tion with retrieved content and SRL-aligned instructions. This enables the chatbot to provide
tailored support based on a student’s past activities, course progress, and reflective prompts.
The architecture ensures modularity and scalability while maintaining personalized guid-
ance capabilities.
In addition, the evaluation results explicitly addressed the three research questions introduced
in Chapter 1:
RQ1, which examined the chatbot’s capability to use Moodle course data to generate context-
aware responses, was clearly supported by the successful implementation and evaluation of the
retrieval system. Chapter 8 demonstrated that the chatbot consistently delivered accurate, con-
textually relevant responses, effectively leveraging personal history, forum interactions, quiz per-
formance, and course contents. The system’s modular architecture and retrieval pipeline enabled
consistent grounding in relevant materials, even in complex scenarios.
RQ2, focused on the alignment of chatbot responses with Zimmerman’s SRL subprocesses,
was thoroughly addressed through structured tests (T1–T21). The results confirmed robust align-
ment across nearly all subprocesses, particularly when using larger models (e.g., Gemma 3) in
combination with refined prompts. These improvements significantly enhanced the chatbot’s abil-
ity to follow Zimmerman’s framework and provide support across the full SRL cycle. A notable
exception was the subprocess of Self-Instruction, indicating an area requiring further improve-
ment due to its abstract nature, which made it harder for the chatbot to detect and respond to
appropriately in the absence of explicit cues.

[Vista original de la página 157](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-157.png)

![Página PDF 157, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-157.png)


<!-- PDF page 158 -->

## Página PDF 158

Conclusions and Future Work
144
Lastly, RQ3, which explored the chatbot’s potential in supporting learners’ reflection on
metacognitive challenges using simulated inputs, showed promising outcomes. This was par-
ticularly evident in tests T9 and T19, which used the simulated results of the metacognitive chal-
lenge “Autoconhecimento: Sou Programador?!” a quiz designed to prompt self-reflection on stu-
dents’ motivation, identity, and perceived fit with programming. In both cases, the chatbot was
able to interpret the student’s responses and generate personalized, pedagogically grounded feed-
back that encouraged deeper reflection. These results were made possible by the SRL context
retrieval pipeline, which always includes institutionally validated materials aligned with Zimmer-
man’s model and practical strategies for addressing metacognitive difficulties. The retrieval pro-
cess was guided by a prompt-based query refinement mechanism that detects metacognitive cues
such as difficulty, confidence, or motivational concerns, and reformulates user queries to retrieve
relevant feedback strategies. The retrieved content was based on chapters authored by SRL expert
Daniela Pedrosa, ensuring alignment with real pedagogical practices. Despite the use of simulated
learners, the chatbot consistently provided meaningful and context-aware responses, demonstrat-
ing strong potential to support reflective thinking and metacognitive development in asynchronous
online learning environments.
Overall, the modular and scalable architecture, built around the foundations of LangChain
agents and the ReAct pattern, facilitated comprehensive SRL support through context-aware and
adaptive interactions. In sum, the evaluation results robustly answer the research questions, demon-
strating that an LLM-based chatbot grounded in course-specific data can effectively support self-
regulated learning processes, offer coherent alignment with Zimmerman’s theoretical framework,
and successfully foster reflective engagement with metacognitive challenges.
9.3
Research Contributions
9.3.1
Theoretical Contributions
This research contributes to the academic discourse on integrating SRL theory with AI-driven
educational tools. By [106]’s SRL model as a foundational framework, the study advances our un-
derstanding of how each phase and subprocess can be applied in natural language interactions. The
methodology of mapping user queries to SRL subprocesses and testing them against real-world
chatbot behavior represents a novel contribution to both SRL assessment and AI-in-education re-
search.
Additionally, this study addresses gaps identified in Table 2.4, notably the lack of compre-
hensive SRL support across all phases and the underexplored integration of adaptive feedback
features. While sentiment analysis was out of scope for this implementation, the work lays con-
ceptual groundwork for future extensions.

## Página PDF 159

9.4 Study Limitations
145
9.3.2
Practical Contributions
From a practical standpoint, the system was developed specifically for students of the e-SimProgramming
environment within Universidade Aberta. The chatbot provides personalized SRL support by
leveraging structured Moodle data from that courses, thereby enabling students to plan, moni-
tor, and reflect more effectively on their learning activities. Through its integration with course
content, forum discussions, quizzes, and progress tracking, the chatbot addresses the unique chal-
lenges of asynchronous online learning, including lack of immediate feedback and reduced student
engagement.
Compared to state-of-the-art approaches that often support only isolated SRL subprocesses,
this study introduces a more holistic solution by combining prompt engineering, Portuguese-
language preprocessing, and a modular RAG pipeline. The approach is especially relevant for
open universities and distance learning contexts, where scalable, language-sensitive, and self-
regulation–oriented support tools are needed.
9.4
Study Limitations
Despite its contributions, the study presents several limitations. First, the evaluation was limited
to a single course instance and a synthetic student profile, which restricts the generalization of
results. While the structured test cases reflect realistic SRL challenges, they cannot fully capture
the variability of real student interactions in live environments.
Second, hardware limitations constrained the choice of language models. Larger models were
excluded due to long inference times, and all tests were run in a constrained hardware environment
that led to long response times.
Methodologically, the RAG pipeline depended on preprocessed JSON data, which required
significant manual curation and language translation for this course and that would need adapta-
tion for other learning management systems outside of Moodle. Although this ensured content
alignment and metadata preservation, it may have introduced selection biases or missed implicit
context. Additionally, the chatbot was tested only in Portuguese, meaning that multilingual per-
formance remains unexplored.
Lastly, while this study explicitly focused on self-regulated learning (SRL), aspects related
to co-regulated learning, particularly the interactive regulatory dynamic between students and the
chatbot, were not deeply analyzed. This dimension, where the LLM acts as an external regulatory
agent and students reciprocally regulate chatbot interactions through their queries and follow-ups,
warrants further exploration.
9.5
Recommendations for Future Research
Future research can build on this study in several meaningful ways. First, real-world user testing
should be conducted to evaluate the chatbot’s effectiveness with actual students. By deploying the

[Vista original de la página 159](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-159.png)

![Página PDF 159, representación visual original](../../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Related/terceary/last%20year%20tesis/Catmming/page-159.png)


<!-- PDF page 160 -->

## Página PDF 160

Conclusions and Future Work
146
system into the Laboratory Development Software course, we could evaluate realistically on both
learning outcomes and user satisfaction. Observational and longitudinal studies could also offer
valuable insights into sustained SRL improvements over time.
Second, future systems should aim to detect and adapt to learners’ internal states, such as
emotional engagement, frustration, or motivation, using sentiment analysis. This would enable
more human-like tutoring interactions and help address one of the key limitations identified in the
literature.
Third, the integration of explicit co-regulation features, such as collaborative goal-setting
and joint task management, could be explored, particularly in project-based software engineer-
ing courses. Additionally, studies examining the regulatory interactions between students and AI,
exploring how students’ interactions shape and regulate the agent responses, would deepen our
understanding of the co-regulation potential of AI-driven learning environments.
Finally, scaling this solution to other disciplines and LMS platforms would test its adaptability
and further extend its pedagogical reach.
9.6
Concluding Remarks
In summary, this dissertation has demonstrated that a well-designed LLM-based chatbot can of-
fer substantive support for a comprehensive self-regulated learning cycle within asynchronous
online software education. By aligning technical implementation with established SRL theory
and validating performance through structured evaluations, this research bridges the gap between
conceptual frameworks and applied educational technology.
The evaluation confirmed that the chatbot (RQ1) consistently utilized Moodle data, including
quiz interactions, forum participation, and Moodle content, to generate contextually accurate and
pedagogically relevant responses. Furthermore, (RQ2) structured testing demonstrated a robust
alignment with Zimmerman’s subprocesses across the SRL cycle, although the abstract subpro-
cess of Self-Instruction remains an area for refinement. Lastly, (RQ3) the system showed consid-
erable promise in supporting learners’ reflective and metacognitive thinking during metacognitive
challenges. These positive outcomes were enabled by the enhanced SRL context retrieval pipeline
and prompt refinement mechanisms grounded in expert-authored pedagogical resources.
The findings confirm the feasibility and educational value of integrating AI-driven chatbots
into university courses to foster SRL. Nevertheless, challenges remain, and continued research is
essential to fully harness the potential of these tools in dynamic, real-world learning environments.
